THE APOSTLESHIP OF THE SICK 

Those who suffer with Christ carry high Hopes of sharing in the 

Eternal High Priest's redemptive work.

Apostles are the sick; their 

Patience is a sermon; by 

Offering themselves as a 

Sacrifice they are 

Transformed into an 

Oblation to God; their

Love consists in the total 

Abandonment of themselves; they 

Trust in God to give them

Endurance in their vocation of suffering. 

Odors of sweetness break forth from the hearts of 

Fortitude of these holy ones of God;

Soldiers of Christ are they for by their 

Unselfishness and their ardent

Fervor in God's service they reveal their 

Faith in the Providence of God. 

Eternity's bliss will be their compensation;

Resignation and the spirit of 

Immolation will give them the

Nobility of Saints in the realms of 

God's celestial abode. vii

FOREWORD 

IN HIS letter to the Hebrews St. Paul tells us that might sanctify His people.' Sanctification was the purpose of His suffering.

To the worldling such an assertion has no meaning. 

To him suffering is an evil from which one must seek escape; it is useless; it has no values either for this life or the next. Confronted by the meaninglessness of pain, or sorrow, or suffering he often advocates solutions that are destructive of human dignity and even of human life. 

He is puzzled because Calvary is to him a black tragedy only, and not an instrument of salvation. He does not see the sunburst of Easter behind the dark heavens that enveloped the Crucified Source of sanctification. To him as to the pagans of the days of Paul the word of the cross is foolishness.²

But, to the Christian the word of the cross is the power of God. Out of suffering comes a better understanding of the purpose of life, a clearer insight into the vanities and frivolities of this earth, a cleansing from the dross of sin, and a more intimate union with God. The sick, invalids, shut-ins, all who are weighed down by afflictions and tribulations and who have learned the meaning of suffering from Calvary's cross, know that they can not be made perfect by the flesh. They know the answer to 

Paul's cry: "Have you suffered so great things in vain?”* 

¹Heb. 13, 12. 3 

21 Cor. 1, 18. 

Gal. 3, 3. 'Ibid. 4. ix

X Foreword 6 No, not in vain. For, they not only believe in Christ, they suffer for Him. They have united their suffering with 

Christ, and in Christ they glory in their cross. They have come to realize that they have been called to follow the example of the Man of Sorrows and that they must follow His steps." To do so with patience is thankworthy before God. Better than any interpreter of the Scriptures the sickroom has taught them the meaning of their Blessed Savior's words that it was necessary to suffer in order that through suffering they might enter into their glory. No one better than a Christian, who has caught the meaning of Christ's suffering, can understand why out of suffering should come saints. Confined to a bed of pain, like St. Lidwina of Schiedam, the patroness of the

Apostolate of Suffering, neither did they labor, nor did they spin, and yet they were clothed in garments of sanctity more beautiful than the lilies of the field."

Through familiar union with God they absorbed daily more and more of God's holiness and made their souls like unto Him.

The Apostolate of Suffering was founded with a view to teaching the sick that even on a bed of pain they may learn to glory in their tribulations." Their lives are not useless. For Christ they can become apostles, not by active service in home or foreign fields, but by prayer, and suffering, and sacrifice. Members of the mystical body of 

Christ they can "fill up what is wanting in the sufferings of Christ," in their flesh, for His body, which is the 

Church." With Christ they may desire to bring salvation 

5Phil. 1, 29. 

Luke 24, 26. 

11Col. I, 24. 

Gal. 6, 4. "Matt. 6, 28. 

71 Peter 2, 21. 

10Romans, 5, 3.

Foreword xi and sanctification to sin-stricken mankind. While others go about their earthly tasks or plunge themselves with sensuous fever into the pleasures of this world, their time is spent in prayer. Pain may rack their bodies, and, even as did Christ in His agony, they may beseech the Father in heaven to take the chalice of suffering from them, but resignation to God's will is in their hearts and upon their lips. "Father, not my will but Thy will be done.

Their Christian resignation becomes a means of merit of eternal value for themselves and others. 9912 

To Miss Clara M. Tiry, foundress and secretary of the

Apostolate, profound appreciation must be expressed for keeping this high spiritual aim of the Apostolate constantly before its members. Her cheerful disposition, even in hours of great trial and sufferings has been like a ray of golden sunshine to the sick and the afflicted. Her vision, and optimism, and courage, have made the Apostolate what it is today. Frail of body, she is mighty in the power of her spirit. She has relicd in all things on the blessing of God, and this blessing has not been wanting. 

The pages that follow record how generous God has been in showering His good gifts upon the work of the Apostolate. "To the King of Ages, immortal and invisible, the only God, be honor and glory for ever and ever.' 9913 The members of the Apostolate are the beneficiaries of numberless spiritual gifts - Holy Masses, prayers, and generous offerings of devotion and sacrifice. Through the 

Apostolate they feel again the healing hand of Christ, Who loved the sick. It is like balm on a burning wound- 

Christ's consolation that life is yet worth while; that the sick may share in His redemptive work; that the tribula- 

12Luke 22, 42. 

131 Tim. 1, 17.

xii Foreword tions of this life are not to be compared to the glory of the life to come; that through infirmity virtue is made perfect, and through suffering patience is fashioned, and hope, and reward in life eternal. 

In the words of St. Paul the sick may say: "Gladly, therefore, will I glory in my infirmities that the power of Christ may dwell in me,' and through my apostleship of suffering also in the life of others.

PREFACE 

Dthe Apostolate of Suffering, actuated by pious URING the past few years, numerous members of desires, have asked for enlightenment about the origin and founding, the spirit and ideals of the Apostolate of Suffering, a Pious Union of the Sick. 

In order to glorify the Sacred Heart of the Divine Good Samaritan, and to spread the knowledge of this work we deemed it expedient to accede to the earnest request of the members. Herewith we offer them a simple but authentic history wherein are recorded all the interesting facts relative to the foundation, and also a complete exposition of the definite purpose and high aims of the Apostolate. It is also a review of what has been accomplished by the Apostolate and through its members from its inception down to its tenth anniversary. 

The history of the birth and growth of every religious organization within the Church has always claimed the attention and held the interest of the Catholic faithful. 

It is said that "every foundation blessed by God must bear the seal of trial and be proved in the crucible of affliction." For that reason there is always a tinge of divine romance permeating the life and development of every charitable work performed for suffering humanity when it is performed in the true spirit of Christ-like charity. The trials and difficulties which beset this noble enterprise are offset by the conviction that the efforts expended have brought comfort and joy, peace and xiii

xiv 

Preface serenity to thousands of infirm and afflicted persons who walk in the footsteps of the suffering Savior.

The words of St. Paul, “Caritas Christi urget nos," "the charity of Christ urgeth us," and the assurance of Christ that in ministering to the sick we minister unto Him, has been our inspiration throughout these ten years. May this little volume create a Christ-like love for the sick in the hearts of all who read its pages. May it find the way into the homes of those afflicted children of God who have not yet experienced the spiritual joy and deep peace that can be found in suffering for God. May all those "whom the Lord loveth" eventually find shelter within the haven of refuge opened to them through membership in the 

Apostolate of Suffering. 

Mindful of the admonition of St. Paul to "give thanks always," we ask you, dear reader, to pause a moment and whisper a prayer of gratitude to God, the Giver of all good gifts, for the manifold blessings He deigned to bestow so abundantly upon The Apostolate of Suffering and upon those to whom He granted the inestimable privilege to be its founders. This is the humble and prayerful request of 

Milwaukee, Wisconsin 

Feast of the Most Sacred 

Heart of Jesus 

June 24, 1938.

The Historian

CHAPTER I GOD PREPARES THE FIELD 

"It is by my own pains, sufferings, and infirmities that the Lord has been pleased to make me sympathize with the sick, and practice patience and charity towards them." - St. Jane Frances de Chantal

THE 

HE manifold works and associations destined to take root in the garden of Holy Mother Church have been in the plans of God from all eternity. Before the time for the opening of a new field of labor in the Church came, the preparing of the soil had been going on for many years. Man plans only for time, but God plans for eternity. When a new work is started we are inclined to think that it is but the outburst of a sudden impulse, and the enthusiasm of a moment. In reality it is the final budding forth of a seed which had been planted by God in fertile soil but had been lying dormant for many years; God's grace and the co-operation of certain souls brought it to life that it might blossom and grow into another tree in the garden of His Church; beneath its widespread branches His sorely stricken children would find another shelter and protection. 

Thus it has been with all the associations and religious orders that developed in the Church throughout the centuries, and thus it was with the founding of the 

Apostolate of Suffering in America.

Childhood 

In order to give an adequate and true history of the founding of this Pious Union of the Sick, it is necessary to dwell briefly on my own personal history. It will not be amiss to recount a few incidents and experiences of the early years of my life, which in my estimation were the fields wherein God plowed my soul with the instruments of trials and sufferings in various forms. Thereby 

He prepared the soil wherein the seed of the Apostolate of Suffering was sown in later years by Christ's ambassadors here upon earth, His noble priests.

Wandering down the paths of memory, I find that the angel of suffering was my constant companion even in the earliest days of childhood. Mother tells me that even as an infant in arms I was frail and delicate. At one time a lady remarked to her: "Why do you bother yourself and do so much for that child, she will not live." But mother, ever faithful in the discharge of her maternal duties did not give up the battle for the health of her child until she had gained the victory. I am told that good health was my portion for several years, and that statement is verified by several photographs taken during that time. 

When about seven years of age, the angel of suffering drew near again. There are faint recollections of attending kindergarten in a public school; then came the transfer to the parochial school of St. Joseph's Church in 

Milwaukee. The first three years of school were attended regularly; after that, absences became frequent, extending to days and weeks at a time. The painful, lingering

God Prepares the Field 3 disease of chronic bronchial asthma made itself felt in frequent and severe attacks. These attacks would not yield to the best care of the most skilled physicians nor to any prescribed treatments or medicines.

Several years passed in this way; when illness prevented my attendance at school, I tried to keep up with the class by doing the lessons at home. The distance from home to school was nine long blocks; many a time I sat down on the wayside to regain my breath, then trudged along by sheer force of will power. Things, however, could not go on that way. When in sixth grade the attacks of illness become more severe and frequent. A new form of treatment was tried. The doctor's orders were to discontinue school. Those orders were the first in a series of mental sufferings which supplemented those of a physical nature. For three months I obeyed the doctor's orders to the least detail. School work was performed at home, and each Saturday afternoon found me wending my way to the convent of the Notre Dame Sisters of St. Joseph's 

School. Sister Ferdinand, now Mother Ferdinand of Mt. 

Mary College, Milwaukee, Wis., listened to the oral recitations of lessons, and examined the written exercises. ´In December I was permitted to attend school for a half day, and in January for an entire day. This was a source of joy to me, but alas, the joy was of a transitory nature like unto all the joys of this world. At that time instructions for First Holy Communion were being given to the class of 1907. With eagerness of heart I attended the instruction class each day in church, looking forward to the first visit of the Divine Lover of children. But, man proposes and God disposes.

An attack of bronchitis was followed by one of asthma,

and this developed into pneumonia. Within a few days. the doctor informed mother and father of my critical condition and advised them to call a priest. The Rev. 

Joseph LaBoule, then pastor of St. Joseph's, and long since gone to his reward, suggested that I be given the privilege of receiving my first Holy Communion on my sickbed in the form of Viaticum, and then of being anointed with the holy oils in the sacrament of Extreme Unction. When we are children we reason as children, and even to this day I can not help but smile in recalling the attitude I displayed on that occasion. The fear that I would not be able to receive in church with all the other children took possession of me, and therefore this suggestion did not appeal to me in the least. Father LaBoule, however, reasoned with me, assuring me that if I recovered my health and regained my strength, I would be permitted to receive with the class. That settled the doubts in my childish mind; no more objections were raised. 

Well do I remember the private reception of my First Holy Communion, on Sunday, April 7, 1907, the first of thousands of visits of the Lover of Souls to His sick child. 

There was the priest vested in cassock and surplice, the stole of purple and white, the golden pyx, the little white

Host held on high-the first intimate union with the Eucharistic Jesus, and then the first anointing. Strange indeed were these proceedings to a child of twelve years. 

Little did I realize that these acts would be repeated many times during the course of my life. How true it is that "the veil which hides the future was woven by the angel of mercy." Contrary to the doctors expectation I recovered from this illness. No doubt it was due to the healing powers of the holy sacraments.

God Prepares the Field 5 This critical illness put an end to "schooldays." Sadly 

I bade farewell to the familiar classroom, the kind Sisters, and my classmates. Attendance at instructions was taken up again, and when the appointed time for public First Communion arrived, I was at least strong enough to receive with the rest of the class, garbed in white dress, veil, and wreath. Truly, I can understand God's ways now; even in my childhood days He granted many privileges to me which were denied to those in good health. 

Graduation days never dawned for me. To this day I dream that I am ready to receive the diploma, only to wake up with a start at the moment when the diploma is being handed to me. God's ways are not our ways, and

He was preparing the way for the work of the future. 2 

Years of Youth 

"Music hath charms." It always attracted me, and therefore my thoughts turned toward acquiring that art. 

After much pleading on my part, father consented to purchase a piano. Many happy hours were spent in practicing lessons, in learning all about scales, chords, harmony, and the many other intricate points connected with music. During all that time the joyful expectation of success in this art led me on. The hope that with growth in years, growth in health would also come remained in my heart. Again a Sister of Notre Dame, Sr. M. Alphonsine, was my teacher. Very often she changed the hour or day of the lessons to suit my convenience. Seldom did the condition of health permit me

to take two consecutive lessons. Gradually the intervals between lessons became more frequent and of longer duration until finally these, too, had to be discontinued. 

Another fond hope was shattered, another dream was unfulfilled, and all because of ill health. The question arose within me, was I destined to be a jack of all trades and master of none?" The angel of suffering did not whisper to me that God wanted me to learn another art of His Own choosing, namely, the art of suffering. There was no one at that time to teach that art to me. 66 3 Maturity

The next few years were spent in trying to acquire the art of fancy needlework. This work did not require much effort of a physical nature, and therefore I was able to continue at it until proficiency in the art of embroidery work, crocheting, tatting, and various other kinds of fancy needlework had been attained. This work became part of my life, especially because it could be performed within the confines of a sickroom and even during slight attacks of my affliction.

Several years passed in this manner, dotted with spells of illness of a slight or serious nature. In the year 1909 we moved to the less congested western part of the city where the air was fresher and purer. The change was made in the hope that it would be conducive to my health. The opposite, however, was the result; it seemed that God had health of soul rather than health of body in store for me. Change in place of residence meant a transfer to another parish. Going from dear old St.

God Prepares the Field 7 Joseph's to St Michael's caused a bit of heartache, but again there was verified the scripture text that "to those who love God all things work together unto good," for, through the ministrations of the priests of this parish many spiritual blessings and graces came to me. 

For a short period of time there was a slight improvement in the condition of my health. Upon this I built new hopes for the future. This time they were of a different nature than those of childhood days. The sufferings endured during those days had brought me closer to God and had taught me that true happiness could be found only in loving and serving the Divine Master. My thoughts, hopes, prayers, and even dreams centered upon the sublime happiness which would be mine on the day when a doctor's certificate would be the magic key that would open the gates of the convent to me. Alas, the visions of the future were as deceptive as those of the past so far as their realization was concerned. With each passing year there came a gradual decline in health; it seemed that the more prayers were offered the more unconquerable became the disease. The anguish of a child gave way to that of a girl budding into womanhood as the future seemed to hold only suffering in its store. 

Recourse was taken to one doctor after another, but all to no avail. Each one gave the same verdict, "I can do nothing. Try a different climate." Financial and home conditions did not warrant trying this costly remedy; moreover, I had no desire to go into a strange country, and to part from relations and the few friends that were mine. I felt that if God wanted to restore my health,

He could do so right here, and if an early death was

decreed for me, it could not be forestalled by a trip to the western states.

Those years were what I now realize to be the "dark night of the soul." The question contained in the single word "Why?" began to work havoc within me. Has not this self-same question been asked since our first parents Adam and Eve disobeyed the command of God in paradise? Is it not the question which we hear whenever a catastrophe or calamity, war or pestilence, drought or flood, sweep over the earth? "Why?" The answer can be found in those immortal and beautiful lines of Francis 

Thompson 

All which I took from thee I did but take, Not for thy harms, But just that thou might'st seek it in My arms. 

All which thy child's mistake 

Fancies as lost, I have stored for thee at home: 

Rise, clasp My hand and come.

Sometimes these singular visitations of God are the means He selects to draw souls closer to Himself, and to prepare them for the special work he has destined for them. Are they not after all just the "Shade of His hand outstretched caressingly"?

CHAPTER II THE SEED IS SOWN 

"The Sower went out to sow His seed.

Some fell upon good ground and being sprung up, yielded fruit a hundredfold." -Luke 8. 5, 8.

TH 

HE weeks preceding the Feast of the Most Sacred

Heart of Jesus in the year 1911 held much suffering in store for me. Knowing that it would be impossible even to attempt going to church on that day, arrangements were made to have one of the priests of the parish bring Our Lord to me. The priest selected for that sick call was Rev. Joseph F. Kroha, now Monsignor Kroha, and superintendent of St. Amelian's Orphan Asylum,

Milwaukee, Wis. In God's eternal plan this young priest was destined to be the sower of the good seed which was one day to bear fruit different in kind from what had been obtained before. It was he who taught me the rudiments of the art of suffering. This was a difficult task to master, but the teacher was most patient. At times, too, the Divine Principal of the School of Suffering came into the classroom and brought with Him examination papers in the form of severe illnesses and journeys to the very gates of eternity. Every lesson and examination of this nature added to my knowledge of the craft of suffering; it was stored deep within my soul to be used when future trials would come.

ΙΟ 

Shut-in Years

In November of the year 1912 there commenced for me the term of almost five years of confinement to the home. 

The frequency of the attacks had so weakened my constitution that it became impossible for me to walk a greater distance than through the house. The anguish and lonesomeness of those first few years can be imagined more easily than described in words. Had it not been for the frequent, yes, the semi-weekly visits of the Divine Physician it would have been unbearable. Time, however, heals all wounds, and gradually divine grace accomplished its work. Resignation and abandonment to the will of 

God made their home within my heart, and finally I was able to say, "Shut-in! So may it be 

Until the hour God saith to me: 

'It is enough go forth to service with thy might.' 

Either in earthly ways or fields of light 

No more shut-in!”

Weeks passed into months, and months into years. One bright summer day in August, 1913, a heavy trial of a different nature came upon me as suddenly and unexpectedly as if a bolt of lightning had flashed out of a clear sky. My confessor and friend, Father Kroha, was taken from me; he was appointed to the pastorate of St. 

George's parish at Elkhart Lake, in the northern part of the Archdiocese. It was as though the very earth had been swept away from beneath my feet. The invalids and shut-ins who read these lines will understand this desola-

II tion of spirit. "Time and tide wait for no man," however, and the day of parting arrived. Grief was too deep for words, even too deep to find an outlet in tears. The words of farewell were uttered, and I felt that "Out of my life a light has gone 

And yet I know 

If in the darkness God's dear Will be done 'Tis better so; 

If in my loneliness I look to Him, I need not fear;

For love will light the path that else were dim, If He be near."

This loss was lessened to some extent through correspondence and by the joy of an occasional visit from Father Kroha. Then, too, he was replaced by another zealous priest, Rev. James W. Huepper, who ministered to my spiritual needs for almost three years. It was a bit difficult to become accustomed to another priest, for after all, one priest differs from another even as "star differeth from star in glory." His kindness and sympathy of heart helped to heal the wound of sorrow. Very soon life was back to normal again. 

During the last few years my interest had centered on the making of linens and other articles for the altar. The first suggestion had been made by a religious who asked me if I would like to make a cincture as a Christmas gift for the priest who brought me Holy Communion. 

My interest was aroused, and I at once grasped eagerly the opportunity of learning some new phase in the art of needlework. To be able to make articles used at divine services was regarded as a great privilege. Again I had

visions of a future filled with work for God. Thus I transferred my interest in such commonplace articles as scarfs, doilies, pillowcases, and similar things, to those of a higher order, namely, palls, corporals, cinctures, and the like. For some reason or other I felt that I had reached my goal in life. The words of St. Paul, "I have learned, in whatever state I am, to be content therewith," could at last be applied to me.

But God had even greater gifts and pleasures in store for me. At the suggestion of Father Kroha I began to spend my leisure moments in making altar linens for the missions. The little pin-money that I earned by making fancywork or altar linens for other people was used to purchase material and thread to make linens for the missions. As I look back upon those days I realize that those linens were like unto "Bread cast upon the waters"; for, in due time they were returned to me in ways and forms such as my most vivid imagination could not have pictured. With a joyful heart the linens were tucked into the boxes and sent to missionaries in various parts of the United States, and even to Central America. Each letter of thanks received from the missionaries was highly cherished and placed in a box with other treasured letters and precious tokens. In later years many pleasant hours were spent in rereading their contents, and in reliving the happy moments which the arrival of those letters had brought to me. May we not believe that the fervent prayers of God's priests and missionaries obtained for me that measure of strength and health so sorely needed in future years?

2 

Future Founders Meet 

In Advent of the same year, 1913, Our Lord sent me a penance of His Own selection in the form of a severe illness. The Sacrament of Extreme Unction was administered for the fourth time. In the afternoon of the same day, my confessor, Father Huepper, visited me. He brought with him another young priest, also an assistant at St. Michael's parish, namely, Rev. Aloysius J. Muench.

Little did I surmise what an important part he was destined to play in my life or how closely we would be united in the great work of teaching the art of suffering to our brethren in Christ. We spoke of many things, and in the course of the conversation Father Muench said: “Yes, Clara, life is full of . . ." There was an interruption, and to this day both of us wonder just "what" fills life. 

Suffering, perhaps? Yes, but sufferings are blessings in disguise. That was the case with the sufferings I endured in the past; it was the case with this particular illness. 

Somehow, on that day I felt that "God never loved me in so sweet a way before, 'Tis He alone Who can such blessings send. 

And when His love would new expression find 

He brought him to me and said - 'Behold a Friend'." 

Kindred thoughts of heart and soul forged a bond of friendship that was welded more firmly with each passing year. Within the next few years Father Muench was given an opportunity to bring Our Lord to me on several occasions; once when Father Huepper was confined to

• 

 bed with illness, and again when he was on his vacation. 

In May, 1916, Father Huepper was transferred to Elkhart 

Lake to succeed Father Kroha. Again the pain of parting had to be endured.

Divine Providence arranged that Father Muench was appointed to take care of me. Within a few months time, he, like his predecessors found it advisable to prepare me for the final journey. The crisis was passed safely and improvement came quickly. During the following winter a marked change in my health became apparent. 

By a strange intervention of God's Providence a new course of treatments and medicines was tried. These, coupled with fervent prayers brought about the desired results. New strength and vigor were imparted to me; new life seemed to awaken within me. To the astonishment of all relations and friends, I was about to go out again when the warm summer days arrived. On June 21, 1917, I went to church for the first time after an absence of four years and seven months. Father Muench accompanied us into the church, leading us through a side entrance where there were only a few steps to climb. It is true that our visit to the Prisoner of Love was but a short one, but oh, what ineffable joy surged through my heart and soul during those brief moments spent before the tabernacle. Like Simeon of old in the temple, I could truly say, "Now, Thou dost dismiss Thy servant O Lord, according to Thy word in peace," for once more I had been privileged to kneel before the altar of God.

The angel of suffering and sorrow treads swiftly in the footsteps of the angel of joy. A few days after this memorable visit to church, Father Muench informed me of the new appointment given him, namely, chaplain at

St. Mary's Hospital, Madison, Wisconsin, and assistant to 

Dr. Hengell at St. Paul's University Chapel. This news brought great sorrow to many hearts in the parish; I was not alone in my grief. He had been a kind father and friend to the sick, both in word and in deed. 

When the moment for parting arrived, I said: “Father, I am losing you as a confessor, but I hope I shall never lose you as a friend." Steadily his kind eyes penetrated the very depths of my soul, and from his lips came the reassuring words, "I do not think there will ever be an occasion for that." Prophetic words these were, as the future was to show. We kept in touch with each other by letters and by an occasional visit on his part to our home. 

Within my chest of happy memories are stored delightful recollections of one week that mother and I spent with him in July, 1918, while he was chaplain at St. Mary's 

Hospital in Madison, Wisconsin.

When in autumn of 1919 Fr. Muench went to Europe for a course of advanced studies, it was far more difficult to say farewell than when he left St. Michael's parish, for it meant a separation of three years. Within that period of time, many attacks of serious illness were my portion. Very often I felt that I would never see him again. Even though "hope deferreth maketh the heart sick," we kept on hoping throughout the years he dwelt abroad.

In November, 1919, a few months after the departure of Fr. Muench, the John P. Daleiden Company, dealers in church goods, in the city of Chicago, offered me a position in their embroidery department. This offer was accepted gratefully; it proved to be most delightful work, and at the same time it was a source of income. All my

work was done at home and then sent to the company through the mail. Later on they suggested that I hire some girls to assist me in order to turn out a greater supply of work. Feeling that such a procedure would be too much of a strain for one in my condition, I did not carry out the suggestion; moreover, the idea of supervising did not appeal to me; I preferred taking orders to giving them. Had some one told me at that time that in years to come I would be directing a work of the nature and size of the Apostolate, I would have thought it an impossibility. It seems that God at times withholds His gifts and talents until an emergency arises; then He showers them most lavishly upon us thus proving that the work we perform is not due to our own efforts but rather to the grace and strength He imparts to us. 

In this way, three years passed by, and in August, 1922, Fr. Muench, now Dr. Muench, 'returned to Milwaukee. 

We welcomed him into our midst again with hearts filled with gratitude to God Who in His infinite goodness granted us the joy of reunion. In September of the same year, Dr. Muench took up his new duties as professor at the Seminary of St. Francis de Sales, St. Francis, Wisconsin. His occasional visits to our home, and my annual visits to him at the Seminary were always a source of joy to us. Each visit brought us closer to the time set by God from all eternity for the founding of a work so dear to His Heart.

At times when serious illness brought me to death's door only to have the angel of suffering lead me back to life again and bid me to take up anew the cross of illness, I wondered why God permitted it. Then it seemed that from across the shores of eternity His answer came in words of this nature:

"I can not take thee yet, My child! The journey 

Is still a little longer. Train thy heart

To meet with fortitude the weary hours 

That will confront thee in the great world's mart. 

Rest on My love, whate'er thy trials be- 

The most afflicted hearts are most beloved by Me."

At such times, Dr. Muench was always a great comforter. His words taught me to derive from every severe illness a better understanding and a greater appreciation of the mystery and the value of suffering, until gradually suffering seemed less a mystery to me, but rather an opportunity to "do things" for God. My one desire was to do all the good I could while there was time, mindful of the Lord's warning that "the night shall come when no man can work." Due to my condition of health, I felt that the number of years allotted to me on this earth would be few in number; therefore I wanted to labor so that I could meet the Lord with my hands filled to overflowing with good works. Little did I realize the great amount of work the Lord had in store for me! 3 

First Literary Efforts 

During the many years of illness and the ensuing hours of enforced leisure when even the lightest of fancywork proved too great an effort, the perusal of magazines and books afforded a favorite pastime. Few indeed were the number of worthwhile volumes that came into my hands. 

The little sums of money given to me at Christmas and on birthdays were invested in books. A search through the catalogs of publishing houses revealed the dearth of

books written especially for the consolation of the sick. 

The need in this field was evident, for diligent inquiry brought no hidden volumes of such a nature to light. 

The thought of writing a book for the sick took hold of me; it filled all my waking hours, and intruded even. upon my peaceful slumbers. Feeling that this idea might perhaps be an inspiration from on high, I decided to confide my thoughts to a priest friend of mine, Rev. 

Arthur F. Shank, who, having gone through the mill of suffering himself would understand my desire to fill the need for such a book; moreover I felt that he would not laugh at the high aims and perhaps unattainable goal I had in mind. He thought that the idea was a very practical one, and that a book for the sick would be a source of comfort and strength to countless sufferers. Knowing something about my ability of writing, through our correspondence covering a period of four years, he felt that I would be equal to the task and suggested that I consult Dr. Muench for further advice and information. 

No time was lost in following this suggestion. The very next day a letter was sent to Dr. Muench asking him to call on me at the first opportunity, as I had a most important matter to discuss with him. On Ascension Day, May 21, 1925, Dr. Muench came, and we discussed the book I had in mind. Upon his suggestion a few chapters for the proposed book were written and submitted to him for perusal and examination. As his verdict was favorable, the writing of the book was commenced in earnest. Every spare moment was devoted to this project; weeks of illness afforded time to think and ponder; in days of convalescence the thoughts were put down in words. Gradually the material for the book took form,

and in the spring of 1926 the manuscript was ready to be typed. Not being versed in the art of typewriting myself, this important and laborious work was entrusted to Miss 

Theresa Muench, the sister of Dr. Muench.

Here we must anticipate a bit and pass over the founding of the Apostolate down to autumn of 1926 when Dr. 

Muench brought the typewritten manuscript of my book to me. After a second revision of the copy, it was sent to the B. H. Herder Book Company in St. Louis, Missouri. In 

November of the same year the information reached me that the Herder company had accepted the manuscript for publication. Few persons realize how much detail work goes into the making of a book; one must have had a share in forming some part of the finished work in order to appreciate one fully. The correspondence that is involved, the proofs that must be read, the trial pages that must be approved, all this and many other details demand attention, labor, and time.

The months passed rapidly, and on June 9, 1927, the twentieth anniversary of my First Holy Communion, the first copies of my book “Comfort for the Sick” were delivered to me by a special messenger. The joy of an author over his first book is second only to that of a mother over the birth of her first-born child; neither of them remember the hours of labor but give thanks to God for the happiness that is theirs. This joy of mine was shared by Dr. Muench who had devoted much time and effort to assist me in this project. The many fine comments accorded the book, but above all the words of gratitude that came from the lips and hearts of the afflicted who benefited in some way by the contents of the volume were ample reward for the labor we expended.

20 In the summer of 1925 while the manuscript for the book was being written, I had the good fortune to meet 

Rev. Fred Lynk, S.V.D., editor of the Christian Family

Magazine published by the Society of the Divine Word, Techny, Illinois. In the course of the conversation he suggested that I write some articles for their magazine. 

This took me by surprise, for never before had I attempted to write an article for publication. I promised to think about the matter. The result was that in January, 1926, I sent him two articles, one "Sanctifying the Holy 

Season of Lent," the other "The Stations of the Cross." 

Both articles appeared in the March, 1926, issue of the Christian Family. This encouraged me and I decided to write again. An article on "Mother's Day" found its way into the issue of May, 1926, and another "The Greater 

Love" appeared in the July issue of the same magazine. 

About that time I became acquainted with Rev. Winfrid Herbst, S.D.S., the editor of the Savior's Call published by the Salvatorian Fathers in St. Nazianz, Wisconsin. Several articles, "Thoughts for each day during the month of May," and a similar one for the month of June appeared in the May and June, 1926, issue of their magazine. Another, "The Holy Angels" was published in 

October of the same year. Several other articles appeared in later issues. After that, the work of the Apostolate became so heavy that lack of time prevented me from writing regularly for magazines with the exception of articles related to the work of the Apostolate. This opportunity for developing my literary talents prepared the way for my future labors in editing "Our Good Samaritan," our own official quarterly publication for the sick. Moreover, the financial remuneration received,

which was put aside for some yet unknown purpose, proved to be a veritable God-send when the Apostolate was founded. The Lord was providing in advance for the needs of the future years.

CHAPTER III THE FRUITS 

"O Lord, Thy work, in the midst of the years bring it to life. In the midst of the years Thou shalt make it known." - Habacuc 3.2. 

PRINGTIME of 1926 arrived. In the city of Chicago, Illinois, the great transportation center of the United States, our Catholic brethren were preparing to act as hosts to their fellow citizen and brethren of the entire Catholic world. In the month of June, the International 

Eucharistic Congress was to be held in our country for the first time.

For many weeks and months prior to the dates set for this solemn manifestation of love for Jesus in the Blessed Sacrament the congress was the subject and topic uppermost in the minds and hearts of the faithful. During the time immediately preceding the congress, my confinement pressed heavily upon me. To add to my sorrow, another severe illness was sent to me. Again there came forth that cry of "Why?" What a sacrifice it was to remain at home when millions of other people joined in giving tribute to our Eucharistic Jesus. Musing thus in the silence of the sickroom, there arose before my mind a vision of the thousands upon thousands of sick people in our country who like myself would be deprived of the greatest of consolations granted to their more fortunate 22

The Fruits 

23 fellow men, the participation in this great Eucharistic celebration. And yet, these same shut-ins were in far greater need of spiritual comfort than those who would be privileged to attend.

The great and marvelous modern invention of the radio was in its infancy, and its benefits were available only to those who were blessed with material means. 

This meant that the greater number of invalids, like myself, would be deprived of even this means of participation in these solemn services, and would have to be content with reading about their grandeur and splendor in the Catholic and daily press. Somehow, my heart went out to them; I felt that they were being neglected, put aside, placed upon a shelf. Thoughts beget thoughts. The fact that every class of persons, young and old, men and women, priests and religious, all except the sick, have some organization or society which provides for their spiritual comfort and needs was brought home to me. 

Why is it that the infirm, the shut-ins, the invalids, and the crippled are deprived of such a consolation? Why is there no society formed solely for the purpose of bringing comfort and cheer to the afflicted, and wherein they could be united in prayer and suffering? Mutual intercourse and the example of other heroic sufferers would be an inspiration to take up and carry one's own cross with renewed courage. Our Lord said, "Where there are two or three gathered in My name, there am I in the midst. of them." If this is true of united prayer, is it not even more true of united prayerful suffering?

An Idea Blossoms 

The idea of a society of this kind took deep root in my heart. Thought upon thought came to me, until in my own mind it was a completed whole, with every detail carefully planned. To mother I confided my plans with the remark, "Mother, if I were well, I would organize such a society myself." Sorrowfully, yet comfortingly she replied: "It would be a very wonderful thing for the sick, but in your condition it is impossible to do anything.” Again I was confronted with the same problem; shattered health and physical handicaps were as stumbling blocks in my path; never before had my infirmities weighed so heavily upon me as at that moment. It seemed that I was building more air-castles which were destined again to fall into ruin, and entertaining new hopes only to have them shattered as so often before. The seed of desire for active work had germinated and was struggling to find its way through the heavy crust of physical suffering obstructing its growth. There seemed to be no hand outstretched to remove the obstructions, and so the thoughts and plans were hidden away within my heart, but kept alive by thoughts and prayers. 

It never occurred to me to confide these thoughts and ideas to some one in higher authority, yet I hoped that some day, in some way, God would inspire someone to found a work of this nature and then the Lord's afflicted would come into their own.

The darkest hour is that preceding the dawn. In His own good time God sent His own representative, one who in later years was to be a successor of the Apostles,

The Fruits 

25 to open the way. It was not in the power of this young priest to remove the obstacles of physical infirmity that seemed to bar the way for the founding of the planned society, but his deep interest in the sick, his words of encouragement, his frank and open countenance inviting confidence, and his cheerful yet sympathetic smile were like rays of sunshine and refreshing dew that warmed and moistened the seed, causing it to sprout forth, blossom, and bear abundant fruit. Sometimes we are inclined to think that only the large and important things in life are worthy of our consideration and esteem while very often the opposite is true, and we find that it is the little things that have the farthest reaching effects. This was the case in the founding of the Apostolate of Suffering. 2 The Fruit Appears Our blessed Lord often delights in using the most insignificant things to serve His purposes. A series of clippings from a newspaper were the means of paving the way for the founding of the Apostolate. 

It is a striking coincidence that the birth of an association which would consist of souls called to be Apostles for Christ through their life of suffering should take place on the feast of an apostle and martyr. 

It was on the feast of St. Bartholomew, August 24, 1926, just two months after the solemn closing ceremonies of the Eucharistic Congress were held in Chicago, that Dr. Muench called at our home. His visits always brought a ray of sunshine into my life; but there was nothing to indicate that this particular visit was to be a most memo-

rable one, the result of which would bring rays of sunshine into the hearts of thousands of afflicted persons throughout the years to come. In the course of the visit he said: "Here are some clippings from the Catholic 

Daily Tribune which I saved for you. I think you will find them very interesting." My curiosity was aroused. "What is it about, Father?" Placing the clippings on the table he said: "It concerns the sick; and as you are sick so often, I thought you would enjoy reading the articles." 

I looked at the title of the article and there in large type was printed "Eucharistic Triduum for the Sick." To my question "Do you want me to read them at once?" he answered, "No, it is not necessary, for you may keep these articles." Abstractedly came my reply, "Very well, Father, I shall read them after you leave. While you are here we want to use these precious moments for visiting with each other."

Whenever I think of that afternoon, I feel that I was not an attentive listener. The sight of the word "Sick" fascinated me, for seldom does an article relative to the sick appear in a paper or magazine. Somehow, all the thoughts and plans about formulating an association for the sick, and which I had thought were buried forever, were resurrected. An irresistible force seemed to compel me to open my heart and place my ideas before him.

Perhaps he would know of some one who could undertake a work of that kind. It was impossible for me to quiet that wee, small, inner voice. A mental argument took place. Why should I tell him? Why bother him with these quaint ideas of mine? But always that persistent voice kept urging me on. No doubt it was my guardian angel prompting me to heed the inspiration of the Holy

The Fruits 

27 Ghost. When the time for his departure drew near, I took courage into my hands, and said: "Father, the title of those articles brought to my mind several plans and ideas about which I have been thinking for some time. 

At first they were very indefinite, but recently they have taken on a more concrete form. Perhaps the ideas are very foolish, perhaps they are worthwhile. Whatever the case may be, at least I would like to tell you about them." He at once invited my confidence and urged me to tell him everything that was on my mind. 

The task was not an easy one. Father, however, was a good listener, and if the beginning was difficult it became more easy as I went along. My face became scarlet as I realized my boldness in even thinking of a work which I was convinced would one day be an important one in the Church. After I had unfolded my plans in a rather broken and disconnected manner, he pondered a moment, then, with a note of finality and approval in his voice said to me: "The idea is a splendid one. It is bound to be productive of much good for the souls of the sick. I advise you to write an article with the aims and purposes outlined as you mentioned them to me. After I revised the same we shall send them to the Catholic Press for publication and see if there will be a response from the sick."

Thus within a half hour's time the plans which before had seemed impossible of being carried out, were shaping themselves into a reality. The seed had burst through the obstructions which kept it within the ground; the stumbling blocks were removed, and the way was open. 

At a time when it was least expected, God granted that which He had withheld for many years, the opportunity

to do real active work for God and souls. Thus was born the Apostolate of Suffering.

A few important points were discussed that very afternoon. We decided that this proposed Pious Union of the 

Sick was to bear the official title "The Apostolate of Suffering," with the sub-title "A Pious Union of the Sick," and would be placed under the protection of the 

Sacred Heart of Jesus and of Our Lady, Health of the 

Sick. As our patroness we selected the humble, lowly, and invalid virgin, St. Lidwina of Schiedam, Holland. Ever since childhood days when I first read her life's history and saw her picture in a german edition of the volume Lives of the Saints this particular saint occupied a special place in my heart, affections, and devotions. Thirty-eight years of suffering deserved recognition here on earth in addition to the eternal reward she enjoys in heaven.

The purpose of the Apostolate, then as it is today, was to unite all the sick, infirm, crippled, and defective into one large family, where the example of one will encourage the other; to promote among them the spirit of love for suffering; to instill into their hearts a filial resignation to God's Holy Will; to hold before them an ideal for which to live, a goal for which to work; a purpose for which to pray and to suffer. 

In order to keep before the minds of the sick the various intentions for which their sufferings could be offered we deemed it advisable to formulate a list. This list included the following: The conversion of sinners, vocations to the priesthood and the religious life, the success of missionary work, the sanctification of priests, and reparation for the sins committed against the Blessed Sacrament. 

Some months later, upon the suggestion of Rev. F. X.

HIS EXCELLENCY THE MOST REVEREND SAMUEL A. STRITCH. D.D. 

Archbishop of Milwaukee Episcopal Protector

The Fruits 

29 Lasance, two more intentions were added, namely, the salvation of the dying, and the holy souls in purgatory. 

This brought the number to seven, one intention for each day of the week.

The conditions of membership read as follows: 

1. The firm resolution to accept all suffering as coming directly from the loving hand of God. 

2. To bear them in a spirit of Christian resignation. 

3. To offer them for the intentions of the Apostolate. (This does not exclude other personal intentions.) 

4. To send name and address to the office.

At the time the Apostolate was founded the Spiritual benefits derived from membership were as follows: 

1. One Holy Mass each month for all the members and their intentions. 

2. A Holy Mass on Christmas, Easter, on the feast of St. Lidwina, April 14th, and that of the Most Sacred

Heart of Jesus. 

3. A share in the prayers of all members, and in the merits of the good works performed in the spirit of the Apostolate.

Realizing that the sick seldom have a source of income, and very often are dependent upon the various members of their family for funds to purchase little extras or to pay their dues in various organizations, we decided upon a voluntary contribution of twenty-five cents a year.

These small amounts would cover the stipends for the Holy Masses, and would defray the expenses of postage, printing, and the like.

All this was definitely settled on that memorable afternoon. After Dr. Muench's departure, the greatness of the undertaking almost overwhelmed me. Many misgivings arose in my mind. Would I be able to do justice to the new work? Would my health hold out under the strain of new duties entirely foreign to me? These thoughts were cast aside as temptations. A priest of God had advised me to follow the inspirations; he would be my faithful leader and helper. Why then should I fear?

God seemed to want this new association in His church, the sick needed it, and, His ambassador encouraged it. 

My part in the work was to carry it to fulfillment. 

The first step was the preparation of the article for publication. In it were embodied all the points mentioned above. It appeared for the first time in the issue of The 

Catholic Citizen of Milwaukee, Wisconsin, on September 18th, 1926, under the title "The Apostolate of Suffering; A Proposed Society." Within three days responses were received from three persons, Miss Mary Richatsch of Dubuque, Iowa, Mrs. Sarah Healy of Duluth, Minnesota, and Mr. James Kearney of Ackley, Iowa. Two of these persons were invalids for a number of years, and all three were filled with enthusiasm for the proposed society; they voted it a most necessary work in the Church and a most laudable undertaking in behalf of the sick. 

They assured us that such an Apostolate would fill a long felt want in the lives of those who are called to follow Christ on the royal road of the cross. Their letters were most encouraging and the interest of these prospective members was an incentive to continue our labor of love. They requested leaflets or typed copies containing

The Fruits 

31 information about the planned society for distribution among their relations and friends. 

The beginning of the Apostolate was a very humble one, as is the case in mostly all associations. We had no finances to fall back on, therefore it was necessary to be as conservative as possible. The need for a typewriter and a desk was imperative. Fortunately, a little nest-egg of savings, consisting of remunerations I had received for various articles written for several Catholic magazines, was at my disposal. These had been put aside for the proverbial rainy day, and now served to purchase the first furnishings in the office of the Apostolate. With the aid of a book of self-instruction I mastered the intricacies of the typewriter. Page after page of information slips were typed and distributed to those who requested them. 

Thus the work of the Apostolate was gradually made known.

The next question was, "Who will be the Spiritual 

Director?" Personally, I was of the opinion that no one was better suited to fill this position than Dr. Muench who had been instrumental in founding the Apostolate. His experience among the sick while assistant in a large parish and chaplain in a hospital convinced him of the timeliness of this association, as well as of the great need which the afflicted have for spiritual encouragement. 

Moreover, it was necessary that the priest appointed for this work must reside in close proximity to our office. 

My condition of health and my inability to go outdoors in winter would require frequent trips on his part to the office for consultation and discussion of business matters. 

On Saturday, September 25, I placed these facts before him, and asked him if he would accept the position,

providing the Archbishop approved. His answer was: "The Apostolate is a great work, a new work, and has a large future before it. To be its first Spiritual Director is an honor and a privilege." Thus another important question was settled satisfactorily. We felt that we had many reasons to utter a fervent "Deo Gratias.”

On Sunday, September 26, the holy sacrifice of Mass was offered for the first time by Dr. Muench for the prospective charter members. Truly, it was an occasion of joy and happiness to all who were interested in the founding of this new work. The number of those who had sent in their application for membership was fiftythree.

Next in order was the preparation of the material for the printed leaflets of information and the certificates of membership. The former were arranged in question and answer style, and covered the purpose, dues, conditions, and spiritual benefits of the Apostolate. The certificates contained the above information in condensed form. 

Space was provided for the name of the member, the date of enrollment, and for the signatures of the Spiritual 

Director and the Secretary. Beneath this appeared the daily prayer or offering prescribed for the members. It was very difficult to find a picture suitable for this leaflet, one which would express the thought that had inspired the founding of the Apostolate, namely, the comforting of the afflicted. After a great deal of research work on the part of Dr. Muench and myself, we discovered the one which adorns both leaflet and certificate. It is the picture of Christ with arms extended in welcome eager to gather under the mantle of His love and protection all the sick and infirm, the crippled, and the blind and lame

The Fruits 

333 persons surrounding Him. Another strange coincidence worth recording is that this picture was found in The 

Life and Passion of Our Lord Jesus Christ a book from the pen of the Rev. Joseph Berg given to me by Dr. 

Muench on Christmas, 1916, when he was curate at St. 

Michaels. How plainly we can see the hand of God ordering all things years in advance. 

Everything was now in readiness for the launching of the proposed Apostolate. But one thing remained, an opportune moment in which to obtain the consent and approval of His Grace, the late Most Reverend S. G. 

Messmer, Archbishop of Milwaukee. 3 

The Archbishop's Approval 

On Monday, October 4, 1926, the feast of St. Francis

Assisi, which was also the Seventh Centenary of the death of this beloved Poverello, our late Archbishop, S. 

G. Messmer, officiated at the closing of the solemn 

Triduum held in the chapel of the Motherhouse of the Franciscan Sisters at St. Francis, Wisconsin. It was the day decreed by God from all eternity for the ecclesiastical approbation of the Apostolate. Dr. Muench was among the assistants of this pontifical Mass. Feeling that an opportune moment had arrived, he approached the Archbishop and placed the plans for the proposed Apostolate before him. His Grace, greatly interested in this new movement gave his hearty approval to the undertaking. 

He suggested that a Holy Mass be offered for the sick on the 15th of September, the Feast of the Seven Dolors of

Mary, in addition to those we had selected. With pleasure we carried out his suggestion. 

Whilst Dr. Muench was in consultation with the Archbishop, I was at home, hoping and praying for a successful outcome of the interview. So much, nay everything, depended on the decision of the venerable Archbishop. 

With trepidation and anxiety I awaited the telephone call of Dr. Muench which would announce the verdict. 

What happiness was mine when with a ring of joy in his voice he informed me that the desired approbation had been received, and the permission to start had been granted. Profound joy and unspeakable gratitude welled up in our hearts, and like Our Blessed Mother, we, too, sang our Magnificat. Our souls magnified the Lord, and our spirits rejoiced, for He hath indeed regarded our humility, for henceforth the hungry souls of the afflicted would be filled with all good things. 

Charter members numbered sixty on the day ecclesiastical aprobation was received. The states of Wisconsin, Iowa, Michigan, and Arizona were represented. The first person after mother and myself, to send in her name for membership was Mrs. Catherine Holdtmann of Milwaukee, Wisconsin, and the sixtieth was the Rev. Stephen 

Duren of Phoenix, Arizona.

In this way, the feast of St. Francis of Assisi became a special day in the annals of the Apostolate. May we not entertain the hope that St. Francis, the lover of poverty, will assist those who are "poor in health" to use their poverty as stepping stones to the attainment of the eternal riches and happiness of heaven?

After due consideration we decided to add five Holy 

Masses to our list. One on March 19, the feast of St. Joseph,

The Fruits 

35 for the grace of a happy death; one on July 18, the feast of St. Camillus, the patron of the sick; one on the feast of St. Francis Assisi, our approbation day, another on the 

16th of November, the Feast of Our Lady, Health of the Sick, and one during the month of November for the deceased members of the Apostolate.

The printing of our first leaflets and certificates was done by the Schaafs Publishing Company of Milwaukee. 

The generosity of Mr. Schaafs was a big factor in the upbuilding of our work; his interest and his co-operation enabled us to do more for our members than it would have been possible to do without his help. Several years ago he was called to meet Him Who promised to reward even the cup of cold water given in His Name. 

When the first leaflets were printed, Dr. Muench suggested that we publish a little quarterly paper. As an appropriate title for a publication serving the spiritual. 

needs of the sick that of "OUR GOOD SAMARITAN” 

was selected. Like the Samaritan in the Gospel this little paper was to go on a long journey through the entire country and pour the oil of soothing balm and the wine of comforting strength into the wounds of those who had fallen a prey to bodily suffering and disease, and perhaps had even been stripped of the greater spiritual treasures of hope and trust in the providence of God.

This little paper would also inform the members of the dates on which the Holy Sacrifice of Mass would be offered for them. Lack of finances prevented us from issuing a printed paper, therefore it appeared in the form of three mimeographed sheets. These were made by the seminarians of The Seminary of St. Francis De Sales. Mr. 

Joseph Kools made the heading for the first page of the

issue, and Mr. Norbert Rank did the mimeograph work. 

In that way the only expense we incurred was the cost of the materials.

It is of interest to note here that the young man who made out the bill for these materials was Edward A. 

Weis, then a student at the Seminary, who later on went to Maryknoll, N. Y., to become a foreign missionary. 

Little did we or he dream that six years later he would be the recipient of an altar linen shower sponsored for him by the Apostolate before he sailed for the Orient. 

On the 20th of October we sent out the first issue to ninety-two members then on our register. With it was enclosed the certificate of membership, the information leaflet, and the little verse card "Send your Angel to Holy Mass." This little card found universal favor with the members. Thousands of copies have been distributed, and as many or more have been sold. 

In glancing over the contents of the first issue of "Our

Good Samaritan" we find that the first page contained an article on "Our First Appearance," the "Approbation of Archbishop Messmer," the appointment of “Our

Spiritual Director" and "The First Mass" for the members. The second page described the certificate of membership, gave a short sketch of the life of St. Lidwina of 

Schiedam, our patroness, and contained a little article on the Holy Rosary. The third page carried a brief article on the Holy Souls in Purgatory, a "Prayer in Time of 

Suffering," and another "Prayer in Honor of the Sacred 

Passion of Christ" by Rev. William Doyle, S.J. The Holy

Masses for the months of October and November were listed - there were but three! The concluding article was an expression of appreciation to our benefactors. To this

The Fruits 

37 very day we look upon that first copy with a spirit of pride, joy, and gratitude, for it represented the fulfillment of dreams come true, the first appearance of the only paper in the United States devoted exclusively to promoting the spiritual welfare of the afflicted. 

A letter accompanied by an article regarding the establishment of the Apostolate was sent by Dr. Muench to the N.C.W.C. News Service in Washington, D. C. 

Through their weekly news bulletin the founding of the Apostolate was brought to the attention of the editors of all the Catholic papers in the country. A very large number of papers published this information, and thus the Apostolate was brought to the knowledge of the sick in their homes, in hospitals, and in sanatoriums. These articles appeared about the first part of November. Many inquiries were received, and thereby a great number of new members were gained for the Apostolate. 

On September 22, the name of our first priest member was recorded. It was that of Rev. Joseph F. Kroha, my confessor of thirteen years ago. When he called at our office, he said to me: "Clara, do you realize what a heavy burden you have taken upon your shoulders?" No, I am sure that I did not, but perhaps it was just as well. Why bear the burdens of the future? Sufficient for the day is the burden thereof; with each coming day God would give new strength.

The next priest to enter our ranks was Rev. Stephen 

Duren, as mentioned in a previous paragraph. On October 

21 we had the pleasure and honor to enroll our first 

Monsignor, the esteemed Rt. Rev. Joseph Rainer, Rector of the Seminary of St. Francis De Sales, and on October 

23, Rev. F. X. Lasance, the renowned author of the famous prayerbooks.

The first Holy Mass offered for the members after the Archbishop's approbation was received, was celebrated by Dr. Muench on October 24, the feast of the Archangel

Raphael. Here again there is a striking coincidence, for, exactly nine years later he came as a newly consecrated bishop to offer a Holy Mass of Thanksgiving in the very room in which the idea of the Apostolate was conceived, and where it was brought into existence. Truly, the ways of God are wonderful, and beyond our comprehension. 

In the latter part of November we received a letter from 

Rev. Sudbrack from Leutesdorf am Rhein, Germany. He expressed his joy on hearing about our Apostolate; he promised to pray for us and for our members. His interest centered in the "Triduums for the Sick" which originated in Holland and had also been introduced in various cities in Germany. He urged us to try and inaugurate a similar movement here in America. At that time it was impossible to carry out such a program, but we kept the plan in mind, and never failed to hope and pray that some day America, too, would open the doors of its churches to welcome the wounded soldiers of Christ.

The December issue of the little bulletin was again a mimeographed paper. It was sent out to the members, then 436 in number, on December 8, the Feast of the

Immaculate Conception of Our Lady. With it was sent a little holy picture of the Infant Jesus with an appropriate prayer on the reverse.

In the latter part of December we received a letter from Rev. August Hackert, S.J., of Cleveland, Ohio, asking us to send him 4,000 leaflets of information in regard to the Apostolate. He offered to pay the cost, and to send the leaflets to the members of the Happy Death Society

The Fruits 

39 of which he was the director. In this way he hoped to spread the Apostolate. Gladly and with a grateful heart we accepted his kind offer. Through his splendid cooperation many new members were obtained during the next few months.

On the last day of the year 1926, a little conference was held. Dr. Muench felt that our finances justified the planning of a printed copy of "Our Good Samaritan" to be published in March. Mr. Joseph Kools again offered to draw the picture title for the little paper. The subject was to be taken from the picture on our certificate but arranged in different form, depicting Christ inviting the sick and the afflicted to come unto Him.

With these plans in mind, we bade farewell to the civic year of 1926 as it passed into eternity. It was an eventful year for us and for the sick. We gave thanks to God for the blessings He lavished upon our work, for the success we had, and for the progress we made. At the close of the year there were 608 names on our register. Confidently and trustingly we looked forward to the future years, eagerly waiting to see what they would have in store for the Apostolate.

CHAPTER IV 

ST. LIDWINA OF SCHIEDAM 

"O my Lord, may I receive in good part the strokes of 

Thy hand, and may I be content to see that Thou dost not spare my body from pains, for it is a great mark that 

Thou hast some good design over me." - St. Lidwina. 

HE life of the humble invalid virgin, Saint Lidwina of Schiedam, Holland, was very little known here in the United States before she was chosen as patroness of the Apostolate of Suffering. The heroic patience and fortitude with which she endured her afflictions merited for her the honor and distinction of being selected our patroness, a model and exemplar for an association of persons who are called to follow Christ on the royal way of the cross.

Youth 

St. Lidwina was born in Schiedam, Holland, on Palm 

Sunday, March 18, 1380, while the Passion of Christ was being sung. Her name, regardless of its various forms of spelling and sound is derived from the Flemish word, "lyden," meaning, "to suffer," or, as one of her biographers remarks, it signifies "great patience." Truly, 'to suffer' was her vocation, and 'patience' was her outstanding virtue. 40

St. Lidwina of Schiedam 4I From childhood on, she cherished a special devotion to the Blessed Virgin, and tried to imitate her in practicing the virtues of innocence and humility. Endowed with rich gifts of nature and grace, though only a young girl, she was the object of many admiring suitors who desired her hand in marriage. Desirous of remaining a virgin forever, she pleaded with God to destroy her beauty of body rather than have it bring ruin to the beauty of her immortal soul. God, to Whom the future is as the present, heard and granted her petition.

In her fourteenth year, St. Lidwina was stricken with an illness; even before this had been entirely cured an accident befell her which no doubt was God's direct answer to the petitions she had sent forth to His throne. 

In the year 1395 she went with several comrades to enjoy herself in innocent amusement on the ice. Suddenly, one of the children ran up against her; the force of the contact threw her down upon the ice, thereby causing a fracture of a rib. From this day onward her life of suffering commenced. At first only a small ulcer made its appearance; later on a mass of sores developed; finally, gangrene set in. She suffered intensely from headaches and toothaches; fevers racked her body, dropsy set in, and sleeplessness was her portion during the night. For twenty years she was unable to take any nourishment except the Blessed Sacrament.

The doctors were unable to cure her. Foreseeing strange things one of them said: "This sickness is not under our jurisdiction." Another remarked: "The hand of God is on this child; He will work great marvels in her; would to God that she were my daughter." And so, St. Lidwina suffered excruciating pain and agony of every nature for a period of thirty-eight long years.

2 

The Vocation of Suffering 

In the beginning of her illness, St. Lidwina did not find it easy to be resigned to her lot in life, to be confined to the house, and to be sentenced to a life of invalidism. When she watched her companions enjoying life in perfect health, a spirit of rebellion overcame her. 

For three years she endured the agony of spiritual darkness which masters of the spiritual life call "the dark night of the soul." It seemed that both mankind and 

God had forsaken her in the hour of direst need. Is not that hour of the night the darkest which immediately precedes the dawn?

Thus it was in the life of our patroness. The good God, Who tries none of His children beyond their power of endurance, came to her assistance. It is true, He did not cure her disease, nor did He take away her physical sufferings, but He sent a very pious priest to her, who as her director taught her to look upon suffering in a spiritual light by seeking comfort and strength in meditating upon the sacred passion of Christ. This at first was a difficult task for her to learn, but in obeying her confessor she gradually acquired the art of meditating, and then found this practice to be a tower of strength. She began to feel happy despite her pains, for she recognized the hand of God guiding her destiny and felt that His holy will had chosen for her a very special and extraordinary vocation, namely, that of suffering. It seemed that the more severely bodily suffering abounded in her, the more intense became her love for her neighbor and her God.

When pestilence spread over Holland she was the first victim. Two abscesses formed on her body. When in-

St. Lidwina of Schiedam 43 formed of this she smiled and replied sweetly: "Two, that is well, but if it pleases Our Lord I think three in honor of the Blessed Trinity might have been better!"

Truly, the words were worthy of one who had offered herself to God as a victim for the salvation of souls. God granted the heroic desire of this unselfish maiden, for within a short time a third abscess formed in her cheek. 

Among her outstanding devotions and virtues were her love for the poor, her reverence for priests, her devotion to the Blessed Sacrament and the Passion of Christ, her patience and resignation, and her childlike trust and confidence in God.

St. Lidwina had her passion-tide while here on earth, but she also experienced a foretaste of heavenly bliss. 

Jesus, His Blessed Mother, and her own guardian angel appeared and conversed with her. Sinners came to visit her, some out of curiosity, others to seek her intercession. 

They marvelled at her patience, and many were converted from their evil ways. Priests came to seek her advice; her admonitions induced the lukewarm of heart to mend their ways; the fervent of heart became more holy through their contacts with her. The poor came to her for assistance, and though one of that class herself she always had enough to supply their wants; it seemed that her supplies never diminished no matter how much was taken from them. Thus she was looked upon as the benefactress of the poor. 

On the 14th of April, 1433, on the Tuesday after

Easter, St. Lidwina finished her course in the novitiate of pain. Her heavenly Spouse called her to receive the incorruptible crown of eternal life promised to those who persevere unto the end. After her death, her body was restored to its original beauty and the fairness of a young

girl. This fact and the occurrence of her death in the joyous Easter-tide seem to be a proof that those who share the sufferings of Christ in this life will also partake of the glory of His resurrection and the ineffable joys of eternity. Pope Leo XIII raised her to the honors of the altar on March 14, 1890. 3 

Spread of the Devotion 

St. Lidwina is the most appropriate saint for all those who suffer, regardless of their position or state in life. 

She is a model for the young, the simple, the poor, and the unknown. Her life was as monotonous and commonplace as that of the least of God's afflicted children; it was filled with trials and sorrows of every description. 

She is a model for religious, for although she never dwelt within a convent, she made a cell within her heart where she dwelt in loving converse with Jesus, her divine Spouse.

She is also a model for priests, for on the altar of pain and sacrifice, she offered herself to God for the salvation of souls, and especially for priests. 

We are not expected to imitate the heroism of St. 

Lidwina by asking for additional suffering. It is sufficient if we do our best to bear patiently that measure of pain which God metes out to us. Patience is an important virtue; it is the magic wand which turns the sufferings of this earth into precious coins which purchase for us those joys which "eye hath not seen, nor ear heard, neither hath it entered into the heart of man what things 

God hath prepared for them that love Him."

St. Lidwina of Schiedam 45 In order to spread devotion to St. Lidwina, leaflets containing a picture of her, a short sketch of her life, prayers and aspirations in her honor, were printed in March, 1929, and sent to all the members of the Apostolate. In March, 1931, medals of this saint were procured from a merchant in the city of Schiedam, Holland. A plaque portraying St. Lidwina on her bed of pain, hands folded on her breast, and eyes gazing on the Sacred Host suspended miraculously above her bed is treasured in the home of the Apostolate. Before it, encased in a reliquary is our first class relic of the holy body of our patroness. 

In April, 1933, we observed the fifth centenary of the death of St. Lidwina. We commemorated the closing of this centenary by dedicating the entire issue of "Our 

Good Samaritan" of March, 1934, to our patroness.

A Novena of Holy Masses was offered for our members in preparation for her feast which in that year fell on a Saturday. On each day of the novena, a different group of sick persons was commended to the prayers of our members, namely, the invalids, the shut-ins, those confined to hospitals and sanatoriums, the religious, the priests, the sick who need the grace of conversion, those in the agony of death, and on the last day, our living and deceased members and benefactors.

Each year a Holy Mass is offered on the feast of St. 

Lidwina for the members of the Apostolate. It is our hope and prayer that through the powerful intercession of the patient sufferer of Schiedam countless graces of body and soul will be showered upon all those who have recourse to her. 

St. Lidwina of Schiedam, pray for us!

CHAPTER V PROGRESS 

"I have planted, Apollo watered, but God gave the increase." 

Tit Political, economic, social, or religious, is always a HE growth and development of every enterprise, be slow progress. It can be accomplished only with great labor and many difficulties. In that way it acquires strength and stability. It is always interesting to watch the growth of an association, and to know just what ways and means were used in its upbuilding.
