Even as a gardener watches over his plants and flowers, uses new methods of fertilizing, pruning, and grafting, in order to raise strong, healthy, and gorgeous flowers, so, too, the leaders of an association must ever be on the alert, must devise new ways, and grasp every opportunity to spread the knowledge of their work. 

Mindful that the Lord loves not only a cheerful giver but also a grateful receiver, Dr. Muench offered a Holy 

Mass on the first day of the New Year of 1927 in thanksgiving to God and in gratitude to our members for all the blessings and co-operation vouchsafed to us during the past months. 46

Progress 47 

The First Printed Copy 

As the days passed into weeks, and the weeks into months, the membership increased rapidly. In February of 1927, Dr. Muench suggested that we make definite plans for a printed issue of "Our Good Samaritan." Truly, we were "launching out into the deep." This was an entirely new adventure for me. Just how much material or copy to prepare in order to fill a certain amount of space was a deep mystery to me- for at that time a printer's ways of measuring lines and space was beyond my comprehension. The attempt, however, was made. 

When the first proofs arrived, Dr. Muench helped to read them. Most vividly do I recall that particular Sunday afternoon of February 6, 1927, and parts of the conversation that took place. "Well, Clara, the next step is to make the dummy.” “But Father, what in the world is a dummy?”

Then, with great patience he explained the intricacies of this new phase of work. How ignorant I was of the work connected with the publishing of a paper or a magazine! Again, he was the teacher, I the pupil. "There are two copies of galley proofs, one you correct as we have now done; and from the other you cut out the various articles, and then paste them on a sheet of paper of the same size as the finished product should be. That constitutes a 'dummy.' Then the printer will arrange the page proofs for you according to your pattern and send it to you for a final revision.' 

After the departure of Dr. Muench, I at once began to follow his instructions with a bit of trepidation; yet trust-

ing in God I repeated the words of St. Paul, "I can do all things in Him Who strengthened me." The task was completed with many misgivings as to its correctness. 

Even to this day I never arrange the 'dummy' without recalling that first experience. But the Lord was with me, and the results of our first efforts in the form of the printed copy were pleasing to behold. 

The picture adorning the top of the first page is a pen sketch drawn by Mr. Joseph Kools of the Seminary of 

St. Francis De Sales, now Father Kools stationed in the 

Green Bay diocese. In the center is the figure of Our 

Divine Savior with arms extended, inviting and welcoming the group of afflicted persons surrounding Him. In the far distance is seen the figure of the "Good Samaritan" of the Gospel bending in loving compassion over the pilgrim wounded by the robbers. The subtitle "Bulletin" was changed to "Visitor," for, was not this little paper to do by way of the printed word what we would love to do in person, namely, visit each one of our members? 

The words "Be of Good Heart" were added to the picture because Our Lord used this salutation so often when He made His appearance among the apostles, the sick, and the sin-laden, and when He comforted those who were afflicted with sufferings of a physical or a spiritual nature. 

Two stately columns or pillars uphold the arch surrounding the picture. In each upper corner is an adoring angel, and in the center of the arch is a small circle with the X and P, the two first letters of the Greek word Christ. 

Around these initials are the words "Peace be to you in 

Christ," and the name of our association. Forming a border around the whole picture are two pillars bearing bowls of burning incense. From these come forth clouds

Progress 49 of smoke symbolizing the prayers and sufferings of the members rising to the throne of God on high. Truly we were proud of our little paper and we gave thanks to God for crowning our efforts with success. 

On the first of March, 1927, this first printed copy of "Our Good Samaritan" was placed in the mail with its message of mercy to 1,215 members of the Apostolate.

The first page, then as to this very day, carried a special article for the members. Within its pages were articles relative to the feasts of the Church and the various saints; a poem, prayers, and uplifting sayings of the saints and holy people. A column entitled "From Ocean to Ocean" contained news items in regard to the progress of the 

Apostolate; another column named "Glad Tidings" expressed the sentiments of the members as culled from their letters. Through our correspondence we found that this column served as a fountain of inspiration to the members whence they derived new hope, new life, new courage, and additional spiritual strength to fight the battle of a sufferer's life.

Within a boxed article were listed the days and dates when the Holy Masses were offered for the members. A little notice on the bottom of the list reminded them to send their guardian angel to Mass in their place if they were unable to attend in person. 

Of special mention, in view of future events, was the little article suggesting the possibility of taking the sick to church mentioned for the first time in that particular issue. It was entitled "The Wounded Soldier's Hour."

The thought was based on the special services held for the sick in European countries. Would America remain behind? Nine years elapsed before our dream became a reality.

An article bordered in black requests the prayers of the members for their departed associates whose names appear within it.

This constitutes the general arrangement of our little official paper. In September, 1927, the "Ocean to Ocean" column was changed to "Around the World," and in 

September, 1929, it gave place to "Just a little Chat" - a heart to heart talk between the members and myself.

Being very informal in style it takes the place of my personal visit to the bedside of the patients. This feature has become a very popular one with the members. 

In June, 1930, the column "Glad Tidings" was changed to "," the invitation used by the priest when he intones the preface of the Mass. 

It is providential that throughout these ten years not a single issue failed to make its appearance. The material for many of them was written and gathered under strange conditions, due to my illness. Propped up with pillows while in a sitting position on the davenport, lying flat on my back in bed during several nervous breakdowns, getting out of bed to type a page or two- all these are but a few of the many ways which had to be used in order to turn out the copy for the printer. Through it all, God was very good to us. One of my confessors remarked at a time when I became ill, immediately after an issue had gone to press, "God always sends you illness at the right time; never in your busiest season; He is very considerate in your regard." The truth of that statement has been verified on many occasions. Usually the attacks occur several times a year, and it was certainly providential that during the first nineteen months after the Apostolate was founded not a single severe attack

Progress 

51 troubled me. Indeed, God is never outdone in generosity, and though our needs may be very small yet He is attentive in supplying our wants. How true are the lines, God knows, He loves, He cares, Nothing this truth can dim He gives His very best to those 

Who leave the choice to Him. 2 

Ways and Means 

During the month of October, 1926, a letter and a leaflet of information was sent to the members of the "infirm" section of the Happy Death Society of the Gesu Church, Milwaukee, Wis. The first reply was received from Mr. Arthur Beyer who welcomed the opportunity of being enrolled in the Apostolate. He was our first member to pass into eternity, being called to his eternal reward on November 3, 1926. Later on, his wife came to our office to meet the persons, who, as she expressed it, did such wonderful work for the sick. To this very day she is one of our faithful helpers, especially when there is need of extra workers. Many others appreciated receiving the information about the Apostolate, and thus new names were added to the register. The new members thus obtained passed on the good word to other afflicted persons and thus the membership increased with each passing day.

In the early part of November, 1926, the plan of asking our members for names of their sick friends was tried. A slip with the title "Are we doing enough for our Sick?" was sent to the members at the time of their enrollment.

This slip contained space for a number of names and addresses. They in turn filled out the slips and returned them to us. A letter was then sent to these persons telling them about our association; samples of our literature were enclosed. This method, while bringing us a number of new and truly worthy members, did not meet with sufficient results when compared with the work involved and the expenses incurred; therefore it was discontinued after some time.

Some years later, in observance of the Seventh Birthday of the Apostolate an appeal was made to the members asking them to present a birthday gift to the Apostolate in the form of enrolling a new member. Blanks to be used for this purpose bearing the title "My Birthday Gift" were sent to each member with the issue of September, 1933. The direct response attributed to this appeal was very favorable for it brought over 300 new members into our ranks.

The Catholic Press From time to time, as leisure moments permitted, various articles were written and sent to Catholic papers and magazines for publication. These articles when published always brought us some new members from new states, not only at the time of publication but even in later years. To this very day we receive letters from the sick telling us that they have just read about the Apostolate in a certain magazine of three, four, and even five years ago. It seems that people save their copies and then later on, perhaps when looking for some article stored away in the attic, come across these hidden magazines. 

Then they distribute the same either directly to their

Progress 53 sick friends or through the medium of some persons visiting the sick in public sanatoriums. Thus we can see how far-reaching is the effect of disseminating Catholic literature, and how powerful is the printed word long after the writer may have laid down his pen forever.

A number of parish publications or bulletins both here in Milwaukee, and in other cities carried announcements or articles on the Apostolate. This proved to be a successful method, for it seemed that people were inclined to read such a publication more thoroughly than one of a larger size. These publications brought to our knowledge were 

The Parish Messenger, St. Michael's, Milwaukee, Wis.

St. Boniface Parish Review, Milwaukee, Wis. 

The Tertiarie's Monthly Bulletin, St. Peter's, Chicago, Illinois.

Occasionally, some of the articles in "Our Good Samaritan" were reprinted by other papers and magazines. This afforded us a great deal of satisfaction, for it showed that our little paper was being read by other editors and that they deemed the articles worthy of being reprinted. 

The following articles were reprinted: 

In the Shadow of the Cross-St. Rita's Letter and also in Our Sunday Visitor.

Various Prayers from "Our Good Smaritan" were reprinted in the editorial columns of Our Sunday Visitor of November 10, 1929.

Progress 55 The readers of these papers and magazines wrote to the editors for our address and thus the reprinting of articles was an indirect means of bringing new members into our ranks.

The Apostolate in Hospitals 

Hospitals and Sanatoriums, Homes for the Aged and Infirm, are veritable Houses of God. Of them it can be said: "Truly, the Lord is here and I knew it not.” Within them are confined hundred of thousands who seek that most prized of all treasures, health. Some are successful in their search; joyfully they return to their homes and families. Many must continue diligently in their search for a long period of years until, like the good woman in the gospel who finds the lost coin, they may exultantly invite relations and friends to rejoice with them because of the health which was lost and is found again. Then there are those, and their number is legion, whose search is fruitless, from whom God desires the sacrifice of one of His greatest gifts, that of health. For this reason, hospitals and sanatoriums provide a fertile ground in which the seed of the Apostolate can take root. As early as 

March, 1927, copies of "Our Good Samaritan" were sent to the Superiors of Hospitals for distribution among their patients.

Later on, offers were made to the Superiors for quantities at special prices. One dollar pays for 12 copies of each issue, four times a year, two dollars for 25 copies, and four dollars for 50 copies. The first response came from 

St. Mary's Infirmary, St. Louis, Missouri. Disseminating

Catholic literature of this sort among the sick is truly a form of real Catholic Action, and ought to be encouraged

to the utmost. To have a pamphlet rack in the lobby of a hospital is a good thing but it is not sufficient, for it does not reach the person for whom it is intended. It must be brought directly into the sickroom, nay, even into the very hands of the patient. In some non-Catholic hospitals a leaflet containing a scripture text and a little meditation is placed on the patient's tray each Sunday when dinner is served. Ought we be less zealous for our sick than our separated brethren?

In June, 1928, the plan of sending copies of "Our Good Samaritan" to the Hospitals was placed before the members with the suggestion, and the secret hope, that some of them would be willing to pay for such subscriptions. 

They were given the privilege of selecting the Hospital to which the papers should be sent. Subscriptions for 

15 Hospitals were received. This is indeed a work of charity, a work which should be continued and propagated by those who have the means. Here is a field of labor for the members of sodalities and other organizations who wish to do some active work. 

Several priests and even a Bishop are paying the subscription price for copies to be sent to Hospitals in their diocese. We invite others to do in like manner. 

Every time "Our Good Samaritan" is issued, about 300 back copies are sent to Hospitals, and to the Superiors of those Religious Orders whose members visit and nurse the sick in their homes. Practically every Hospital and 

Sanatorium in the United States has been solicited in this manner. While the results are not in proportion to the expense incurred and labor expended, we do find that there are always a few new members received who become loyal and staunch supporters and even promoters in our work.

Progress 57 In August of 1928 a flyer entitled "How the Sick can be Apostles" was printed. Since then a copy of this flyer is inserted in every copy of "Our Good Samaritan" sent out for distribution among the sick. The first few paragraphs explain the purpose of the Apostolate, emphasizing the fact that the sick can be Apostles even on their bed of pain. Then follows the enumeration of the spiritual benefits derived from membership, the amount of the dues, and other information. At the bottom is attached an application blank for membership, thus simplifying the work of the sick who wish to enroll in the Apostolate. 

The fact that the sick must conserve their energy for fighting the battle of health has always been uppermost in our thoughts when dealing with present and prospective members. It is indeed a source of great satisfaction to see one of these flyers come back to us, bearing the name of a new member, for it proves that our labors are bearing fruit. 3 

New Features

In the spring of 1927 arrangements were made for the printing of our first letterheads. Before and up to that time, our letters were written on plain or ruled sheets of white paper. Our official title with the sub-title "A Pious 

Union of the Sick" in plain black type with our address beneath it occupied the center of the page. In the left hand upper corner was placed the name of Dr. Muench, our Spiritual Director, and in the right hand corner appeared my own, in the capacity of secretary. Using official stationery made the writing of letters more fascinating, and added prestige to our Apostolate.

This first supply lasted until March, 1930. It was then that Mr. Joseph Kools, at that time a deacon, designed the emblem which now adorns our letterheads. A prefix, too, was placed before the name of our spiritual director who in August of 1929 had been appointed Rector of the Seminary with the title of Very Reverend. The names of our two faithful secretaries, Martha Rebholz and Gertrude Simmert were added to the list of officers. The new letterheads printed in dark blue ink were very pleasing to the eye, and the emblem with the crown surmounting the cross, with the salutary reminder "No cross, no crown" served as an inspiration to give such comfort to the afflicted that they might be able to say in the midst of adversity, “The Lord is Our Joy." The circle connecting the four arms of the cross is symbolic of the bond of union that should exist between all the members, thereby uniting them to Our Lord Who suffered upon the cross.

It seemed that every time our supply of letterheads diminished, our spiritual director advanced another step in the Church, for in March of 1935 we had the joy of placing the title of "Monsignor" before his name. Secretly we cherished the hope and even had visions of changing that title to one of a higher rank on some future day some years hence, - never dreaming that even before we started to use the new stationery his elevation to the Episcopacy would be announced. In this and many other ways manifold joys and pleasant surprises were connected with even the minor phases of our work. 

Another supply of letterheads was ordered after his consecration as Bishop of Fargo. This bore his new title, and the name of another officer, the Reverend William S. 

Gensler, the newly appointed moderator of the Apos-

Progress 59 tolate, who is also the Spiritual Director at the Seminary of St. Francis De Sales. 

Messages of Condolence 

When the angel of death summons a beloved member of a family, expressions of sympathy from friends and relations tend to alleviate the poignant sorrow and sense of desolation of the bereaved. The same holds good for the sympathy extended by members of the organizations to which the deceased belonged.

It was our first note of sympathy sent to the bereaved wife of our first member that won for us a loyal helper.

Realizing what a source of comfort this expression of sympathy on our part was to her in the hours of sorrow, we felt that it would mean as much to the family of the other deceased members. Therefore we adopted the practice of sending a note of sympathy to the bereaved family if their address is known to us.

In May, 1928, we had a special form of a sympathy card designed for this purpose; this card we enclose with our letter of condolence. We found that this little act of christian charity won many staunch friends for the Apostolate. After all it is the performance of a corporal work of mercy.

After the death of a member, the name is listed for insertion in the next issue of "Our Good Samaritan."

A marked copy of that issue is sent to the family or survivors of the deceased member. We feel that this is the last act we can perform for those who have been with us in life and who are now resting from their suffering. 

With St. Ambrose we say: "We have loved them in life,

and we shall not forget them in death until by our prayers we have conducted them into the house of the Lord." 

Life Membership 

The first requests for Life Membership in the Apostolate reached us in November of 1927 from a member in Milwaukee and from one in the city of Pittsburgh, Pennsylvania. After giving the matter careful consideration we decided to introduce this new feature. A voluntary contribution of $10.00, paid in monthly installments, entitles one to a Life Membership. Life membership assures the prompt arrival of "Our Good Samaritan" for the member, by taking away the possibility of failing to renew the membership promptly upon expiration and thereby being deprived of the many spiritual benefits to which only members in good standing are entitled. 

The first life membership was taken out by Miss Margaret Niederche of Milwaukee, on January 19, 1928. 

Since that time fifty-eight members have made applications for a similar form of membership. Of this number, four have passed to a better world. 

The amount of money received from life membership is safely invested, and the interest only is used during the lifetime of a member. Upon their death the amount of the life membership is turned over to St. Lidwina's 

Burse Fund as explained in another chapter of this history. Subscriptions

At various times, persons in good health, and therefore ineligible for membership in the Apostolate, desire to help along the good work and are eager to receive the

HIS EXCELLENCY THE MOST REVEREND ALOISIUS J. MUENCH, D.D. 

Bishop of Fargo

Co-Founder and Spiritual Director

Progress 61 little paper. These are accepted as "subscribers." They pay the annual subscription price of twenty-five cents a year or $1.00 for four years; they can also take out a “Life

Subscription" for the sum of $10.00. In this way they share in all the good works performed by the Apostolate, and have a claim on the prayers and sufferings of the members. Then, if at some later date an illness or affliction makes them eligible for membership, and they inform us of it, their name is transferred from the list of subscribers to that of the members. They begin at once to share in the spiritual benefits which the Apostolate offers its members.

Correspondence Among the Members 

The little excerpts from letters received from the members and published in the "Glad Tidings" column aroused in the hearts of the sick the desire to correspond with other members. Perhaps because "misery loves company,' but very often the motive is far more noble than appears on the surface. Requests for publication of names came to us; in deference to these requests the first names appeared in June, 1928. Correspondence of this sort is a source of mutual encouragement, for, the example and fortitude of a patient sufferer will influence another less advanced in the school of suffering. In this way the intense feeling of utter loneliness prevailing in the hearts of the sick is lessened, for, "the bitterness of suffering is nothing to the bitterness of suffering alone." The fact that others suffer more intensely than they themselves is brought home vividly when there is personal contact even though it be through the medium of the pen. It strengthens them in

their resolutions to be faithful to their vocation of being apostles for Christ through vicarious suffering. 

Literature for the Sick

In recent years the output of Catholic literature of every description written by prominent men and women has been on the increase. The book reviews of newspapers and magazines, the Catholic Book Club, and the Spiritual Book Associates offer comments on the best of good books on the market.

It seems that there is a dearth of literature written especially for the sick and afflicted. Steps to remedy this situation were taken at the foundation of the Apostolate. 

The leaflets sent out with every issue of "Our Good Samaritan" were written especially for the sick. We made it a practice to collect all the leaflets, pamphlets, and books written on the subject of suffering, or other subjects relative to it, such as would be of service in encouraging, cheering, and comforting the sick. In the issue of June, 1927, a few of such pamphlets and books were listed for the first time. With each succeeding issue new titles were added in the column "Information." This grew to such proportions that other articles of greater importance were crowded out; therefore it was deemed advisable to discontinue this feature, and advertise this literature in another way. In December of 1933, a "price list" was printed and a copy sent to each member. This contained the titles of all leaflets, pamphlets, books, religious articles, and other miscellaneous items carried in our stock. After that, newly discovered literature of interest to the sick was mentioned in the pages of our little paper.

Progress 63 It is very consoling to note that within the last few years a number of books have appeared on the market that are written expressly for the sick. More power to the pens of Catholic writers who apply their talents in this field and thereby contribute their part in bringing sunshine of a spiritual nature to the sick, and dispelling the gloom and darkness which hover over the sickroom. It is a noble work of charity that will be rewarded by Him 

Who said: "I was sick . . . and you visited Me."

CHAPTER VI THE HARVEST 

"They shall come with joyfulness, carrying their sheaves." - Ps. 125.7. 

NE of God's most precious gifts is time. Time brings the restoration of health, it heals the wounds of body and soul, it calms the troubled spirit, and soothes the bruised heart. Moreover, it brings about the materialization of cherished hopes and dreams. 

The sower plants the good seed of a noble work, but it takes time, yes, weeks, months, and in many cases years, until it brings forth fruit and is ready to be bound into sheaves by the harvesters who gather the ripened grain. 

Each anniversary of the foundation of an organization can be called harvest time, for then it is that we survey the work of the past year. Very often, in the plans of 

Almighty God, the sowers of the seed do not live to reap the harvest of even one year. By the special providence of God, the founders of the Apostolate were granted the privilege of living to see not only one but ten such anniversaries of harvest time. The work of the first year was the sowing of the good seed, but even then there was a little harvest. The future years saw many new developments and much progress, due to the labors of zealous promoters, faithful helpers, and the efficacious prayers and sufferings of our beloved members. 64

The Harvest 

65 

The Holy Father's Blessing 

On October 3, 1927, the eve of the first anniversary of the Apostolate and the feast of the Little Flower, we received word from Rome that our Holy Father, Pope Pius XI, had bestowed his blessing upon the foundress and all the members of the Apostolate of Suffering, and granted them a plenary indulgence at the hour of death. 

We consider this a treasured birthday gift from God, a "rose" from the Little Flower. The original autographed photograph, commemorating this grant of our Holy Father's blessing, adorns the wall above the desk in the office of the Apostolate. In order that the members might share even the pleasure of possessing a copy of this photograph of our Holy Father, a fac-simile or reproduction was made and sent to each member of the Apostolate. On the reverse of the picture was a prayer composed especially for recitation by the sick in behalf of the Holy Father. 

O sweet Jesus, Divine Shepherd, graciously hear the prayers which we, Thy afflicted children, members of the 

Apostolate of Suffering, offer to Thee for Thy Vicar, our Holy Father. 

We offer for him: our mental sufferings — grant him the light of supernatural wisdom; our bodily painsgive him health and vigor that he may safely lead his flock to the eternal sheepfold; our inability to work for 

Thee-strengthen him that by the might of his power 

Thy kingdom may come to all on earth; our spiritual sufferings renew in him the joys of his sacerdotal unction by lightening the burdens of his Pontificate.

May each day of his life bring him closer to Thy sacred Heart, and when his earthly pilgrimage is ended, do 

Thou receive him into Thy heavenly kingdom. Amen.

First Reports 

At the end of the first year our files showed that 3,132 members had been enrolled. Included among these were one Monsignor, 74 priests, and 387 religious. During this first year 71 members passed into eternity, including one of our charter members. Three non-Catholic members received the grace of conversion.

It is interesting, too, to note which States were the first to have a member represented on our register. Even though the proverb says that "no prophet is welcome in his own country," the first 32 members enrolled were from the state of Wisconsin. Then followed Iowa, Michigan, Arizona, North Dakota, Minnesota, Montana, Missouri, California, Arkansas, Ohio, Washington, Colorado, South Dakota, Illinois, New York, Texas, Nebraska, New 

Mexico, Kansas, Indiana, Pennsylvania, New Jersey, Massachusetts, Maryland, District of Columbia, Kentucky, West Virginia, Vermont, Florida, Wyoming, Connecticut, Louisiana, Alabama, North Carolina, Oregon, Virginia, Idaho, Rhode Island, Georgia, Oklahoma, Tennessee, Mississippi, Utah, Delaware, New Hampshire, and Maine.

In Other Countries 

The first member living beyond the boundary of the United States was Sr. St. Thomas Aquinas, of Montreal, Quebec, Canada. She became a member on November 3, 1926. A few weeks later, on the 29th of the same month, we had the pleasure of enrolling Rev. Charles Doyle, S.J., Dublin, Ireland, a brother of Fr. William Doyle, S.J., who was killed in the World War. With his name we also received those of three nuns, one his own sister, all living in Ireland.

On the 13th of December we received a letter from a lady in Cuba. It was written in Spanish, and therefore we had to enlist the services of some one versed in that language before we could learn the contents of that letter. 

This lady had seen a notice of the Apostolate in a news- †Deceased.

The Harvest 

69 paper and had sent in her own and her mother's name for enrollment. 

The third day of the year 1927 brought us a member from the city of Prince George, British Columbia, Canada.

Later on this lady took out a Life Membership. 

After this, members were received from various countries in the following succession: England, Nova Scotia, Newfoundland, Honolulu, Scotland, New Guinea, South 

Africa, Germany, France, Italy and India.

On the 3rd of July, 1928, a letter was received from Brother Joseph Dutton, from the Leper Settlement on the Island of Molokai. He manifested a great interest in the Apostolate and asked us to send old copies of “Our 

Good Samaritan" to him for distribution among the lepers. Now that Brother Dutton has gone to claim his heavenly reward, his letters and an autographed photo are treasured here in the office.

In March, 1929, the name of a religious brother from the city of Palestine, Jerusalem, was entered on our records. It is a consolation to know that in the very city where our Divine Savior labored among the afflicted someone is lifting hands and heart in prayer for us.

From Japan, the Land of the Rising Sun, the first member was received in May, 1932. This was due to the efforts of Rev. Harold Felsecker, M.M., who at that time was studying the Japanese language in Yokohama, Japan.

In January, 1933, he was instrumental in obtaining a member for us in South Manchuria, China, and later on in Korea.

The first member from the persecuted country of Mexico was received on December 17, 1932. Later on

members were received from Canal Zone, and the Virgin 

Islands. 

Truly, the arms of the Apostolate reach out and embrace the afflicted living in the uttermost parts of the earth, where the spiritual values concealed in the art of suffering are as yet unknown. May God speed the day when every country will have its own Apostolate with literature in the vernacular for the benefit of the natives. 3 

Zealous Promoters

In our work we find that the best promoters are the sick themselves. A satisfied and an enthusiastic member is the best advertisement in favor of the Apostolate. A great deal of the success of the Apostolate is due to our faithful members, living and deceased, residing within the precincts of their own homes, in hospitals, sanatoriums, and homes for the aged.

One of our charter members, Miss Mary Richatsch, of Dubuque, Iowa, became a very fine promoter for us, even though she is an invalid and confined to the house. 

Through her friends, and through her correspondence she does her part to make known the Apostolate. In this way she obtained many new members for our ranks. 

Another faithful worker is Mrs. Sara Healy, of Duluth,

Minnesota, one of the three persons who responded to the first article which appeared in the Catholic papers. 

Through her efforts information about the Apostolate was imparted to hundreds of sick persons. Her work is carried on mostly among the inmates of the Home for Aged.

The Harvest 

71 In the State of Texas we found a very splendid and zealous promoter in the person of Mr. Charles V. Scherer, a faithful member of the St. Vincent de Paul Society in the city of San Antonio. 

Here in Milwaukee one of our faithful promoters is

Mrs. Mary Kemp, who never passed an opportunity of commending the Apostolate to the sick with whom she came in contact.

It is most interesting to note the various methods employed by our members to spread the Apostolate. One lady watches the columns of the Catholic papers and magazines for names and addresses of readers, hoping to find among them at least one interested person. Another sends a leaflet of information to the officers of the various associations with which she is acquainted. A third person sends literature to the priests who are her personal friends.

Another means of promoting the Apostolate was tried by a member living in the East. She obtained a list of the sick parishioners from her pastor, called on them in person, explained the Apostolate, and left some of our literature with them. In this way she obtained many new members. Others tried the same method, and some who were shut-in did their part by writing letters instead of making personal calls. The afflicted know, too, that "where there is a will there is a way." 

In September, 1934, a certain Dr. Francis Pounds, of 

State Sanatorium, Hamburg, Pa., became interested in our work. Through his efforts and close adherence to his formulated slogan "A member a day from Hamburg, Pa.," the membership increased rapidly. Here again was genuine proof of what zealous members can do to pro-

mote the Apostolate, depending of course upon their condition of health, circumstances, and surroundings. 

A lady in the state of Pennsylvania desired to increase the membership of the Apostolate. Not knowing many sick persons, she visited the home of the Little Sisters of the Poor and enrolled 25 of the inmates. This good deed was appreciated most highly by them, and they in turn spread the knowledge of the good work to their relations and friends.

A certain branch of the Vincent de Paul society in 

Dubuque, Iowa, enrolled 40 of the sick poor on their list and paid for the first year's enrollment. It is such poor sick persons whom we are eager to have on our list because they above all others are in need of the encouragement and the comfort afforded through the pages of “Our Good Samaritan" and by the spiritual benefits of the Apostolate.

In the ways described above, and in many other ways the good work goes on, mostly in an unseen, hidden way. 

Hundreds of examples of good deeds could be written, but space is limited. May the perusal of what others have done arouse in you, dear reader, the desire to help us realize in some way the slogan we have set for ourselves, namely, “Every sick, afflicted, or crippled person, a member of the Apostolate." If your condition does not permit you to do active work, do that which is even more important - pray and suffer for those who labor to promote this work.

The Superiors of Religious Communities have always been eager to enroll their sick and invalid sisters in the Apostolate. We in turn are happy to enroll them for we know that these Spouses of Christ will make not only

The Harvest 

73 faithful members but will also strive to interest their sisters in religion in the Apostolate. After all these years of activity we still recall the thrill of pleasure which was ours when on the 14th of February, 1927, the Mother

General of the Sisters of the Incarnate Word in San 

Antonio, Texas, enrolled 46 members of her community.

We felt that this was a gift of St. Valentine to us; no doubt the Sisters considered their enrollment as a similar gift from their beloved Mother.

The religious in the infirmaries of their respective convents assisted in spreading the good work. Even those who labor in the classroom come in contact, directly or indirectly, with those who are eligible for membership.

Other communities among the first to enroll their sick and infirm religious were the School Sisters of St. 

Francis whose Mother House is in Milwaukee and who have a large Infirmary at Campbellsport, Wisconsin. 

Their first list comprised thirteen names.

In April of 1927 a Sr. Colette of the order of the Sisters of Loretto at the Foot of the Cross, of Nerinx, Kentucky, was enrolled. This Sister was instrumental in bringing over 100 members into the Apostolate. Their Mother 

House is indeed well represented. Each year, Sr. Colette sent in all the renewals and always had a few new members on her list.

Many other Communities have taken a similar interest in our work but limited space prevents us from mentioning them individually. Suffice it to say that we are truly grateful for their co-operation.

In the spring of 1928 a letter was received from a Religious in South Africa. Some friend had sent her a copy of my book Comfort for the Sick. The article in

regard to the Apostolate appealed to her and she sent in her name for enrollment. She remarked that a number of other nuns in her convent expressed the desire to become a member, but were not able to contribute the small amount of the membership dues. A notice to this effect was published in the June, 1928, issue with the hope expressed that some members would perform an act of charity and send in the dues for their enrollment. 

The response of the members was very gratifying. The returns were sufficient to cover enrollment dues for 32 members. The good nuns were delighted with their membership and promised to offer special prayers for their unknown benefactors. Truly, this was real Catholic 

Action on the part of the sick, for in their own suffering and misery they were mindful of those who had left all to follow Christ and labor for the salvation of souls. He 

Who promised to reward a hundredfold the sacrifice of the religious will also be generous to those who do good unto them for His sake. The first one of the nuns to pass to her reward was Sister Rose, who died on August 8, 1929. The writer of the first letter, Sr. M. Berchmans, lived to enjoy her membership a few years longer. She went to her Master on April 20, 1936.

In the spring of 1931, a similar letter was received from a Good Shepherd Nun in Bangalore City, India. Having seen an advertisement about my book she wrote and asked for a copy for their hospital. Later on she gave the names of a number of Nuns who were eligible for membership. 

These were enrolled gratis. The good Sisters expressed their gratitude by giving the names of Aloysius and Clara to the little pagan babies whom they baptized. Thus there are many little "thieves of paradise" interceding not only

The Harvest 

75 for their god-parents but also for the sick whom God has entrusted to us. Several of these Nuns have gone to heaven, and also several of the natives who requested membership in this Pious Union.

The first priest to take an active part in promoting the Apostolate was Rev. Charles Kremer, S.J., assistant pastor of the Gesu parish, in Milwaukee. On a cold winter day, January 11, 1927, he called at the home of the Apostolate. 

Handing one of our information leaflets to me he said: "Just today I found this leaflet; I want to know more about this organization so that I can enroll my sick people in it." With pleasure we acceded to his request. The result was that within a short time he enrolled 15 invalids in the Apostolate. Some of them have retained their membership to this very day. 

Another active promoter in those first difficult years was 

Rev. Joseph C. Ei, S.M., of Maryhurst Normal, Kirkwood,

Missouri. As Provincial of a religious Congregation he spent much of his time in visiting the various missions of the community. In this way he found all the sick religious who were hidden within the infirmary, and he at once enrolled them. We looked forward to his letters for we knew that they would bring an increase to our membership.

Priests who acted as chaplains in public and private sanatoriums and in hospitals did much to spread the Apostolate among their patients. At times their letters contained as many as 18 to 30 new members. Such days were indeed red letter days in the history of the Apostolate; they were also days of joy to us here at the office. 

Although the number of priests who co-operated with us is too large to give credit to each one individually,

we feel that one in particular must be named. He is Rev. 

Theodore Wemhoff, O.F.M., a sincere and true lover of the sick, and an ardent promoter of the Apostolate. For a number of years he had charge of the large Tuberculosis Sanatorium in Warrensville, Ohio, and several other institutions, such as the Poor Farm and the Workhouse. He obtained his first knowledge of the Apostolate through an article in The Acolyte a magazine for priests. To him it was a wish realized, for in his very first letter to us he mentioned that he had been wishing for some time that a society of this nature would be formed for the sick. 

In January of 1931 he sent in the names of 107 patients for enrollment, and in February a list of 56 was received from him. Every month or two we heard from him, and always he had names of new members to be enrolled. He kept up the good work for several years until his duties became so heavy that he was forced to turn the work of promoting the Apostolate over to one of the patients at the Sanatorium. 

Students for the priesthood also took an active part in making known the Apostolate to those "Whom the Lord loveth." The most remarkable interest was displayed by the seminarians of St. Mary's Seminary, Roland Park, Baltimore, Maryland. Within the walls of the renowned 

Seminary there flourishes the St. Camillus Society. It is composed of about eighty students. Each Wednesday of the school year, they go forth in groups of eight to visit the inmates and patients of the various public institutions of the city and the surrounding area. During these visits they came in contact with the infirm and the affiicted. In March, 1927, the secretary of that society sent us the names of 24 patients from a tuberculosis sanatorium

The Harvest 

77 for enrollment. It seems that once we gain a foothold in such institutions there is always further progress to be expected, for the good word spreads rapidly, and the sick are eager to grasp the opportunity to join the Apostolate.

In the year 1931 especially great interest was manifested for the work of the Apostolate; James Kortendick of 

Milwaukee, a student at St. Marys at that time was largely responsible for this. Through his efforts, while 

President of the St. Camillus Society, a very great number of patients from the city hospitals, sanatoriums, and other institutions were enrolled. Though he left the seminary at his ordination in May, 1934, the good work is carried on by the present officers and members of the St. Camillus Society. May their work prosper and flourish for the benefit of those who carry the heavy cross of pain and suffering.

Realizing the great influence a priest has with the sick, and how they take his word in matters of importance especially in the things pertaining to their spiritual welfare, we have always tried to arouse the interest of the clergy in the Apostolate. Knowing that it would be impossible to reach each one individually, an article entitled "The Priest and the Sick" was written for The Acolyte a magazine devoted to the needs of the clergy. It appeared in the issue of October 8, 1927. This brought a response and inquiry from about fifty priests who until then had never even heard about this Pious Union of the Sick. 

Through their co-operation and efforts many new members were received. A number of these priests enrolled their sick parishioners, friends, or relations and paid the small dues for the first year's enrollment. In this way the

interest of the sick was obtained and held. Through the regular visits of “Our Good Samaritan" they learned to appreciate their membership to so great an extent that when the time of expiration arrived they renewed it of their own accord. Thus the good seed sown by the kindhearted priest bore fruit in the soul of the sick, and in many cases the seed was carried by the gentle breezes of gratitude and benevolence into other souls that they, too, might enjoy the consolations offered by the Apostolate. 

The sick have learned well the lesson of charity, and the good they can do by sharing their blessings with their fellow-sufferers. They have taken to heart the suggestion contained in this little verse. "Have you had a kindness shown, Pass it on. 'Twas not given to you alone, Pass it on. 

Let it wander down the years, Let it wipe another's tears, 'Till in heaven the deed appears 

Pass it on. 

Our Faithful Helpers 

A great deal of the success of our work is due to the persons who labor so unselfishly and faithfully behind the scenes and who are unknown to the world at large, and in our own case unknown even to the members of the Apostolate. In the first weeks of the Apostolate's existence I was all alone in the work; my very first helpers were my niece Anna C. Tiry, and my nephew, Robert J.

Tiry, who at that time were but nine and eleven years of

The Harvest 

79 age. Folding the leaflets and the mimeographed copies of "Our Good Samaritan" kept them busy after school hours and on Sundays. I can still see Anna trudging along carrying a box of filled envelopes under each arm to the postal station just a few blocks from our office. My brothers, Frank and Peter, their wives, and later on other nieces and nephews gave a helping hand whenever the need arose. Mother was at all times a busy worker in many ways, and even my father joined us occasionally in the folding of leaflets. We called it a "folding bee" - in place of the old-fashioned "quilting bee."

In March, 1927, we found it necessary to put in a filing system for the membership cards. Up to that time the names of the members had been entered only in books, and the envelopes addressed from the same. The membership had increased so rapidly - we reached the thousand mark on the 9th of February-that it became necessary to install a card system.

In September of 1927, the good Lord saw that more regular assistance was needed to help me carry on the work, and so he sent a faithful friend in the person of 

Miss Martha Rebholz. Two former classmates of mine from St. Joseph's School, Miss Clara Kilbert and Mrs.

Isabel Gengler, also took an active part in the work. Later on they were joined by Mrs. Gertrude Beyer, Miss 

Margaret Inkmann, Miss Cennie Kumhera, Miss Virginia

Janda and Miss Gertrude Simmert. Thus a small group was formed who did the addressing of the envelopes and who came together regularly every three months to insert the copies of "Our Good Samaritan" and the leaflets into the envelopes they had addressed. 

Many other ladies also assisted at various times and in-

tervals with some phase of the work, but they are too numerous to be mentioned separately. Worthy of noting is the fact that practically every one who at some time or other assisted in this work, with the exception of one or two persons, had been afflicted with physical suffering, borne mental sorrow, or a bereavement. Perhaps it was this personal experience, the tasting of the bitter chalice, the bearing of pain and suffering, sorrow and loss, and the lonely hours of confinement and convalescence that instilled into their hearts a Christ-like sympathy for the afflicted and the desire to alleviate their sufferings to some extent. Here in the work of the Apostolate they found an outlet for their zeal. 

On the first Friday of November, 1929, a second cousin of mine, Miss Mary Grode, of Webster, South Dakota, came to Milwaukee in search of a position. Having had a splendid secretarial training and having held offices in various Catholic organizations, her advice and assistance were invaluable to me. This was especially the case when serious illness disabled me temporarily, and when the time came for the founding of "."

In autumn of 1931, the firm where Miss Grode worked closed its doors here in Milwaukee and so she accepted another position with their branch office in Indianapolis, Indiana. Reluctantly we bade her farewell, realizing that the place left vacant here in our group by her departure, would be difficult to fill by another person. Yet, we thanked God for having sent her to us at a time when she was most needed. She had centered her heart and soul in this work for the sick. In May of 1931 she left Indianapolis at the time her brother was ordained, and since then she has been living with her aged parents in Webster,

The Harvest 

81 

South Dakota. Perhaps some day she will be with us again, if God so wills. 

The month of March, 1930, found me critically ill, unable to attend to my duties. It was then that Miss 

Martha Rebholz and Miss Gertrude Simmert were appointed assistant secretaries. The former was given charge of entering new members in the books and card system; to the latter was entrusted the care of St. Lidwina's Burse 

Fund. Their services were rendered voluntarily and gratuitously, after their own duties at home or in the office were performed.

In March, 1935, another severe illness followed by a breakdown disabled me for many weeks. The need for a regular assistant became imperative, and therefore outside help had to be taken in. Our first assistant was Miss 

Dorothy July who was with us for four months, and left us to enter the holy state of matrimony. Her place was filled by my niece Anna, who had just graduated from Messmer High School. For one year we worked together until a permanent position was offered her. Since July, 1936, Miss Loraine Knoechel, formerly one of our patients, has filled her place.

In our work and correspondence with the sick we have at all times striven to maintain personal contacts. Every letter is given personal attention, and up to March, 1935, all the letters to new members were written in longhand. 

Then, for the first time in nine years, we were forced to resort to the typewriter in taking care of the correspondence. The personal touch, however, has been retained in so far as each letter is taken care of individually. Every certificate is signed personally by the Spiritual Director, Bishop Muench, and by myself as Secretary. This personal

human element, devoid of mechanical process so prevalent in these times, is a great factor in winning and holding the interest of the sick. They appreciate these individual personal touches, and are thereby assured that the Apostolate is not a business enterprise, but that it has both the material and spiritual welfare of each member at heart. How often we wish that instead of writing to the sick we could visit them in person! As that wish can not be realized, we do the next best thing, namely, tuck a little prayer beneath the flap of the envelope asking God to grant to the recipient the special graces needed on the day the letter arrives. We are confident that He will hear and grant this humble petition.

CHAPTER VII SPIRITUAL BENEFITS 

"We give thanks to God always for you all: making a remembrance of you in our prayers without ceasing." 

-1 Thess. I. 2. 

HE aims and purposes of the Apostolate being spirit- TH thoughts centered upon increasing the spiritual benefits accruing from membership. Countless petitions of a physical and a spiritual nature were commended to us by the sick and the afflicted. They represented aching hearts, bruised bodies, tired minds, and throbbing souls, and the owners all trudging along life's highways toward the hill of Calvary. How were we to alleviate such manifold sorrows? It was a task that could be accomplished only through the medium of prayer. 

The Holy Sacrifice of Mass 

Each morning our beloved Spiritual Director, Bishop

Muench, commends all the members and their known and unknown needs to Our Lord in the Holy Sacrifice of the Mass. When the Apostolate was founded he was the only one to do so, but as time went on and we became 83

acquainted with other priests who manifested an interest in our work I asked them to remember the sick in their daily Holy Mass. This was done in person and through the mail. At times it was a difficult task, but I always bore in mind what Dr. Muench told me many years before, namely, "What is not worth asking for is not worth having." That thought and the fact that some sick were in need of the special graces derived through a "memento" at Holy Mass gave me the courage to make the request. To this very day we continue to make similar requests when meeting other priests. At the closing of the tenth year our records show that 162 priests remember the sick daily at their Holy Mass. Through the habitual practice of making these and other similar requests the name of "Spiritual Beggar" has attached itself to me. Even priests whom I have never met seem to have heard of my "habit" - and wait for the request. Mostly always success attends my efforts thereby gaining a few more spiritual gifts for our beloved sick. A thousand teasings would be gladly endured if thereby a thousand mementos could be secured! 

As mentioned elsewhere in this history, the original schedule of spiritual benefits included a Holy Mass each month and on various special feasts during the year. Our financial resources did not permit us to increase the number. Occasionally a member sent in a stipend for a 

Holy Mass for the members. Whenever possible the date for the same was announced in "Our Good Samaritan." 

On the feast of Corpus Christi in 1927 an introduction was given me to Rev. Joseph Stehling, then assistant at 

Holy Angels' parish, Milwaukee. In the course of conversation he inquired about our work and asked me to

Spiritual Benefits 

85 call at the rectory some day. Had I known the "glad tidings" in store for me, I am sure I would have gone there at once. Several months passed by before I kept my promise. On the feast of Our Lady of Dolors, September 

15, 1927, I stopped at the rectory. Imagine my surprise and astonishment when he informed me that he would offer the Holy Sacrifice of Mass for our members on the first Sunday of each month. This was indeed so great a spiritual gift to us and above all to the sick that words of appreciation could not be found to express the thoughts in my heart. Moreover, this promise on the part of Fr. 

Stehling opened the way or door to eliciting the interest of other priests in following his example. 

Taking courage into my hands I wrote to a very dear friend of mine, Rev. Arthur F. Shank, then at Eagle

River, Wis., telling him of Fr. Stehling's gift; the result was that he volunteered to offer a Holy Mass on the second Sunday of each month. This letter arrived on the feast of St. Margaret Mary, the 17th of October. Just a few days later, on the 21st of the month, the Rev. 

Hubert Van Meer, then at Litchville, North Dakota, informed us that he would offer the Holy Sacrifice once a month on a day designated by us. Thus with each passing year the interest of the clergy in our work increased. 

Through their co-operation the total number of Holy Masses offered for the sick during one month is approximately 60 in number.

It was interesting to note the various feasts, months, and days selected by the priests for the offering of their Mass. Some selected a certain Sunday of the month, others a particular month of the year. In number the promises range from one Holy Mass a year to one a week.

What a pleasure it was to announce to our members every increase in the number of Holy Masses through the pages of "Our Good Samaritan." How well I recall making the remark “I do hope I live to see the day when the Apostolate will have an average of one Holy Mass each day for the members." Somehow the day arrived faster than I anticipated. It was in the summer of 1932 that my earnest desires in this respect were gratified, and in the issue of September the good news was imparted to the members.

At various times during the course of these ten years, God prepared special surprises for us. In our records they are listed as "red letter" days. We followed the ancient custom of printing Saints' days in red ink, and have entered the days of special good fortune in that color. 

Imagine our delight when on August 14, 1931, the vigil of the feast of Our Lady's Assumption, a certain bishop outside of the United States informed us that he would recite the Divine Office every Friday for our members, and that he would also offer Holy Mass for them every Friday during the year 1931. Since then he has renewed that promise from year to year, and in his last letter he remarked that he would continue to do so until the end of his life. We must confess that a bit of selfishness prompted us to breathe the wish that his years upon earth would be many in number!

Three years later, on September 27, 1934, one of the bishops of our western dioceses sent us the good news that he would offer the Holy Sacrifice for the members on the first Saturday of each month during the year 1935 in honor of our Blessed Mother. In November of 1935 he not only renewed but augmented the promise, changing

Spiritual Benefits 

87 it from every first Saturday of the month to every Saturday of the year. That was a breath-taking moment; the news seemed "too good to be true." His letter, however, was concrete proof of this splendid spiritual co-operation on the part of one of God's chosen and faithful members of the hierarchy. Since then the good bishop has renewed the offering each successive year. 

It was always a pleasure for us to increase the number of our own Masses for the members of the Apostolate when financial circumstances permitted it. Our Christmas gift to the members in the year 1929 consisted of five additional Holy Masses each year, on feasts of obligation, namely, that of the Immaculate Conception, New Year's, the Ascension of Our Lord, the Assumption of Our Lady, and that of All Saints.

Mindful of the debt of gratitude we, and all our members, owe to God for the innumerable blessings and graces showered upon us during the course of a year, we added a Holy Mass on Thanksgiving Day in 1930. This is now offered annually on our national holiday. 

In December, 1931, our gift to the members at Christmas was twelve additional Holy Masses each year, one on the feast of each Holy Apostle. For those who strive to be "Apostles for Christ" through patient suffering, it is well to foster a devotion to the members of the first group sent out by Our Lord to conquer the world for Him. We cherish the hope that through the veneration hereby accorded the holy Apostles a portion of their heroic fortitude and perfect resignation to God's Holy Will in suffering of body and soul may be instilled into the hearts of our beloved members.

On many other occasions the Holy Sacrifice was offered

for the sick but due to the late arrival of the information it was impossible to record these in advance in our little paper. Sometimes we were told about it only after the 

Holy Sacrifice had been offered. Thus, for instance, when some of our priest members, or other priests interested in our work, travel to the holy places in Europe, they are mindful of the sick, and offer the Holy Sacrifice for them at various shrines, such as Lisieux, Lourdes, Assisi, Rome, and other holy sanctuaries. 

At one time, three newly ordained priests of the Society of the Divine Word offered Holy Mass for the members in the city of Rome. Again, some of our interested priests living in Rome for the purpose of pursuing higher studies offer their annual or monthly Mass for them in the eternal city.

Some of our lay members who were privileged to travel to the holy places in search for lost health or the cure of an infirmity were mindful of their less fortunate comrades while on their pilgrimage. Such genuine unselfishness on their part was a source of edification to us, and a proof that the members not only appreciated the Apostolate but that they were willing to share their good fortune and thus give from their abundance in return for what they receive. 2 

The Treasures of the Breviary

With the exception of the Holy Sacrifice of the Mass and the Sacraments, Holy Mother Church possesses no treasure so abundant in grace as the Divine Office. It is the liturgical prayer of the Church as the mystical body of Christ, therefore it stands alone, far above all other

Spiritual Benefits 

89 prayers and devotions. It is that form of prayer which will be our occupation throughout the ages of eternity, namely, that of giving praise to God. The Divine Office, or Breviary as it is sometimes called, is divided into seven parts, called the Canonical Hours. It is composed of psalms, hymns, canticles, lessons from the Scriptures, sketches from the lives of the saints, and excerpts from the sermons of the Fathers of the Church. Included are prayers, versicles, responses, intermingled with frequent repetitions of the Lord's prayer, the Hail Mary, and the Apostles Creed. Attached to this official prayer is the added merit of being recited under obedience, in the name of the Church and commanded by her authority. Every student for the priesthood assumes the obligation of reciting the Divine Office daily under pain of mortal sin when he is raised to the order of Subdeaconate. 

The excellency of this Divine Office, and the many graces it obtains from Our Lord, urged us to solicit these prayers for the sick. In the spring of 1929 several Discalced Carmelite Fathers and Brothers from St. Florian's

Church, Milwaukee, promised to recite the Divine Office for the members once a week. In May of 1930 my confessor, the Rev. Frank M. Schneider, suggested that I start campaigning for promises of the Divine Offices from the many priests with whom I was acquainted and from those who had shown an interest in our work. The appeals made in person and through writing met with so splendid a response that within a few months 73 priests had promised to recite the Breviary for the members. Promises varied between once a week and once a month. 

In the fall of 1933 we ventured into a new field. Letters of appeal were written to all the Deacons at the Seminary

of St. Francis De Sales, St. Francis, Wisconsin. In this way they were informed about the Apostolate and its work for and among the sick; their interest was aroused, and their sympathies enlisted for the afflicted among whom they would labor after their ordination to the priesthood. Our first efforts met with a 100 per cent response. This was an inducement to approach the next class; that was done shortly before they received subdeaconate. In this way we felt secure in the knowledge that not all their intentions were promised for other purposes. Again the response was 100 per cent.

It was in the spring of 1934 that our splendid co-worker, Rev. James Kortendick, then a deacon at St. Mary's Seminary, Baltimore, Md., interested each one of his 72 classmates in saying the Divine Office for our members. Those were busy days at our office, for each letter is answered in person; to each priest, deacon, or subdeacon is sent a holy picture with a notation on the reverse, stating the day on which they promised to say the Office. The date the promise was received is also recorded. The recipient is asked to place the picture in his breviary as a token of our gratitude and as a reminder of his promise. Here at the office a detailed record is kept of all such promises. This responsible work was entrusted to the care of Miss 

Margaret Inkmann.

After our beloved Spiritual Director became Bishop of Fargo, we made the acquaintance of the seminarians studying for his diocese. Thus our work became known to the students of the Seminary of St. Paul, St. Paul, Minn. 

Gradually the knowledge of our good work spread among the seminarians, and in September of 1936 the entire class of 22 deacons promised to offer the Divine Office every Friday for the members of the Apostolate.

Spiritual Benefits 

91 Thus the good work goes on and on. The seminarians are ordained, they continue their interest by remembering the sick at the Holy Sacrifice, and by offering the Divine Victim for their spiritual and temporal welfare. It is only in eternity that we will realize the innumerable graces obtained for us through the intercession of God's anointed priests. What a consolation, too, to know that when we are unable to pray, some priest or seminarian is presenting our needs and petitions before the throne of Divine Mercy.

Thus each year new recruits for this army of spiritual implorers are enlisted. At the end of the 10th year, August 24, 1936, the names of 376 priests and seminarians reciting the Divine Office for the sick are on our records. 

Besides the excellent prayers noted above, many other devotions are offered for the sick by the clergy and religious. Among them are included the rosary, stations of the cross, holy hour, benediction of the Blessed Sacrament, visits to the Blessed Sacrament, and frequent remembrances in daily prayers. 

A survey of our records shows that the following religious orders are represented among those priests who contribute spiritual gifts to the sick, The Discalced Carmelites, the Society of Jesus, the Society of the Divine Savior, the Society of the Divine Word, the Order of 

Minor Capuchins, the Order of Friars Minor, the Claretian Missionaries, the Benedictines, the Redemptorists, the

Fathers of the Blessed Sacrament, the Precious Blood

of the Sacred Heart, the Dominicans, the Assumptionist Fathers, the Augustinian Fathers, the Vincentians, the 

Sulpicians, the Salesians, the Trinitarian Fathers, the 

Trappists, the Premonstratensians, the Camillian Fathers, and the Chinese Mission Society of St. Columban. 

Through the kindness of several priests, the members and their intentions are included in the Holy Masses offered at the shrine of Our Lady of Lourdes, France, and that of Our Lady of Victory, and the Sacred Heart, both in Paris, France.

The members also share in the prayers, good works, and sacrifices of a number of religious communities, namely,

The Missionary Catechists, Huntington, Indiana, The Trappist Order, Gethsemane, Kentucky, The Poor Clare Nuns, Rockford, Illinois,

The members of this Apostolate, are included in the prayers of the sick who belong to the Apostolates in the various countries of the world. To our knowledge there are at present about 26 such Apostolates.
