Various Devotions The Sacred Heart of Jesus 

When the Apostolate of Suffering was founded it was placed under the special protection of the Sacred Heart of Jesus. Therefore we have always cherished a special love for the various devotions which give honor and glory to the loving Heart of the Master. Owing to the kindness of several priests, a Holy Mass is offered for the members on the First Friday of every month. In 

September, 1935, the salutary practice of making a monthly novena to the Sacred Heart in preparation for the First Friday was adopted. Each novena begins on the Thursday of the week preceding the First Friday. 

The members are invited to send their petitions to us; these are placed at the shrine of the Sacred Heart and are included in all the prayers offered during the course of the novena.

The feast of the Sacred Heart of Jesus ranks high in the Apostolate. A special novena in preparation for it commences on the feast of Corpus Christi, and ends with Holy Mass on the feast itself. The proper spirit of this fast is that of loving reparation, and as "reparation for the sins committed against the Blessed Sacrament" is one of the seven intentions for which the members of the Apostolate pray and suffer, it is but meet and just that this feast should hold a foremost place in their lives. Our 

Holy Father, Pope Pius XI, has raised this feast to the rank of a double of the first class with a privileged octave of the third order. In conformity with the desires of the

Holy Father the Apostolate seeks both to instill into and increase the love of the sick for the Heart of the Divine Physician of souls. That Heart has known all our infirmities and all our sorrows, and in that Heart we shall find our refuge and our consolation. From It we shall obtain mercy and grace in the hour of trial and suffering. 

Our Lady of Perpetual Help 

Within the last few years devotion to Our Lady under the title of Perpetual Help has spread with rapid strides throughout the world. Millions of Mary's devoted clients flock to the services conducted in her honor. In behalf of the members of the Apostolate, particularly for the sake of those who are shut-in and reside in parishes where this devotion is not observed, we initiated this devotion in the home of the Apostolate, in autumn of 1935. The first services were held in private on the 26th of August. The official prayers are recited each Tuesday. Members have the privilege of sending in their petitions to our office. 

Each high feast of Our Lady is preceded by a novena of nine Tuesdays. The first novena commenced on Tuesday,

October 8, 1935, and was held in preparation for the feast of the Immaculate Conception.

Mary, our Mother, who has borne far greater suffering than any other person on earth will vouchsafe to lend a willing ear to our supplications. Though very often health of body is not given to us in answer to our petitions, we know that never was her help implored in vain. Health of body can not be compared to health and beauty of soul. The latter are very often Our Mother's choicest and richest gifts to her loving children.

Spiritual Benefits 

95 

Novenas and Triduums 

Once each year a Novena of Holy Masses is offered for the members. The first one was held in preparation for the feast of Christ's Resurrection. It is held always before some feast of Our Lord, Our Blessed Mother or one of the saints of God.

Other novenas are held at various times in the course of the year, according to the seasons or the feasts which are observed. 

Triduums or periods of three days of prayer are held at intervals. These days of prayer being fewer in number than a novena of nine days are not so strenuous for the sick and therefore more easily observed by them. The first one was held in honor of the Immaculate Conception of Our Lady, from December 6 to 8, 1930. Another in preparation for the feast of Christ the King, October 27 to 29, 1933. Leaflets with prayers to be used during these 

Triduums were sent to each member. 

At Christmas and Easter 

At Christmas time a beautiful little crib is erected in the home of the Apostolate. Prayers for the members are offered before it each day until the octave day of the Epiphany which ends the Christmas cycle. The sufferings endured by the Infant Savior are a powerful incentive toward bearing our own infirmities in silence and in patience.

The statue of the Risen Savior is given the place of honor from Holy Saturday until the feast of Our Lord's

Ascension into heaven. This representation of Christ as 

Victor over sin, death, and hell, reminds us that only those who fight deserve to be crowned, that after the

battle comes the victory, that only sacrifices generously made can claim a reward. Many are the prayers offered to the Risen Savior that each and every member of this great family of suffering souls will one day be taken into heaven there to enjoy the beatific vision for all eternity. 

The Holy Souls in Purgatory

The Holy Souls languishing in the flames of the purgatorial fires, especially the souls of our departed members, are given a special remembrance in our prayers. 

Daily they are remembered in the Memento of the Dead during the Holy Sacrifice offered by our Spiritual Director; daily, too, they are the recipients of special prayers offered for them here at the home of the Apostolate. 

Mindful of the exhortation contained in the text from Holy Scripture "It is therefore a holy and wholesome thought to pray for the dead that they may be loosed from sins" (2 Mach. 12.43, 46), the devotion to the holy souls has always been recommended to our members. The value of the many indulgences which can be gained during the period of a single day merely by the forming of a good intention in the morning is enormous. The specific enumeration of certain classes of souls gives added zest to our practice; keeping a list of the days or dates when beloved relations and friends passed to their reward tends to keep alive their memory and reminds us to fulfill the sacred duty of praying for those "who have gone before us with the sign of faith and sleep the sleep of peace.'

A beautiful practice aiding us to remember in death those whom we have loved in life, is carried out in many religious communities and also here at our office. On a

Spiritual Benefits 

97 little card is written the name of a deceased member, and the card is then deposited in a box consisting of two compartments. It is kept within reach of the hand or near a doorway through which one passes. At the striking of a clock, or whenever the eyes rest on the box, a card is drawn, the name is read, a prayer is uttered, and the card deposited in the other compartment. When all cards are drawn the process is reversed. This constitutes a very simple yet practical and effective way of remembering the holy souls. This practice could easily be adopted by every sick person. Think of the many friends and loved ones who have gone before you and who await the alms of your prayers.

At one time we suggested a plan to follow during the month of November. This was received with such enthusiasm on the part of the members that we are reproducing it herewith. On each day of the month offer some prayer and the indulgences of the day for the souls of the groups of persons specified in the attached list. 

1. The members of your family. 

2. Those of relations and friends. 

3. Your and our benefactors. 

4. The members of the Apostolate. 

5. Those for whom you promised to pray. 

6. For whom it is your duty to pray. 

7. For the souls of the Popes. 

8. For Archbishops and Bishops. 

9. For priests and all in holy orders. 

10. For Religious, men and women.

II. For Seminarians. 

12. For Missioners. 

13. For the soul nearest to heaven. 

14. For the most forgotten soul. 

15. The soul suffering the longest.

16. Those who died suddenly. 

17. For those buried at sea. 

18. For those buried in foreign lands. 

19. For those buried in unconsecrated ground. 

20. The souls of insane persons. 

21. For those killed in accidents. 

22. For your enemies. 

23. Those who neglected Holy Mass. 

24. Those most devoted to the Sacred Heart. 

25. Those most devoted to Our Lady. 

26. For the clients of St. Joseph. 

27. The most destitute soul. 

28. The souls of children. 

29. Those who nursed the sick. 

30. For all holy souls. 

Vigil Lights

Each day of the year Our Lord holds audiences in the courts of heaven. Early in the morning we present to him the needs and petitions of our members. But duty calls and so we must leave God at the altar to find Him again in our work for the sick. We leave behind us, however, a vigil light, a symbol of our prayerful hopes and aspirations. It signifies prayer, and sacrifice, and life with 

God-virtues that blossom in the souls of those who live beneath the shadow of the cross.

Here in the home of the Apostolate, a little red light is kept burning each day for our members before the relic of the True Cross of Christ, the statue of the Sacred Heart, and the plaque and relic of our patroness, St. 

Lidwina. On Sunday it burns in honor of the Blessed

Trinity, and on Monday in behalf of the souls of our deceased members. On Tuesday it pleads for the restoration of lost health before the statue and relic of St.

Spiritual Benefits 

99 

Anthony; and on Wednesday this honor is accorded to dear St. Joseph, the Hope of the Sick and the Patron of the Dying, who, as stated by St. Teresa of Avila, never refused a request. Thursday is dedicated to the Holy 

Eucharist, but as we do not enjoy the privilege of having the Blessed Sacrament in our midst, we keep the light burning before the statue and relic of St. Clare of Assisi, who is represented with a monstrance in her hands to remind us of the victory she gained over the Saracens when they sought to invade her convent. Friday is dedicated to the Heart of Jesus, the Sacred Passion, and the Holy Cross, and so the little light is symbolic of our special prayers for those who are suffering severe pain and agony. With each flickering ray we send forth a plea for the alleviation of their suffering, and for strength to bear it patiently in imitation of Him Who endured suffering and death for them, and now permits them, as St. Paul says, "To fill up those things which are wanting of the sufferings of Christ, in their flesh, for His body, which is the Church." cf. Col. I.24. On Saturdays, the faithful light bears our prayers and our greetings to Our Lady, the Health of the Sick, and the Mother of Sorrows, to her who as St. 

Bernard says never left unaided any one who fled to her protection, implored her help or sought her intercession. 

Votive Light, Pure and bright, Balm unto the heart you are 

Shining there, Bear our prayer 

To the great white throne afar. 

In your flames, Rise the names, Of the friends we love the most;

Friends still near, Fond and dear 

And the friends we loved and lost. 

Vigil keep, Flames upleap

In the chapel, peaceful, dim, And when low, Is your glow, Flicker last my name to Him.

CHAPTER VIII THE LITTLE FLOWER TRAVELING LIBRARY 

"When we pray, we speak to God; when we read a good book, God speaks to us."

IN TH -St. Augustine. 'N THE spring of 1928 several members wrote to us inquiring if the Apostolate possessed books which it would be willing to loan to them. At that time we had no books on hand for such a purpose, yet we were eager to grant the requests of our faithful members. Mother embroidered a scarf, and Miss Martha Rebholz donated a hand-made towel; chances were sold on these articles netting the sum needed to purchase a few copies of "Comfort for the Sick." These were circulated among the members.

This news spread rapidly, and within a short time more requests were received. It was then that serious thought was given to the establishing of a traveling library for the members of the Apostolate. Appraising at once the value of a good book for the spiritual life of the sick, dependent as they are upon the printed word to supply them with food for mind and soul which those in good health obtain by listening to sermons and instructions, as well as by attending spiritual exercises and retreats, Dr. 

Muench favored the proposed venture. But there were difficulties to be surmounted. Where and how would material means be forthcoming to finance and carry on ΙΟΙ

such an undertaking? Could the library be made selfsupporting? To be considered also was the matter of the added labor such a project would entail. Would it be too much for one who already carried the burden of the Apostolate on frail shoulders? 

Reflecting on the words of Our Lord, "whatever you do to the least of My brethren you do unto Me," and considering the hours of enjoyment and recreation plus the spiritual comfort such a library would bring to the sick, the invalids and the shut-ins, especially to those living in isolated places where no books of a Catholic nature are available, we decided after mature deliberation to launch out into deeper waters. 

First Steps 

In the issue of "Our Good Samaritan" for September, 1928, the library project was placed before the members. 

An appeal was made to them for books and also for cash donations with which to defray the cost of books, library equipment, and other expenses connected with the operation of a library of this nature. 

The new library was placed under the protection and patronage of St. Therese of Lisieux, the Little Flower of 

Jesus. Her statue stands on one of the bookcases together with a first class relic of her holy body. Vigil lights and flowers adorn this shrine and act as silent petitioners for a few of the "roses" she promised to send upon earth.

The very first "rose" received was the unexpected acquaintance of a young lady, Miss Cennie Kumhera, who came to our home with Miss Martha Rebholz. In the course of conversation the subject of books was mentioned.

To my astonishment Miss Kumhera was well versed in library work. Through her kindness I learned the first rudiments of this type of work which was entirely new to me. The information and assistance she gave me were a great help in purchasing the necessary materials and the equipment.

A library system to take care of 1,000 books was intalled. 

A book plate bearing the likeness of the Little Flower with a book in her hand adorns the flyleaf of every book in the library. On it is a quotation from her writings, "Books, interesting, yet providing food for both mind and heart, were my favorite recreation.”

Letters of appeal for books of interest to the sick were sent to eighteen publishing houses and Church goods dealers in the States. Responses were received from the 

Diederich-Schaefer Co., and the Bruce Publishing Co. of Milwaukee; the Herder Book Company of St. Louis; the John P. Daleiden Co. of Chicago; the E. M. Lohmann

Co., and the Catholic Art and Book Shop of St. Paul, 

Minnesota, the P. J. Kennedy and Sons, and the Sentinel

Press of New York, and the Ave Maria Press of Notre Dame, Indiana. Some Religious Communities also contributed books, namely, the Maryknoll Fathers, the Society of the Divine Word, and several other individual convents. The response from our members was very gratifying both in the number of books and of cash donations received.

The students of the Seminary of St. Francis De Sales, St. Francis, Wisconsin, made mimeograph copies of the list of books in our library. This list was placed in each group of books sent to the readers, and from this list they made their selections of books they desired to read.

The weeks which followed were filled with activity. 

The classifying and cataloging of books, the writing of cards, the covering and labeling of books, entailed a great amount of work. In this labor of love for the sick I was again assisted by my niece Anna Catherine Tiry, then a child of eleven years of age. Each day for several weeks she came to our home after school hours. Together we saw the work progress until one day everything was in readiness for the first books to be sent forth on their mission of love. Sentiments of joy and gratitude filled our hearts for that which had been accomplished, and a prayer was on our lips that God might bless the good seed which was being sown so that it might bring forth abundant fruit in the hearts and souls of the readers, ever leading them on to heights of sanctity. 2 Progress

Up to January, 1929, the books were kept wherever space was available. Some found a place in the bookcase of the Apostolate, others were stored in drawers, china cabinets, on shelves, in clothes closets, and in other nooks and corners. A religious from Kansas City, Missouri, had sent us a table scarf in the hope that in some way we could dispose of it and use the money for the library. 

This was done, and the sum we realized helped to purchase our first bookcase. We were as happy as a child with a new toy when this case arrived as a storehouse for our treasured books. Each book is covered with a brown paper wrapper; when the book is returned in soiled condition the wrapper is replaced, and thus every book is always in a neat and clean condition.

In the spring and summer of 1929 one of our faithful members, Mrs. Elizabeth Eckhardt, of Sandpoint, Idaho, a staunch supporter of the Catholic Daily Tribune of 

Dubuque, Iowa, rendered us a most valuable service.

Through the Home Circle Page of that paper she launched the project of piecing quilt tops. The thought was to raffle them and give the proceeds to the library. 

Donations for materials, and for quilt blocks were solicited in various ways. The response was generous, the proceeds amounted to $30.00. We owe a debt of gratitude to this member for her zeal in behalf of the library. It is a comfort to know that she benefited by the reading of our books over a period of seven years. We regret that she did not live to celebrate the Tenth Anniversary of the Founding of our Apostolate. She was called to receive her eternal reward on Tuesday, September 10, 1935. We are confident that from heaven's home she smiles down and intercedes for the library for which she labored so zealously while it was in its infancy. 

In March, 1929, a supplement booklist was mimeographed by the seminarians. In July of the same year chances on a rosary were disposed of, netting the sum of $7.00. A matting box in which to store wrapping paper and corrugated board was purchased. At the end of the first year there were 570 books on the shelves of the library. We had every reason to be grateful to God and the Little Flower for the success which attended the first year's work of the library.

During the winter months of 1929 preparations were made for a printed catalog of all our books with a little comment on each volume. How proud we were of the finished copies! The fine pen sketch of the Little Flower

adorning the cover of the catalog was drawn by Mr. 

Urban Ziegler of Milwaukee. On the inside cover of the library catalog are printed the rules which must be observed by every reader. In condensed form they are as follows: 

Books are loaned gratis to the members of The Apostolate of Suffering.

The reader pays ten cent for the catalog; this helps to pay the original cost of printing. The catalog is the property of the reader so long as he draws books from the library. If lost or mislaid a new list will be furnished at the same price.

The reader selects ten or fifteen titles from the catalog and places it in the package of books when returning them. Two or three books are chosen by the librarian according to weight and size, and from the volumes which are on the shelves when the request is received.

Books may be kept for the period of one month but may be retained for another month if notice is sent to the librarian. 

Books are not loaned to members who are afflicted with a contagious disease or in whose homes such diseases prevail. This is a precaution we must exercise in order to safeguard the health of other readers.

The drawing of books from the library is a privilege and readers are requested to regard it as such by handling the books with care and by packing them securely when they are returned.

The library pays the postage when sending the books; the reader must pay the return postage.

In order to help pay the cost of printing these library catalogs, an embroidered scarf and a hand painted plaque of the Little Flower were raffled by the young ladies who devote their time and efforts to assist with the work of the Apostolate. Their untiring zeal manifested so frequently in this phase of work was at all times a source of encouragement to us. Our printer, too, was most generous in remitting one-half of the cost of these catalogs.

The new year of 1933 found the seminarians busy with mimeographing a supplement list to the printed catalog.

At that time the library numbered 791 volumes. The need of another bookcase became apparent; even the plate rail in the dining room was claimed for storage. The treasury, however, did not permit this expenditure, and so we placed many books behind those already in the cases. 

Thus placed the books sometimes played hide-and-seek, but diligent search uncovered them. The thought of better accommodations in the future helped us to endure the inconveniences of the present in a cheerful spirit. 

The ladies of the Good Samaritan Guild came to our rescue in the summer of 1934. This time, a prize of two and a half dollars in cash was the attraction. The proceeds obtained were sufficient for purchasing another bookcase. What a relief when in October, 1934, we could place the books in these new sections. We filled the entire case. The books that were left over will have to await new accommodations which we hope to obtain for them this year. 3 

A Few Facts 

During the eight years in which the Little Flower Library has functioned, 248 members residing in 25 States as also in the Provinces of Ontario, Canada, and in Nova Scotia availed themselves of the privilege of drawing books. It is indeed a “traveling library," for the books were sent on their mission of cheer to the northeast as far as New Hampshire, Massachusetts, Connecticut, Maryland, and New York; to the east and southeast including 

Pennsylvania and West Virginia; in the south to Louisi-

ana and Texas; to the far-western state of California; to 

Washington, Oregon, and Montana in the northwest, and to the more centrally located states of Ohio, Indiana, Illinois, Missouri, Kansas, Idaho, S. Dakota, N. Dakota, Nebraska, Minnesota, Iowa, Michigan, and Wisconsin.

A statistical report of the books read during the eight years maybe will afford a few items of interest:

Duplicate copies of books were placed into the library. 

As time went on we realized that two copies of most books sufficed for our purposes. We therefore decided to send the extra copies to our members residing in public sanatoriums. In several instances this was the means of starting a Catholic Library in these respective institutions. 

Books were sent to the following Sanatoriums: Muirdale, Wauwatosa, Wis., Rocky Knoll, Plymouth, Wis., Hickory 

Grove, West De Pere, Wis., River Pines, Stevens Point,

Wis., Municipal Sanatorium, Chicago, Ill., and Statesan, Tewksbury, Mass. 

Some duplicate copies of books were given to individual members who because of their disease were prohibited from borrowing books from the library and whose financial circumstances prevented them from purchasing and enjoying the luxury of books.

During the course of the eight years 814 books were donated, 88 were purchased by the Library and 26 by the Apostolate, making a total of 928 books. Of this number 

71 books were given to Sanatoriums and individuals, and three were sold to members. The final accounting, on 

August 31, 1936, the end of the eighth year, shows that there are 854 books on the shelves. 

That the Little Flower has not failed us in the confidence we placed in her can be seen from the fact that during these eight years our accounts have never been in the red, even though they were close to it on numerous occasions. The financial report covering the eight years is given below.

Letters received from members who patronize the Little Flower Library reveal to some extent the good which has been accomplished. Our efforts have not been in vain, for through the medium of our books many afflicted persons have found solace in hours of suffering, acquired a keener realization of the Providence of God in their behalf, attained to a more intimate knowledge of their Divine Master and to a closer union with His Sacred Heart. All this is an incentive to continue the work with renewed courage and zeal. 

There are great possibilities in the work of the library. 

A vast field lies before us, if only we were not handicapped by lack of financial means and limitations of space. We still entertain visions of a room given over entirely to the purpose of library work, with bookcases lining each wall instead of one case in each room in the house. A desk in the center with all the necessary laborsaving devices would lighten the work. May St. Therese inspire many generous souls to hasten to our aid. We know that she will shower "roses" in abundance upon all who will assist in this noble enterprise of providing uplifting and inspiring reading matter for the sick deprived of many opportunities of pleasure that are enjoyed by those in good health.

CHAPTER IX ST. LIDWINA'S BURSE FUND 

"All salutary works which strive to preserve, promote, and help priestly vocations, We bless and praise with all our heart." 

- Pope Pius XI. 

HE above words of the Supreme Pontiff, taken from This Encyclical on the Catholic Priesthood, proclaim his great interest in and desire for an increase of priestly vocations. The Catholic Priesthood is the most important and sublime vocation in the world. What would life on earth be for man, above all, what would it be for the sick, the invalids, and the shut-ins, if there were no priests? 

The cleansing Sacrament of Penance, the strengthening Sacrament of the Holy Eucharist, and the consoling Sacrament of Extreme Unction are the very best of medicines that God has given to us. They influence the emotions of the afflicted to a great degree, making them calmer, more satisfied, more obedient, and more resigned in their illness.

It is therefore a consolation and a joy to know that the members of the Apostolate of Suffering are complying with the Holy Father's request and carrying out his wish by striving to create a fund for the education of poor but worthy boys to the priesthood. The blessing of the 

Supreme Pontiff, ratified in heaven by the Eternal High

III

Priest, Jesus Christ, will descend upon them in a shower of richest graces and blessings. 

A Member's Suggestion

In January, 1930, one of our invalid members residing in the province of Ontario, Canada, made a suggestion to start a fund for the education of poor but worthy students to the priesthood. The text of her letter was as follows: 

"In a copy of the "Annales" published in Lisieux, France, I read that a number of sick people have made up a fund for the education of a priest. The thought came to me, why could not the members of the Apostolate do the same? I believe the amount required is about $1,500.00. In France it took more than a year to raise the amount; it was made up of tiny contributions garnered from little sacrifices made by the sick. I know that many of our members are very poor, but then, are not their offerings all the more precious in the sight of God?

Despite all the difficulties I foresee, and the extra amount of work this would add to our secretary's burdened shoulders, I felt that I had to mention this thought to you. Perhaps some member of the Apostolate who lives close to the office would be willing to act as secretary of this fund. How wonderful it would be to have our own priest."

The suggestion appealed to us. Dr. Muench, as rector of the oldest seminary of the Northwest, realized the great

St. Lidwina's Burse Fund 

113 need of Burses established for the education of lads who desire to enter the priesthood, but whose poverty prevented them from answering God's call. The history of the Church shows that many of the most illustrious and holiest of priests came from the ranks of the poor. Did not Our Lord Hmiself select twelve poor men for His apostles?

The suggestion of our member was accepted, but it was decided to aim a little higher, namely, to establish a Burse in perpetuity instead of a fund to cover the cost of the education of a single priest. The amount needed for such a purpose is $6,000.00. This sum is invested, and the interest is used to pay for the yearly tuition of a student. Thus, when one student is ordained, another takes his place. This means that when once the Burse Fund is completed, we shall be able to send forth a priest into 

God's vineyard every six years. 2 Motives

There was no difficulty in regard to the selection of a name for the Burse Fund. Ought not this honor be given to our beloved patroness, St. Lidwina of Schiedam, whose reverence for the priesthood was more than ordinary, and therefore worthy of our admiration and our imitation? 

Through the ministrations of God's priests many graces were given to St. Lidwina, especially that of patience and resignation. In return, she offered her prayers and sufferings for the clergy, thus drawing abundant graces from heaven for the living, and bringing deliverance to many a priestly soul languishing in the prison of Purgatory.

This project was mentioned for the first time to the members in the March, 1930, issue of "Our Good Samaritan." The idea was received with enthusiasm by the members. Contributions came in gradually from the sick residing in all parts of the United States. Most of these contributions were small amounts, the so-called "widow's mite," coming from those who were poor in this world's goods. Their gift was accompanied by the wish and prayer that others in better circumstances might be inspired to give of their abundance. 

Most interesting and edifying were the letters which accompanied the contributions, insofar as they mentioned the motive or intention that inspired the gift, and the ways and means whereby the money was obtained. One member in the state of New Hampshire induced her sister to hold a card party, the proceeds of which were sent to the Burse. Others sent the number of pennies equivalent to the number of years God had thus far given to them here on earth. Some gave their offering in memory of loved ones departed, and others in thanksgiving for favors received. Occasionally the gift represented the contents of a mite-box, or of a self-denial fund; then again it was the interest collected on a bond, or the amount of a pension check.

Contributions were received from members in 36 States of the Union, the District of Columbia, three Provinces of Canada, Nova Scotia, Ireland, and even from our member in Yokohama, Japan. The smallest amount received was ten cents, and the largest Fifty Dollars. The first bequest came in the form of a ten dollar check from the estate of Mrs. Mary Wurm, of Milwaukee in January, 1932. After that several other bequests were received, one of $25.00

St. Lidwina's Burse Fund 

115 from a member in Erie, Pennsylvania, who had been an invalid for many years; the other in the amount of $50.00 from an aged sailor in Baltimore, Maryland. Both of these members had sent contributions regularly during their lifetime, and made provisions that St. Lidwina's Burse

Fund would receive a portion of their estate when God called them home. In this way they expressed their gratitude for the many spiritual benefits they derived from their membership in the Apostolate of Suffering.

In May, 1934, we made the decision that upon the death of a Life Member of the Apostolate the amount of the membership, which is $10.00, would be turned over to St. Lidwina's Burse Fund. In this way, every member who takes out a Life Membership has the assurance that at his or her death this sum of money will immediately be applied toward the completion of this Burse. 

Thus far, four persons holding a Life Membership in the Apostolate have answered the Master's final summons. They are Miss Mary Kennedy of Ardmore, Pa., who died Dec. 25, 1931, Mrs. B. McInnerny, Omaha, Nebraska, died in April, 1934, Miss Elizabeth McEvoy of 

Carthage, Ohio, died August 7, 1934, and Miss Ada Mc- 

Gill, Pittsburgh, Pa., died February 3, 1936.

The first interest on invested funds was received in 

September, 1930, and amounted to $1.03. Now the annual amount of interest received is approximately $60.00. This of course helps to swell the fund considerably. 

In each issue of "Our Good Samaritan" a report of the standing of the Burse Fund is given, thus keeping the members informed of the progress of the same. 

To the sick, the priest takes the place of a father, confessor, advisor, and friend. In times of trials and sorrows,

in the dark hours of despondency and spiritual desolation, he is their staff and support. Furthermore, he remembers them and their manifold needs at the altar of God where remembrances are most sacred and where every thought is a prayer. What a source of consolation it is to the sick and infirm to know that their contributions will lead other students to the priesthood, upon whose prayers they will have a special claim, who will pray for them during their years of preparation, and will remember them after their ordination in every Holy Mass they will be privileged to offer. Then too, the contributors have the assurance that after their death, when all other relations and friends have forgotten them, these priests will continue to remember them when offering the Holy Sacrifice.

Who can enumerate the blessings and graces that have and will come to the Apostolate and all its members through the prayers of those priests who owe the realization of their hopes to the alms of the sick? Next to having a son or brother serving God in the priesthood there is no greater privilege than aiding the sons of other parents to reach this holy state. A Holy Bishop once said: “To raise temples to the glory of God is certainly praiseworthy but it is a still more beautiful charity to give to the Church a priest who will raise up living temples in the souls of men." The above objective is that for which St. Lidwina's . Burse Fund is being established. 3 Results 

As the Apostolate closed its tenth year of service in behalf of the sick, the account of St. Lidwina's Burse

Fund was as follows:

St. Lidwina's Burse Fund

No expenses are incurred, because the cost of mailing acknowledgments, and the printing of the little "thank you" holy pictures sent to each donor is borne by the Apostolate. 

Miss Gertrude Simmert of St. Anne's parish, Milwaukee, has always been in charge of the work connected with the Burse Fund.

Although the Burse Fund is growing very slowly, we entertain the fond hope of living to see it completed. We cherish even the higher hope that God in His Own good time and way will send us a priest filled with love for the sick and zeal for their sanctification, who will be free to devote his entire time to promote the Apostolate and to labor among the members even as Christ labored among the sick while He was on earth. This would mean that we shall also have need for a fairy godmother who will be able to supply the maintenance for the priest. All things are possible with God, and we feel that the fulfillment of this our ardent desire would be His way of rewarding the labor expended in establishing St. Lidwina's Burse.

The sentiments of gratitude which fill the heart of

every priest who reaches the priesthood through the means of a Burse Fund finds expression in the following beautiful poem found on so many First Mass souvenir cards.

Boyhood dreams of long ago 

Saw an altar fair,

Consecrated, trembling hands, Lifted there in prayer. 

And those dreams have led me on, Dreamlike though they seemed, Now, dear friend, thank God with me, I am what I dreamed!

CHAPTER X 

MISSION ACTIVITIES 

"Pray ye therefore the Lord of the harvest, that He send forth laborers into His harvest." -St. Matthew 9.38. 'HEN the list of intentions, for which the mem- Wbers of the Apostolate were to offer their sufferings, was formulated, the missions were given every consideration. The intention "The success of missionary work" includes all missionary endeavors in home and foreign countries, ranging from the prayers for vocations up to the Holy Father's missionary intentions. 

As Apostles for Christ, the sick foster a special interest in all missionary activities. As true lovers of Christ and souls they realize that they too, even though confined to their room, bed, or chair, can effectively co-operate in the sublime work of saving souls. To pray and suffer for the missions is truly an expression of apostolic zeal. It is an act of faith, for the cause for which they labor and pray is not visible to their eyes; an act of hope, for they rely on God's promise to grant their prayers; it is an act of unselfish charity, for they are not even acquainted with the objects of their solicitude. Christ in His own good time and way will reward abundantly such apostolic zeal. 119

Altar Linen Showers 

In the autumn of 1930 plans were made to enlarge this phase of mission work among the sick. In order to increase the love of the sick for the missions and to give them some active work to perform, arrangements were made for an altar linen shower to be given in honor of the Eucharistic Savior. Long dreary hours of the winter months could be spent in a profitable manner by plying the needle to fashion the various linens and other articles used at Holy Mass and in the administration of the Sacraments. Ardent prayers for temporal and spiritual favors could be woven into the dainty linens upon which the body of Christ would rest, and into the silken veils which would beautify His earthly home, His home of 

Love. 

The suggestion appealed to the members. Box after box of linens arrived; some linens were plain, others fancy, and some most artistically embroidered. Many of the invalids and shut-ins who were unable to sew or do fancy needlework sent cash donations. Numerous were the "spiritual bouquets" tucked in with the gifts; verily, the sick know that prayer is the greatest need of the missionary.

The recipient of this first altar linen shower sponsored by the Apostolate was the Rev. Harold J. Felsecker, M.M., of Milwaukee, a member of the Catholic Foreign Mission Society of America, better known as the Maryknoll 

Fathers, of Maryknoll, New York. He was ordained 

February 1, 1931, at the Seminary of St. Francis De Sales,

St. Francis, Wisconsin, and was preparing to sail for the 

Orient in the summer of the same year.

Mission Activities 

121 On the evening of the feast of St. Peter and St. Paul, June 29, 1931, Father Felsecker was guest of honor at the shower. A temporary altar had been erected; the decorations were in the papal colors of yellow and white, with an added touch of red, the color worn on the feast of martyrs. The front of the altar was adorned with the Maryknoll emblem, the XP surrounded by a circle. 

Within the altar were concealed 60 gift packages, each wrapped daintily in white tissue paper and tied with yellow ribbon. What a labor of love it had been to wrap these parcels! Not one of those present on that occasion will ever forget that evening, for the temperature was around 100 degrees. As Fr. Felsecker opened one package after another, exclamations of astonishment and joy were heard, as the loveliest of gifts were revealed. Among them were corporals, purificators, palls, altar cloths and laces, lavabo, sacristy, and finger towels, amices and cinctures, baptism and sick call stoles, surplices and albs, and other little articles. The total number of articles received was 

313. Within the tabernacle the most precious of the gifts were hidden, namely, a pyx, the little golden vessel, like the case of a watch, wherein the Blessed Sacrament is carried to the sick; a triple oil stock, the tube-like golden vessel for carrying the holy oils used in baptism and 

Extreme Unction; a five dollar gold piece to pay the ransom of a little pagan baby to be named Clara Marie. 

These articles were purchased from the cash donations which had been received from the members. Besides these gifts Father discovered a card case containing a check for $150.00 to be used in purchasing such articles of church goods as he may need after he commences active work in the fields afar. Last but certainly not least, he found

the magnificient spiritual bouquet of 158,871 flowers gathered by the members of the Apostolate. These were tastefully arranged in booklet form of ten pages, and artistically decorated with hand painting by Mr. Urban 

Ziegler of Milwaukee.

The sentiments of gratitude which surged through the heart and soul of Father Felsecker can be more easily imagined than expressed in words. Daily he remembers the members of the Apostolate at Holy Mass, weekly he recites the Divine Office for them, and each year on the feast of the Most Sacred Heart of Jesus he offers the Holy 

Sacrifice of the Mass in their behalf. On August 5, 1931, he left Milwaukee, and for over five years he has labored in Japan and Manchuria; at present he is secretaray to the 

Apostolic Delegate in Tokyo, Japan. After another five years we hope to welcome him into our midst again.

The success which accompanied this shower and the interest displayed by our members were so encouraging that two more showers were held in the following year. 

On June 29, 1932, a shower was held for Rev. Edward

A. Weis, M.M., also of Milwaukee, and a member of the 

Maryknoll community. He was ordained at Maryknoll, N. Y., June 5, 1932. In arrangement it was similar to that held for Fr. Felsecker. The gift packages contained 150 articles of various kinds, also a pyx, a triple oil stock, a five dollar gold piece for the ransom of a "Martha Marie,” a check for $50.00, and a Spiritual Bouquet consisting of 

32,000 flowers. On August 4, 1932, he bade farewell to

Milwaukee, and since then he has been laboring in the mission fields of Manchuria and Manchukuo. Like Fr. 

Felsecker, he too, remembers the sick at every Holy Mass, offers his breviary for them once a week, and once a year

Mission Activities 

123 on the feast of the Conversion of St. Paul, January 25, he offers the Holy Sacrifice for his friends in the Apostolate. 

How innumerable are the blessings which flow into the lives of the sick from these Holy Masses! Perhaps therein lies the secret of the heroic patience with which the sick bear their pain and suffering. Then again, in the prayers and sufferings of the sick lies the secret of the numerous and at times almost miraculous conversions witnessed by the missioners. Truly, even in the spiritual life there is a sort of give and take, for God is never outdone in generosity. 

Just two weeks later, on July 11, the feast of the sainted

Pope Pius I, a third shower was held. This time the recipient was the Rev. Leo Grode, S.V.D., who was ordained on May 22, 1932, at Techny, Illinois. Father 

Grode is a brother of Miss Mary Grode, Webster, South

Dakota, who had resided in Milwaukee for several years and had been very active in helping with the work of the Apostolate and in organizing the Good Samaritan Guild. The gifts received by Fr. Grode included 94 articles of altar linens, a triple oil stock, a check for $55.00, and a Spiritual Bouquet consisting of 32,294 flowers. His gratitude, like that of the other two missionaries, finds expression in the daily memento, the weekly Divine Office, and the annual Holy Mass on the feast of Our Lady of Lourdes, February 11, which he offers for his sick friends. Since September 1932, Father Grode has been teaching in the Seminary of Our Lady, Help of 

Christians, Binmaley, Pangasinan, Philippine Islands.

During the years following these showers, many members continued making altar linens in their leisure moments. Whatever we received we kept on hand until we

could fill a little box for some needy priest. On several occasions some articles were given to newly ordained priests; at other times to missionaries who went to labor in foreign fields.

An itemized report of what has been given by the sick to the missions in the line of spiritual and material offerings proves that those whom the Lord visits with sickness and infirmity are filling a special niche in the great missionary work of the Church.

These articles and prayers were given and offered by the members living in 22 States, also in three Provinces of Canada, and in the countries of Nova Scotia and Japan. 

Cancelled stamps and tinfoil are also gathered by the sick. This we turn over to some society that collects such articles for the missions. The above described activities provide a pleasant and a profitable pastime to wile away the many lonesome hours confronting the invalids and shut-ins. 2 

The Holy Father's Desires 

In one of His Encyclical Letters promoting Catholic missions, our Holy Father, Pope Pius XI, called “the Pope of the Missions," expressed the desire that the faithful make it a regular practice to pray for the missions. He referred to prayer as "the very nourishment of the missions," and said that "there should ascend to heaven every day a prayer that the Divine Mercy may descend

upon so many beings and upon such populous pagan nations." From this we draw the conclusion that the true mission spirit does not confine itself to an occasional or spasmodical offering of prayer and suffering for the mission cause, but rather, that it manifests itself by giving the missions a daily remembrance in our prayers, sacrifices, and above all in the sufferings we are called upon to endure. On one occasion our Holy Father remarked that “suffering is the highest form of prayer."

In order to comply with the Holy Father's wishes and desires, a regular phase or feature of mission work was introduced into the Apostolate in December, 1931. In the issue of "Our Good Samaritan" for that month, and in every succeeding issue, a certain missionary community, or a certain mission country, or some special missionary feature or work, was commended to the prayerful charity of the members for the coming three months.

Love for the missions should be universal according to the command of Our Lord Who said "preach the gospel to every creature"; therefore no restrictions are made in carrying out this mission feature. The first community to benefit from this enterprise was the Maryknoll 

Society for Foreign Missions. Why? Because this feature was started upon the suggestion of a member of that order, the Rev. Edward A. Weis. The Maryknoll Community is one of the youngest mission societies in the 

United States. 

Other missionary communities were commended to the prayers of the sick; a community was usually selected whose members are giving the sick a share in their prayers and the fruits of their labors. Here, too, the proverb, "One good turn deserves another" is applied.

Mission Activities 

127 Besides the Maryknoll Fathers mentioned above, the following communities have been the recipients of the prayers of this Pious Union of the Sick: St. Columbans 

Mission Society of St. Columbans, Nebraska; the Society of the Divine Word, Techny, Illinois; the Society of the 

Divine Savior, St. Nazianz, Wisconsin; the Priests of the Sacred Heart, the Pallotine Fathers, Milwaukee, Wisconsin, the Missionary Catechists of Huntington, Indiana, the Jesuits, and three Franciscan Orders. This always includes the priests, brothers, and sisters of the order, the countries wherein, and the people for whom they labor. 

Individual mission needs are remembered too, namely, missioners held in captivity, exiled and persecuted bishops, priests, and christians; the Negro, Indian, and missions of other races in our own country; vocations, and every other possible phase connected with missionary work. 

Truly, the needs of the missions at home and abroad are so manifold that the days of the month are not sufficient to permit of taking but one intention for each day. A

Maryknoll practice has been suggested to our members: when you have finished saying the rosary, go back to the cross and offer the two Our Fathers and three Hail Marys for the missions. Once this beautiful practice becomes a habit, then no day will pass without us having done our little bit to further the interests of the Heart of Jesus and to spread His Kingdom here on earth.

The attention of the sick was also called to the observance of Mission Sunday held annually on the third Sunday of October. Participation in the universal observance of the Church Unity Octave held each year from the Feast of St. Peter's Chair to the Feast of the Conversion of St. Paul on January 25 is commended to the

members. Prayers for the success of the various missionary conventions, especially those of the Mission Crusade is another of the many ways in which the afflicted help the mission cause. 3 The Day of the Sick for the Missions Within the last few years a movement was inaugurated in Rome through the efforts of the Missionary Union of the Clergy. In the year 1930, all the sick and infirm were asked to offer their prayers and sufferings on Pentecost Sunday for the Missionary Intentions of the Holy Father. 

In 1932 the movement was extended to all Italy, and since then its observance has spread into the countries of 

Belgium, Spain, and elsewhere.

When the Holy Father was informed of the spread of this movement, and told of the numerous prayers and sufferings offered for himself and the missions, he exclaimed: "Beautiful, beautiful is this bond between the suffering children and the Common Father. This enterprise is beautiful and holy, beautiful in its significance of love for the Pope and the Missions and holy in its divine efficacy." His Holiness then expressed the wish of seeing this movement extended to other countries of the world.

In the year 1935 this praiseworthy enterprise was carried out here in America for the first time through our Apostolate. The 5,000 members who comprise this association were invited to offer all their prayers and sufferings on Pentecost Sunday, June 9, for the missions according to the Holy Father's intentions. Thereby, that most vital force, divine grace, was to descend upon the

Mission Activities 

129 missions even as it came upon the Apostles on that first Pentecost through the descent of the Paraclete, the 

Divine Dispenser of grace. 

A copy of the June issue containing the announcement of the inauguration of this movement here in America was sent to the Holy Father together with a letter assuring him of our interest and pledging our wholehearted support. We were pleasantly surprised when some months later the following letter was received from the Supreme 

Shepherd of Souls.

The Vatican, 

July 28, 1935. 

His Eminence Cardinal Pacelli, Secretary of State of His

Holiness begs to inform Miss Clara M. Tiry, Secretary of the 

Apostolate of Suffering, that the Holy Father was very happy to learn of the arrangements made by the Apostolate for the participation of the sick in America in the observance of Pentecost Sunday as a day of prayer for the Mission Intentions of His Holiness. From His Heart, the Holy Father sends to the members of the Apostolate the Apostolic Benediction. 

In 1936 the movement was carried out on a larger scale. Printed leaflets on which the sick could mark their various spiritual offerings were sent to every member of the Apostolate and to the hospitals in the Archdiocese of Milwaukee. The local branch of the Society for the 

Propagation of the Faith collaborated with us in this work. Pentecost Sunday, May 31, 1936, brought a shower of spiritual blossoms upon the missions. The guardian angels of the sick must have been kept busy in gathering these flowers and carrying them to the throne of God. 

The sick were very generous with their prayers and sacrifices; the total number of flowers received amounted to 

1,351,614. These were contributed by members living in

thirty-six States, five Provinces of Canada, Nova Scotia, Panama, the Virgin Islands, and Scotland. What a con solation it is to the sick to know that they have a large share in the harvest of souls which the missionaries gather into the granary of heaven.
