CHAPTER XI CATHOLIC ACTION AND THE SICK 

"Wherefore let them also that suffer according to the will of God, commend their souls in good deeds to the faithful Creator." 

1 Peter 4. 19.

IN N THESE days, Catholic Action, so forcibly stressed and urged by the Holy Father, Pope Pius XI, is generally thought to have meaning only for groups of persons in good physical health. Every day we hear and read about the fine work performed by them. What about the sick, the infirm, and the shut-ins? Are they not to have a share in and partake of the blessed work of promoting the kingdom of Christ?

It is said that the blood of martyrs is the seed of Christians. We find that this was true in the early days of the 

Church. Persecutions did not efface Christianity, nay, each one brought forth new life, yes, a veritable new army of souls to fight for Christ. In those countries where the 

Church is a victim of the cruel and unjust dictates of its rulers, where her members must worship in secrecy, and where she must sacrifice not only her faithful flock but the shepherds also, she will in God's good time rise and come forth from the tomb endowed with new life even as did her Founder, Jesus Christ, on that first Resurrection morn. 131

Is there no value for Catholic Action attached to the pain, the suffering, the loneliness, the sacrifices, and the countless other hardships that must be endured by those heroic souls who are called to follow Christ on the way of the cross? We count them as privileged souls, as those "whom the Lord loveth." How great then must be the efficacy of their sufferings which form a very martyrdom extending not over a few days, weeks, or months, but so often over scores of years. Within them is the power of an apostolic spirit, chained it is true by a body rendered helpless through disease or infirmity, yet, from the seclusion of the sickroom there rises unto God like incense the sweet savor of suffering.

The Liturgy 

The first requisite of a laborer in Catholic Action must be sanctity. The fruit of good works is in proportion to the apostolic spirit of the sower. Therefore, in our work with the sick special attention was given to help them live in closer union with Christ and the Church. 

Even as the sunflower turns to the sun, so do the members of the Church and the sick in particular turn to the Holy Sacrifice of the Mass offered on countless altars as the center of our religion. Perhaps no one realizes this truth more than the sick who are deprived from attending in person. They are urged to send their representative, their legate, in the person of their guardian angel to attend the Holy Sacrifice while they at home unite in spirit with the priest and the faithful. Through the use of a daily missal or at least a Sunday missal they find it a comfort to travel through the year with Christ. Praying the self-

same words as the priest at the altar is very meritorious; furthermore, it helps to avoid routine, for each day offers new prayers, new epistles and gospels, and new scripture texts for their inspiration and meditation.

The three Sacraments so important in the life of the sick, namely, the cleansing sacrament of Penance, the strengthening Food of the Holy Eucharist especially in the form of Viaticum, and the comforting and healing oils of Extreme Unction were called to their attention. 

These Sacraments are the great channels of the supernatural life, instituted by Christ Himself. Short explanations and meditations, together with approved prayers in preparation and thanksgiving for their reception appeared in various issues of "Our Good Samaritan.' 

The sacramentals, or smaller channels of supernatural life, instituted by the church were explained during the course of these ten years. Among them were Holy Water, Agnus Deis, Relics, and others. All these are little aids through which the sick can increase their sanctity and thus give to their sufferings and prayers a greater efficacy. 

Suggestions for the sanctification of their lives are also given to the sick in abundant measure. To live and feel with the Church is to view the annual re-enactment of the principal events of the work of redemption. To live and feel with the church is to understand her; to understand is to love; loving the Church will increase love for 

Christ, and love for Christ will make for sanctity. 

Advent sounds the warning note to prepare the heart as a cozy crib for the Divine Infant, each act of mortification, each pain, and each ache is a straw to fill the manger. Christmas with its three Holy Masses is a season of joy before which even the grim spectre of suffering

must retreat; Sylvester night affords pious souls the opportunity to spend the last half hour of the old year and the first half of the new year in reparation and thanksgiving to the Giver of all good gifts; New Years Day brings with it the formulation of new resolutions for increased fervor in the service of God, greater patience in suffering, and more complete resignation to His Holy Will. The winter months speak of the hidden life of Our Lord in Nazareth, so like that of the sick in their hours of loneliness and confinement.

The pre-lenten season leads through the public life of Our Lord, down to the solemn services of Ash Wednesday. With the shadow of Good Friday drawing nearer and nearer, suffering takes on a different aspect and its true significance is brought home to the soul. One dare not complain when the bitter sufferings of Christ are the object of meditation. The sick live always in the shadow of the cross, but Good Friday passes too and soon Mother Church commemorates the joys of the Resurrection of Her Lord and Spouse. It is then that the sick can say with St. Paul, "I reckon that the sufferings of this time are not worthy to be compared with the glory to come, that shall be revealed in us." Towards the close of the Paschal season Holy Mother Church bids her children to spend three days in prayer and supplication that the blessing of God might descend upon the newlysown seed in the field. Rogation Days - who can make better use of them than the sick? Theirs is the privilege to pray that the harvest will be abundant. 

Christ's ascension into heaven from the mount of Olives invites the sick to "seek the things that are above," and is an incentive to rise from the sorrows of earth to the contemplation of the joys of heaven.

The timorous apostles gathered with Mother Mary in the Cenacle introduce the first novena as they wait for the coming of the Paraclete. After the descent of the Holy 

Spirit they venture forth into the world in quest of souls. 

A similar transformation takes place within the hearts of the sick; the saying "tell me with whom you go and I will tell you what you are" can be applied to them also.

As they follow Christ from His birth in Bethlehem to 

His ascension into heaven, and then spend the remainder of the ecclesiastical year in company with Him, His Blessed Mother, the apostles and the glorious army of saints, new strength, new life, and new vigor fill their souls. With St. Paul they can say "I can do all things in 

Him Who strengthens me," and in all sincerity they can whisper to the Lord of their heart, "Master, go on, and I will follow Thee 

To the last gasp with love and loyalty."

The Ember Days 

For some reason or other, seminarians have always occupied a large place in our hearts. Perhaps it is because our Visitor is published in the months when the Ember days occur, perhaps because of the fact that our Spiritual Director was so intimately connected with a Seminary, the training-school of future priests. Ember days have been instituted to pray for a bountiful harvest as well as to ask for the gifts of the Holy Spirit in behalf of those who are to be admitted to Holy Orders. All Catholics have a grave obligation to pray for these levites, who are preparing for the priesthood for, a priest is ordained not for himself but for the people. Throughout these years this intention and the observance of the Ember

days has been recommended to the sick, and from personal contacts we know that they have complied with our requests. Those who pray for an apostle also share in the labors of an apostle. Could we desire a greater reward? 

What joy, too, floods the hearts of the sick on ordination days after having prayed fervently for these levites who soon are to be "other Christs."

The Holy Year 

The privilege of living in the year which commemorated the nineteenth centenary of the death of Our Lord was deeply appreciated by the Apostolate and its band. of patient crossbearers. Every effort was made to bring the fruits of this Extraordinary Jubilee Year to them by suggesting various prayers, practices, and meditations. which they could perform in the privacy of the sickroom. 

The entire issue of March, 1933, was dedicated to commemorate this Jubilee; every article referred to some event connected with Our Lord's passion and death. A special program of prayer was outlined for Good Friday, March 30, 1934. A Holy Mass closing the Holy Year was offered for them on April 2, 1934.

The Holy Father in his solicitude for those of his flock who could not travel to Rome to gain the indulgence of this Jubilee was moved to extend it to the whole world in the following year. Later on it was extended for almost another year, namely to April 28, 1935. Members were urged to avail themselves of this opportunity and to consult their confessors who were empowered to change the requirements to suit their condition of health. 

In observance of the solemn closing of the Jubilee held at the shrine of Our Lady in Lourdes, France, a Triduum

of Holy Masses was held for our members, on April 26, 27, and 28, 1935. In this way we tried to bring to them just a portion of the good things which those in better health can enjoy. Their letters of gratitude in appreciation of our solicitude in their behalf served as a stimulant to be even more generous to them on future occasions. 2 

Eucharistic Congresses 

Eucharistic Congress Days are dear to our hearts because it was during the 28th International Eucharistic Congress held in Chicago in June, 1926, that the idea of the Apostolate of Suffering was conceived. For that reason we have always regarded the Eucharistic Heart of Jesus as our special Protector, and we feel that at the time of a Eucharistic Congress He showers abundant graces upon our work, our members, and our benefactors. 

The spirit of gratitude prompts us to bid a hearty welcome to each succeeding congress, and to do all in our power to promote its ultimate success. 

The first Eucharistic Congress held after the founding of the Apostolate took place in Sydney, Australia, from 

September 6-9, in the year 1928.

The Fathers of the Blessed Sacrament in New York planned to gather a huge spiritual bouquet of 100,000,000 "flowers" for the success of this Eucharistic Congress, and invited all Catholics to contribute their offerings. Through their generosity we distributed 5,000 blanks to our members on which they were asked to list the "flowers" they gathered.

Our efforts met with a greater response than we antic-

ipated. The total number of flowers gathered amounted to 1,346,832. A copy of this report beautifully arranged and decorated with hand painting by Mr. Urban Ziegler of Milwaukee, together with all the slips was sent to the 

Fathers of the Blessed Sacrament. They were very grateful for the co-operation given by the sick in this great undertaking.

Arrangements were made to have a Holy Mass offered for the members on each day of the Congress. A fourpage leaflet, dedicated to Our Lady of the Blessed Sacrament, containing a devotional program for each day of the Congress was issued by us and sent to each member. 

As a token of our appreciation for their generous response a Holy Mass was offered for all members who contributed "spiritual flowers."

The next International Eucharistic Congress was the thirtieth one, and was held in Carthage, Northern Africa, from May 7-11, 1930. Through the kindness of the 

Fathers of the Blessed Sacrament we were able to distribute 5,000 leaflets containing a prayer for the success of the Congress. Moreover, the members were asked to offer their prayers, works, and sufferings for the same intention on the days when Our Eucharistic King received the public homage, honor, and adoration of the faithful. Thus their inability to travel over land and sea to take an active part in the festivities did not constitute a hindrance in glorifying God, but rather, their sacrifices and sufferings generously offered served as a magnet to draw rays of light and peace, grace and blessing, joy and happiness into their souls. Suggestions for spending the days in close communion with the festive services held in Carthage were outlined in "Our Good Samaritan."

Thereby the sickroom was transformed into a miniature oratory for the Lover of souls where He might reign supreme. A Holy Mass was offered for the members on each of the four days of the Congress. One of our priest members from the state of Massachusetts attended the Congress; while there he included all the sick in his Holy Masses.

Time passes rapidly. In spring of 1932 preparations were made for the triumphant coming of Christ into Ireland, that land where for so many centuries Catholics suffered fierce persecutions, and yet remained true to the faith. This thirty-first congress was held from June 

22-26 in Dublin. As we record the celebrations of this nature, we are reminded of the manifold sacrifices demanded from the sick. Though their devotion to the 

Holy Eucharist is as great or perhaps even greater than that of other faithful members of the Church, they are not able to manifest this devotion by being present in person; neither are they able to contribute to the success of a congress by material means; yet, faith tells us that their prayers and sufferings, their hidden acts of faith and love which ascend to heaven draw God's richest blessings upon the active works of their healthier brethren. 

A copy of the official prayer for the Congress was reprinted in the March, 1932, issue of our little paper with the request that the members recite it daily. The June issue contained a program of daily prayers and devotions to be followed during the days of the Congress. 

Again the Apostolate was fortunate enough to be represented by one of its priest members from Wisconsin; he placed the prayers and petitions of the sick before Jesus in the Holy Eucharist during those days of triumph. A

Holy Mass was offered for the members on each of the five days of the Congress.

In the issue of September, 1934, a brief mention was made and prayers implored for the success of the thirtysecond International Eucharistic Congress which was held in Buenos Aires, South America, from October 10-14.

The members were urged to pray that through the Congress many souls would be brought to a greater love for and a more intimate union with Jesus in the Holy 

Eucharist. 

National Eucharistic Congresses 

While International Congresses draw and hold the attention and interest of the faithful in all parts of the world, National Congresses serve to promote and increase the love for the Blessed Sacrament among the people of one nation. The sixth National Eucharistic Congress here in the United States was held in Omaha, Nebraska, from 

September 23-25, 1930. Again the Fathers of the Blessed

Sacrament sent us 5,000 leaflets for distribution among our members, and 1,000 stickers for use on our envelopes.

On that occasion we were privileged to have our Spiritual Director represent us. A Holy Mass was offered for the members every day during the Congress. The September issue of "Our Good Samaritan" was dedicated to the 

Prisoner of Love; through it we pledged the love and loyalty of the sick to the Holy Eucharist. 

The next National Eucharistic Congress was scheduled to take place in September, 1935, in the city of Cleveland, Ohio. Through the kindness of His Excellency, the Most 

Reverend Joseph Schrembs, D.D., of Cleveland, 6,000 holy cards bearing the official prayer for the success of the

Congress were sent to the members of the Apostolate with the June issue. His Excellency also made arrangements for a Holy Mass to be offered for the sick in the city of Cleveland on each of the four days of the Congress. We were privileged in having our spiritual director, then Bishopelect of Fargo, represent the Apostolate during the days when Cleveland was host to our Eucharistic King. 

Thus we have always tried to foster among our members the spirit of love, devotion, and loyalty to Jesus in the Blessed Sacrament, the source of all blessings and graces, the very center of Catholic life. For, it is there that He is the Divine Exemplar for all who are destined to dwell in captivity. 

I love Thee, O Jesus, on Tabor's height 

Alone with Thy chosen three, But here Thou remainest both day and night 

A Prisoner for love of me.

I love Thee, O Jesus, on Calvary's Cross With its cruel arms extended; 

When I think of the cost of this Sacrament, My love with pain is blended. 

I love Thee, O Jesus, my very God 

In heaven's highest sphere, But nowhere in heaven or earth so much 

As in this sweet Sacrament here. 3 

Future Saints 

The quickness with which processes of beatifications and canonizations are sometimes carried out, depends to a great extent upon the number of interested people

who are behind the movement. Three approved miracles must be accredited to the direct intercession of the person whose cause has been introduced. It is but natural that the more people become interested in such a cause, the sooner and the more chance there is for such miracles to take place. The names of many holy persons are never inscribed in the Church's official register of saints simply because not enough persons manifest confidence in their saintly lives. They form the army of the uncanonized saints of God.

The first person whose cause we helped to promote was the saintly Rev. William J. Doyle, S.J., or "Fr. Willie" as he is generally called by those who knew him. At various times during my years of illness, an improvement in my condition was ascribed to his intercession. Leaflets of Fr. 

Doyle had been sent to me by his brother in Ireland.

There were no leaflets of him available in the United States. Therefore, prompted by gratitude for favors received, we felt that through our Apostolate much could be accomplished to promote his cause. With the permission of the Rev. Charles Doyle in Ireland, arrangements were made for the printing of leaflets. Our late Archbishop, the Most Reverend S. G. Messmer, took an interest in the work, and gave his consent and hearty approval to the publication of the leaflet. The first leaflets were sent out with the first printed copy of "Our Good 

Samaritan" in March, 1927. Since then over 24,000 leaflets of Fr. Doyle have been distributed gratis through the

Apostolate to its members, and thousands were sold to those who became interested in him. Many favors received through his intercession have been reported to our office.

In collaboration with the Redemptorist Fathers in Philadelphia, Pennsylvania, pictures and leaflets describing the life of the Venerable Bishop, John N. Neumann, the fourth bishop of Philadelphia, were sent out with the issue of December, 1928. Many miracles have been wrought through his intercession, and we feel that through fervent prayers and earnest petitions the day can be hastened when one of America's bishops will be raised to the honors of the altar. 

The life of the saintly Indian maiden, Catherine

Tekawitha, who was born and lived on American soil is dear to the heart of every loyal Catholic and American. 

Catherine was born in the year 1656, and lost her parents at an early age. Through observing the prayers of the missionaries, who at times lodged with her uncle, the desire for baptism was aroused in her. This grace was given her on Easter Sunday of 1676. Meditating on the sufferings of her Lord enkindled in her the desire to suffer with and for Him. God granted her desire, and on 

April 17, 1680, she passed away while whispering the words "Jesus, I love You."

About 5,000 leaflets bearing a sketch of her life and the prayer for her beatification were sent to the members with the June issue of the Visitor in 1932. This issue also contained an article about her. Will not the prayers and sufferings of those who like Catherine bear the cross of pain help to bring about her speedy beatification? This is our hope and prayer.

Another Apostle whose cause we brought to the attention of our members was that of the saintly priest, Rev. 

Stephen Eckert, O.M.Cap. Mindful that the colored mis-

sions deserve the support of the prayers and sufferings of the sick, a little article about Fr. Stephen was inserted in the September issue in 1933. How better can we fulfill our obligation to the missions than by praying for the beatification of one who labored so zealously for the uplift of the colored race right here in the city of Milwaukee, the birthplace of the Apostolate?

Fr. Stephen was fired with the desire to labor for the uplift of these neglected people, desirous to remove the barriers of prejudice, and eager to create a sympathetic understanding for them among the whites. Great was his joy when he was appointed their valiant champion. The years of his labors among them were not many in number but filled with marvelous results. On one of his journeys he contracted pneumonia, and came home to die among the people whom he loved so dearly. His last words were: "Here I lie while thousands are perishing." On the 16th of February, 1923, he died, but his spirit still lives on. The work for which he sacrificed his life is flourishing, and increasing with each passing year.

Numerous conversions are wrought and souls brought to the true faith by those who are following in the footsteps of this humble Franciscan priest. Thousands of leaflets and many hundreds of little particles of his clothing were distributed among our members. His grave is in Calvary 

Cemetery, Milwaukee, and when we visit this his last resting place we commend to him the needs of all our members.

When we think of truly heroic souls, the name of Father Damien, the Apostle of the Lepers, is one of the first to come into our minds. Twenty-five years he spent as a missionary in the Hawaiian Islands; of this number

sixteen were devoted to the service and the care of the unfortunate lepers in Molokai, until he, too, fell a victim to this disease. 

Through the kindness of the Fathers of the Sacred 

Hearts of Jesus and Mary in Fairhaven, Massachusetts, a large supply of leaflets was received, sufficient for all our members. These were sent out with "Our Good 

Samaritan" in December, 1932. The leaflets contained a picture of Father Damien, a short sketch of his life, and a prayer for his beatification.

We welcomed the opportunity of promoting the cause of these and other saintly men and women who have been magnanimous in the practice of true Christian 

Charity as commanded by our Blessed Lord when He said, "Thou shalt love thy neighbor as thyself." The sick have a wonderful opportunity and plentiful time to devote to prayer for the beatification of such servants of God. New canonized saints mean more powerful intercessors before the throne of God, more intercessors will mean more conversions, and more conversions will eventually mean more citizens in the kingdom of God. 

There will be an increase in the honor and glory given to 

God here on earth, and also more adoration and praise given to Him throughout the endless ages of eternity. 

Who would not be proud to participate in the promotion of so noble a cause? You, dear reader, are invited to do SO. 4 

Helps to Holiness

In the course of the years we recommended various associations to our members as we thought would prove

to be of great value to them in striving for perfection. 

The first one to be thus mentioned was "The League of Intercession for Priests," promoted by the Apostleship of Prayer. This association fits in very well with the scope of our work, especially as one of the intentions of the Apostolate is "the sanctification of priests." The ends aimed at are threefold, namely, thanksgiving - for the institution of the priesthood; reparation- for all sins which in any way are committed against this divine benefit; petitions for an increase in the number of vocations to the priesthood, abundance of graces for the perseverance and an increase of holiness for priests, and the release of the souls of priests detained in purgatory. 

Five thousand leaflets of explanation and the daily prayer of intercession were purchased from the office of The Apostleship of Prayer, and sent to our members with the September, 1929, issue of "Our Good Samaritan." 

The command of Christ, "Pray ye, therefore, that the

Lord of the harvest send laborers into His harvest" was meant for the generation of the present day as well as for the people living at that time.

A second organization brought to the earnest consideration of our members was "The Archconfraternity of Prayer and Penance." Its aim is to repair by voluntary acts of penance and prayer the ingratitude, coldness, and sacrileges of individuals and nations against the Sacred Person of Our Lord, especially in the Holy Eucharist. 

The spirit of reparation is another of the intentions formulated by the Apostolate. It is always best, however, to have some fixed rule to follow; belonging to an association devoted solely to the spirit of reparation provides such a rule. The members are asked to offer one day each

week, each fortnight, or each month (according to their choice), in prayer and penance in union with our Lord's own spirit of reparation. These days are called "Expiation 

Days." Various practices are suggested on the leaflets issued by the Archconfraternity; these are adaptable to the individual member.

The purpose and aims of this Archconfraternity appealed to the members and in response to the article in the June, 1930, issue of our little paper, 73 members sent their names to us for enrollment. Later on more names were received, making a total of 300 new recruits in that great army of souls who make atonement to the Heart of Christ. The Reverend Director upon receiving so many new members expressed his pleasure in the following beautiful words: "In the crusade of Reparation the members of “The Apostolate of Suffering' are in the front rank. By their very state they are the leaders and the captains of the holy war raged against the corruption of the world and the innumerable sins which come from excessive pleasure and self-indulgence. "It is as encouragement to me, as director, to know that I have the moral support and the help of so many suffering members of Christ. As a token of gratitude I am asking all our associates to pray for them."

The general center of the Archconfraternity is at Montmartre, Paris, that for the United States at Assumption 

College in Worcester, Massachusetts.

Another movement promoted by us through our Apostolate and the pages of our little quarterly was that of the "Priests Saturday." The purpose of this movement is to

induce the faithful in general and the sick in particular to dedicate the Saturday following the First Friday to intercessory prayer for the priests of God. The movement received the praise and blessing of the Holy Father and is therefore deserving of everyone's careful consideration. This noble crusade will not fail to draw God's blessing upon the recipient and the giver. Through the courtesy of the Salvatorian Fathers of St. Nazianz, Wis., about 10,000 leaflets were distributed to and through our members. It was an encouragement to receive numerous requests for additional leaflets from our members. The first leaflets were sent out with the June issue of 1935, and later on to new members with several future issues. 

We are confident that the extra work entailed by promoting these various laudable movements is like "bread cast upon the waters"; it comes back to us a hundred-fold in showers of graces and blessings.

CHAPTER XII MEMORABLE DAYS 

"These things we write to you, that you may rejoice, and that your joy may be full." -1 John 1.4. ITHIN the ten years of our work, many important events took place, giving joy to our hearts. 

Some of them were of importance to the entire world, others were of interest only to us and to our members. A few of these deserve to be incorporated into this volume. The Holy Father's Message to the Sick More than ever before were the benefits of the radio appreciated on that memorable day of Thursday, February 12, 1931, when for the first time in the history of the Church, the voice of the Holy Father was heard over the radio by the faithful living in all parts of the world. 

Listening to the voice of the Supreme Pontiff brought untold joy to the hearts of his devoted children, and the text of his message will live forever in their memory. 

Having greeted the faithful in general, he delivered a special message to the various classes of his flock. Then with ineffable tenderness in his voice he spoke to the sick and afflicted. From his lips there came blessed words of comfort, to console all who are and will be called to bear 149

the cross of pain and suffering. The text of that message is of such importance that we feel it deserves to be recorded in this volume thus keeping it ever before our minds. "Our last word is reserved for you, last in time but first in thought and affection. To you who are ill and suffering, oppressed by trials and adversity. We assure you of our prayers, and commend you to the charity of all. As Christ's representative we say to you, 'Come to Me, all you that labor and are burdened, and I will refresh you.""

How the hearts of all the sick must have been stirred to their very depths by such words of solace, and by the knowledge that they are the objects of the Holy Father's special solicitude and prayers. What a privilege to occupy the first place in his heart and his affections.

An account of this important event was given in the pages of our little quarterly. To it was appended the reminder that such privileges call for a slight return, and that prayers and sufferings constitute a means of giving joy to our Holy Father even as other gifts bring joy to our natural fathers for the care they bestow on us. Is not the 

Holy Father our Father in Christ? 2 

His Golden Sacerdotal Jubilee 

The privilege of celebrating the golden jubilee of Ordination is granted to comparatively few of God's priests. It is a very rare and therefore memorable occasion and deserving of joyful commemoration.

Memorable Days 

151 When this extraordinary privilege was granted by Our Lord to Our Holy Father, Pope Pius XI, on December 20, 1929, there was great rejoicing in the hearts of all Catholics.

From all parts of the world tokens of filial devotion were sent to the Supreme Pontiff during the year of his golden jubilee. Priests and religious, men and women, boys and girls, secular and religious organizations vied with one another in sending some message of felicitation to the Holy Father.

Desiring to add our tribute of love and loyalty to rejoice the heart of the Holy Father, our Apostolate made plans to gather a huge bouquet of spiritual flowers. 

Printed blanks whereon the spiritual blossoms could be recorded were sent to the members with the March, 1929, issue of "Our Good Samaritan." The holy season of Lent offered many opportunities for gathering flowers of every description in the garden of prayer and suffering. The goal to be reached was set at One Million flowers. 

The response to this appeal went beyond our fondest expectations. As blank after blank arrived here at the office we soon realized that we would "go over the top" of the goal we had in view. Miss Marie Wagner volunteered to tabulate the figures as the blanks were received. 

This was an enormous task, but it was Marie's share of the bouquet. Many hours of labor were devoted to this work, but our hearts beat high with gratitude when we found that instead of our goal of One Million there were over Thirty-eight Million flowers received. The exact number was 38,120,500. They were contributed by members living in thirty-three States, and in Canada, Newfoundland, Nova Scotia, Ireland, South India, South 

Africa, and Palestine.

These spiritual flowers were arranged in booklet form, enclosed within parchment covers, beautifully decorated with hand painting in designs appropriate for the occasion. Mr. Urban Ziegler of Milwaukee donated the work of his artistic skill as his personal token of devotion to the Holy Father. The booklet, together with a letter conveying the felicitations of the members was sent to the Supreme Pontiff on the feast of St. John of the Cross, November 24, 1929. In this way, the members were afforded the consolation of knowing that although they could not present their message of congratulations and their gift in person they were doing it through the medium of their own association, the Apostolate of Suffering.

On the day of the Jubilee, a Holy Mass was offered for the Holy Father. The members were asked to receive 

Holy Communion on or around that day, and to offer It and all the other prayers, work, good works, and sufferings of the day itself for His Holiness so that through the intercessory power of these offerings Christ Our King and Eternal High Priest might be moved to confer countless gifts of body and soul upon His Vicar here on earth.

Our appreciation for the wholehearted co-operation given by our members in this worthy cause found expression in the Holy Mass offered for all who contributed flowers to this spiritual bouquet. 

Just about a year later, on the feast of St. Catherine, November 25, 1930, the following acknowledgment of our

Spiritual Bouquet was received from the Holy Father.

Memorable Days 

153 

Dear Miss Tiry: 

The Vatican, November 2, 1930.

It is my pleasant duty to express, through Your Illustrious Highness, to each member of The Apostolate of Suffering, the appreciation of the Holy Father for the Spiritual Bouquet which in so humble and gracious a manner you have laid at the feet of the Vicar of Christ on the occasion of his Golden 

Sacerdotal Jubilee. 

His Holiness sends forth from his Heart august prayers for the growth of a work so piously begun and imparts from his heart to all, to the director, and to the associated members the strength of his Apostolic Blessing.

With sentiments of esteem, I beg to remain, E. CARDINAL PACELLI. 

Those treasured words of appreciation from the Vicar of Christ were an incentive to his infirm children to continue walking bravely on the rugged path of suffering. 

What better return can be made to His Holiness than sending forth from our innermost hearts the prayer that "The Lord preserve him and give him life, and make him blessed upon the earth; and deliver him not up to the will of his enemies." 3 

A Consecration 

In the spring of 1933 our hearts were gladdened by the news that our kind friend, the Very Reverend James A. 

Walsh, M.M., Co-founder and Superior General of the 

Maryknoll Fathers was elevated to the Episcopacy and appointed Titular Bishop of Sienna. Bishop-elect Walsh's zeal and labor for the missions are known to all mission lovers of America. Perhaps it was from him and his spirit-

tual sons that we caught a spark of the love for the missions which glowed in the soul of this courageous man thereby re-enkindling our own love for the souls still dwelling in the spiritual darkness of paganism. 

Bishop-elect Walsh was consecrated on June 29, 1933, on the feast of the Apostles St. Peter and St. Paul, by 

Cardinal Fumasoni-Biondi, in the church of the new college for the Propagation of the Faith, in Rome, Italy. 

This was the twenty-second anniversary of the foundation of the Maryknoll Fathers, or according to their official title, "The Catholic Foreign Mission Society of America." 

Bishop Walsh was one of our spiritual benefactors in as much as he offered the Divine Office for the members of the Apostolate on the first day of every month. 

It was while in Rome that Bishop Walsh procured for us our most precious treasure, the relic of the True Cross of Christ. Each evening as we kneel in prayer before it we remember the soul of the good bishop who we hope is mindful also of us as he breathes his intercessions before the throne of God. 4 

Distinguished Visitors 

Another memorable day in the history of the Apostolate was the First Friday of December, 1930. On that day, December 5th, our new Archbishop, the Most Reverend 

Samuel A. Stritch, D.D., deigned to honor our office with his presence. 

Archbishop Stritch received his appointment to the See of Milwaukee, on August 26, 1930, and was installed amid solemn ceremonies as the head of the Archdiocese in

Memorable Days 

155 St. John's Cathedral on the feast of St. Elizabeth, November 19, 1930.

For many days, nay weeks, I had anticipated the arrival of His Excellency, yet, when he did come, accompanied by our Spiritual Director, the hour found me in bed, unable even to rise and bid him welcome. Thus, many times we plan our mode of action for a certain occasion, rehearse just what we shall say, yet when the anticipated moment arrives, our finely laid plans crumble into a desolate heap of ruins. 

It was not the first time, however, that His Excellency sat at the bedside of a sick person, and so he felt quite at home. We spoke about the various phases of our work, about its foundation, and its rapid progress down to the present day. The Apostolate itself, the Little Flower 

Library, the St. Lidwina Burse Fund, the Good Samari tan Guild, all were discussed in the course of the conversation.

His Excellency remarked that he had watched with great interest the progress of the Apostolate ever since the first copy of "Our Good Samaritan" was sent to him in the year 1927 when he was Bishop of Toledo, Ohio. 

He marvelled at its rapid growth, regarded the work as most comforting to the sick and afflicted, and stated that there was a great field of labor before us with many opportunities of doing good unto stricken humanity.

Our hearts rejoiced when His Excellency promised to remember the members at his daily Holy Mass. Then with his gracious smile he said: "But I hope the members will also pray for me." I assured him that they would consider it a privilege to do so. 

Ever since that memorable day, His Excellency has

continued his interest in the progress of the Apostolate, which is carried on under his fatherly protection. His gracious comments, inspiring words, and encouraging guidance coupled with the assurance of his fervent prayers are at all times a stimulus towards carrying on our labor of love and enlarging the scope of our work. 

It seems that Divine Providence in its eternal plans decreed that whenever a Bishop visits our home he finds me a real suffering member of the Apostolate. Such was the case again on the 21st of March, 1934, when we were granted the privilege of welcoming the Most Rev. James

A. Walsh, D.D., of Maryknoll, N. Y. We always refer to that day as “founder's day” because of the fact that we, both founders, met for the first time on the feast of a founder, the great St. Benedict.

At an early morning hour, the ringing of the telephone summoned mother, and the message informed her that Bishop Walsh would be at our home within a half hour. 

Consternation reigned for a few minutes, for due to my illness, I was not at all prepared to receive visitors, and especially a member of the hierarchy, but within an incredible short space of time I was made presentable, and that in the nick of time.

Accompanied by Reverend A. F. Gearhard, the diocesan director of the Society for the Propagation of the Faith, Bishop Walsh entered our home. Our welcome was a most hearty one, for the hour long desired had finally arrived. To speak to one who knew from personal experience the many trials and difficulties which befall those who do pioneer work in an untilled field was a source of inspiration. Before his departure he bestowed his bless-

157 ing and in doing so imparted it also to the Apostolate and all its members. We treasure the memory of that meeting, and also a life-like photo of the good Bishop bearing the inscription "Blessings upon the Apostolate" above his autograph.

CHAPTER XIII THE GOOD SAMARITAN GUILD 

"Be not slow to visit the sick: for by these things thou shalt be confirmed in love." - Ecclus. 7.39. 'OR some time it had been my ardent desire to strengthen the bond which unites the Apostolate to its members. By doing in person what “Our Good Samaritan" was accomplishing by means of the printed word it would be possible to attain a closer contact with the afflicted. By personal visits to the sick, friendly cheer, words of spiritual comfort, and sympathetic encouragement could be given to them. When this plan was mentioned to my faithful helpers they gave it their unanimous approval and promised to give the proposed project their wholehearted support and co-operation. 

The Origin of the Guild 

The first meeting relative to organizing the new association was held on the 24th of August, 1930, just four years after the founding of the Apostolate. Present at that meeting were the Very Rev. Dr. A. J. Muench, Spiritual Director of the Apostolate, the Rev. Francis E. 

Weis, of Pio Nono High School, St. Francis, Wisconsin, and the following ladies, who helped with the work of the Apostolate: 158

Mrs. Gertrude Beyer 

Miss Alice Cary 

Miss Mary M. Grode 

Miss Margaret Inkmann 

Miss Virginia Janda

Miss Clara Kilbert 

Miss Cennie Kumhera 

Miss Martha Rebholz 

Miss Gertrude Simmert 

Mrs. Catherine Tiry 

Miss Clara M. Tiry 

Miss Anna Weisel

The outlined plans received the approval of all present, and the following officers were unanimously elected: 

Miss Clara M. Tiry, President 

Miss Martha M. Rebholz, Vice President 

Miss Mary M. Grode, Secretary 

Miss Cennie Kumhera, Treasurer

Dr. Muench was chosen as Spiritual Director, and 

Father Weis as Moderator.

In a round table discussion the following matters were submitted and approved by all:

As a most suitable name for the newly founded society that of "The Good Samaritan Guild" was selected. For our patron we selected the Sacred Heart of our Savior under the title of "The Divine Good Samaritan." The patron feast to be celebrated on the twelfth Sunday after

Pentecost when the Gospel of the Good Samaritan is read for the edification of the faithful. 

Membership in the Guild is fourfold. Active Members are the ladies who visit the sick. They pay no dues but drop their little offering into a box at the monthly meetings.

Sustaining Members are all persons who in sympathy with the work make an annual offering of $1.00 or more a year, according to their means.

Contributing Members are those who give less than one dollar a year, or who contribute flowers, Catholic books, or subscriptions to Catholic magazines. 

Honorary Membership is restricted to the reverend clergy. Their contribution is of a spiritual nature; it consists of the recitation of the Divine Office once a month or the offering of the Holy Sacrifice of Mass once a year for the members of the Guild.

Any person, regardless of their place of residence can join the Sustaining or Contributing group of members. 

Priests everywhere can become Honorary Members; they can also join the other groups. 

A certificate of membership is given to every member. 

Three months later, at the fourth meeting of the Guild, on Sunday, November 23, 1930, the drafts of the Constitution and By-laws were taken up for consideration. Each article was read and then discussed. A few changes were deemed advisable, approved, and inserted. The draft was then acceptable to all and was adopted by an unanimous vote. The Constitution was then signed by the following 

Officers and the Charter Members: 

Very Rev. Aloysius J. Muench, Spiritual Director 

Rev. Francis E. Weis, Moderator 

Miss Clara M. Tiry, President 

Miss Martha M. Rebholz, Vice President 

Miss Mary M. Grode, Recording Secretary 

Miss Margaret Inkmann, Corresponding Secretary 

Miss Cennie Kumhera, Treasurer 

Miss Virginia Janda

Miss Clara Kilbert 

Miss Clara Kretlow 

Miss Gertrude Simmert 

Mrs. Gertrude Beyer 

Mrs. Isabel Gengler 

Mrs. Catherine Tiry

The signatures were witnessed by Rev. Stephen 

Klopfer, of St. John's Institute for Deaf-Mutes, St. Francis, Wisconsin, and Rev. Frank M. Schneider, assistant at 

St. Michael's parish, Milwaukee, Wisconsin.

Meetings are held in the home of the Apostolate on the fourth Monday of each month with the exception of 

February, May, August, and November. In those months they are held on the fourth Sunday, because on that same afternoon about eight of the members come to insert "Our Good Samaritan" and the leaflets into the addressed envelopes, thus preparing them for the mail. 

Occasionally we are honored by having a priest as our guest at these meetings. In that way, given an opportunity to see our home and the scene of our labors, they become acquainted with the work performed by the Apostolate and the Guild. In most all instances these meetings are a revelation to them and their interest in our work is increased. We in turn profit by the informal talks they give us, and their encouraging words are an incentive towards striving to be even more zealous in our labor of love for the sick. 2 

Phases of the Work 

The main purpose of the Guild as set forth in the Constitution and By-laws is the sanctification of its members, by promoting the work of the Apostolate of Suffering, through visits to the invalid and shut-in members of the Apostolate. This phase of the work is performed by the active members. Aspirants to active membership in the Guild must be known personally to some active member. They may attend the meeting in

order to become acquainted with the work of the Guild. 

They visit the sick for a period of six months, thereby developing a love for works of charity and especially for the sick. If at the expiration of six months they wish to continue the work, they are admitted to active membership.

Visits are made to members residing in sanatoriums, hospitals, and homes for the aged; also to private homes. 

Whenever an opportunity presents itself, calls are made upon the sick in other cities by those members of the Guild who pass through or stop there on matters of business or pleasure.

Each active member offers a Holy Communion and a rosary of five decades once a month for all the members of the Apostolate. In this way the sick who live outside of 

Milwaukee derive at least some spiritual benefit from the Guild.

The Greeting Card Section 

The purpose of this section is to send greeting cards to the sick, whom we visit, for Easter, Christmas, and also for their Birthday. At the time of the death of a patient, a card of sympathy is sent to the family. Miss 

Margaret Inkmann consented to take charge of this phase of the work at the time the Guild was organized, and has been most faithful in carrying out the duties connected with this office. 

The following report covers the activities of six years:

The Magazine Section 

The dissemination of literature is considered a most powerful means of propaganda in these days. Our separated brethren are most zealous in distributing literature promoting or explaining their religion or the organization to which they belong. We find such literature in public buildings, terminals, and conveyances. In our visitation to the sick in public and private sanatoriums and hospitals we find that they spread their literature even in these institutions. For want of something to read, the patients pick up what is closest to them. 

To counteract the evil influence of such literature which at times attacks the Faith and the Church, we undertook the task of distributing Catholic reading matter, such as leaflets, pamphlets, and magazines, among the sick whom we visit. Miss Gertrude Simmert takes care of this phase of the Guild's work.

Beside literature, articles of a religious nature are also distributed. When making the rounds among the sick, especially among those confined in public institutions, it is no extraordinary occurrence to find a patient without a rosary, medal, crucifix, or prayerbook. These little articles are then supplied gratis in such cases; our little fund is used for that purpose. It is most consoling to find the same crucifix tied with a little colored ribbon to the bedstead of the patient, the rosary lying on the table at their side, or the medal and Sacred Heart Badge pinned to their garment, when we visit them again. If those articles could speak, what stories they could relate! 

A fair idea of the good that is accomplished by the Guild in this line can be gained from the figures here submitted. They cover a period of six years.

Visiting the Sick 

The most important phase of the Guild's work is that of visiting the sick. Each member makes at least two calls a month, visiting the persons assigned to them. The sick await the arrival of a Visitor with great eagerness and longing; they know that with the Visitor rays of cheer and sunshine will come into their dreary and monotonous lives. Too often the afflicted are neglected and forsaken; this is especially the case with those who are chronic invalids and those who are confined to a public institution. 

On their visits to the sick, the Guild members perform little services for them, such as reading to the blind, writing letters, praying with them, going on an errand, placing a phone call or rendering such other aid as may be needed at that moment. Sometimes, too, they join with them in recreation of a lighter nature, such as playing their favorite game. To others they teach the art of making altar linens for poor missions thereby affording them an outlet for their zeal and an opportunity for employing both their talents and the many hours of their lonely lives in a worthwhile project.

All work and services in connection with the Guild are rendered gratuitiously by the members. Their one hope

and prayer is that through their ministrations the souls of the sick might be brought closer to God and to a more intimate knowledge of His love for them. This is the only recompense they seek here upon earth. In heaven the 

Divine Good Samaritan will Himself be their reward exceeding great.

Total number of persons visited Besides the above mentioned visits, 157 calls were also made to 390 patients residing in the following cities: 

Elm Grove, Hartford, West Bend, Waukesha, Clyman, Watertown, Rubicon, Muscoda, Beaver Dam, Grafton, Sheboygan, Campbellsport, Statesan, Jefferson, St. Francis, South Milwaukee, Cudahy, Racine and Kenosha.

Visits in other States were made to patients in Chicago, Illinois, and Indianapolis, Indiana. 3 Progress The original group comprised twelve members; in these six years the number has tripled. Two of the charter members resigned because of other duties demanding their attention and time. Our first Secretary, Miss Mary

Grode, returned to her home in South Dakota. New faces have taken the place of the old ones. Thus, time brings many changes.

In June, 1933, our first moderator, Father Weis, was appointed to the pastorate of St. John's Church, Rubicon, Wisconsin. We were face to face with a new problem. 

Who will take his place? The good Lord Who always provided for our needs, did not fail us in this instance. 

The choice of Dr. Muench fell upon the Reverend William V. Groessel, also a professor at Pio Nono High 

School. On July 13th, Dr. Muench introduced me to 

Fr. Groessel. Upon giving him a little more information regarding the Guild and the new duties that would be his, Fr. Groessel accepted the position as second moderator of the Guild. Future years showed that our selection was justified, for under the guidance of Fr. Groessel new zeal and a better understanding of our work and of the beauties and truths of our holy religion have come to the members. 

On July 24th, Fr. Groessel accompanied by Dr. Muench attended the meeting for the first time and was introduced to the members. His pleasing personality and gracious manners put the members at ease, lessened the tension so pronounced at such a time, and made us feel right at home with him.

Officers are elected for the term of one year, and may be re-elected for another year. This gives all the members an opportunity to become acquainted with the various phases of the work, so that they can more readily and with greater ease take over an office when a vacancy occurs. In the course of the six years the following have held offices in the Guild: Miss Mary Grode, Miss Martha

M. Rebholz, Miss Margaret Inkmann, Miss Cennie 

Kumhera, Miss Clara M. Tiry, Miss Marie A. Wagner,

Miss Josephine Fox, Mrs. Elsa M. Dietz, Miss Alyce E.

Rozak, Miss Dorothy Trudell, Miss Rosella Karius, and

Miss Clara Kilbert. 

The members of the Guild have also labored zealously in behalf of the various activities of the Apostolate. 

Through their efforts several bookcases have been purchased for "The Little Flower Library." 

On Palm Sunday in 1936 they sponsored a broadcast of the Holy Mass from St. Boniface Church, Milwaukee, Wisconsin, for the benefit of the invalids and shut-ins. 

At the celebration of the tenth anniversary of the Apostolate when the Eucharistic Day for the Sick was held, the members of the Guild assisted in divers ways. They contributed financially, made arrangements for taking the sick to church, took charge of individual patients during the services, and waited on them when dinner was served.

It is but natural that a work of this nature calls for funds to carry it on. It is really surprising how much can be accomplished with little means if the blessing of God rests upon a work. On several occasions when the treasury was almost depleted, the good Lord sent us a benefactor whose charity enabled us to continue our work of mercy among the sick.

The first active member of the Guild to be called home was Miss Margaret Comfort of West Allis, Wisconsin.

For about one year previous to her death she had resigned from active work, due to the condition of her health. She died on August 7, 1934, and was buried on the 10th from Holy Assumption Church, West Allis.

Though we mourned her loss we felt that we ought to rejoice in having an intecessor in heaven. 

When in August, 1934, Miss Nellie Marks, left our group to join the Sisters of St. Dominic in Racine, Wisconsin, we felt signally honored. Ought not the prayers of a spouse of Christ who had worked and labored with us for several years obtain manifold graces for us from the Master? At her reception into the order she was given

the name of Sr. Gemma, after Blessed Gemma of Galgani, known as the white passion flower of Lucca, to whom she was greatly devoted.

The number of contributing and sustaining members remain about the same; that of honorary members has increased very rapidly during the past three years. We are confident that their united prayers wafted heavenward are the channels through which the blessings of God have come upon the work of the Guild. 

We visualize a fruitful future for the Good Samaritan 

Guild, and we cherish the fond hope that in future years branches of the Guild will be organized in other cities and States so that the sick outside of Milwaukee may be the recipients of the Christ-like charity dispensed by the members of the Good Samaritan Guild.

CHAPTER XIV 

TH 

OUR SPIRITUAL DIRECTOR 

"In all things, Christ." 

Col. 3. 11.

HIS history of the Apostolate would not be complete without a sketch of the life of our revered 

Spiritual Director, the Most Reverend Aloisius J. Muench,

D.D., who was chosen by God to be instrumental in founding this noble work. With indefatigable zeal he guided its steps with wisdom and gentleness combined with a great love for the sick.

The city of Milwaukee, Wisconsin, the birthplace of the Apostolate, is also the native city of Bishop Muench. 

He was born on February 18, 1889, the eldest of the eight children of Mr. and Mrs. Joseph Muench.

He was baptized in the church of St. John de Nepomuc. 

He received his elementary education at St. Boniface's parochial school under the tutelage of the School Sisters of Notre Dame, and graduated from the same school in 

June, 1903.

For the period of one year he took lessons in Latin from the saintly Father Henry Niehaus, chaplain at the Motherhouse of the Notre Dame Sisters. Aloisius was small in stature, and therefore it was one of his ambitions to grow in height so as to reach the mark which the saintly teacher had made on the casing of the door. 170

Imagine his boyish joy and pride when he over-reached that mark!

First Years in the Priesthood

In September, 1904, Aloisius entered the Seminary of St. Francis de Sales to begin his studies for the priesthood. 

Nine years were spent within those sacred walls, and on June 8, 1913, he was ordained a priest of God in the chapel of the Seminary by the late Archbishop S. G. 

Messmer, D.D., of Milwaukee. His first Solemn High

Mass was offered on June 15, in the church of his boyhood years, that of St. Boniface.

Original plans of sending Father Muench abroad to continue his studies were postponed because of the shortage of priests in the diocese. 

He was appointed assistant to Fr. Bernard at St. 

Michael's Church, Milwaukee, in August of 1913. Events were shaping themselves for the future. Here for almost four years he performed all the functions of the holy priesthood. Father Muench found within this large parish many outlets for the fervor and zeal which filled his soul. To the children he was a gentle father, to the young men and women a wise counselor, to his penitents a prudent yet firm confessor, and to the sick a sympathetic comforter. Like the great apostle St. Paul, he tried to be "all things to all men." 

His next appointment came in June, 1917, and took him to Madison, Wisconsin. For the period of two years he was Chaplain at St. Mary's Hospital and assistant to 

Rev. Dr. H. C. Hengell at St. Paul's University Chapel in the same city. His position as chaplain of a large hos-

pital brought him into daily contact with the sick. This afforded him an opportunity to study their spiritual needs, and to become acquainted with the many trials and difficulties that beset the life of those afflicted with sufferings of a physical nature. This experience proved to be very valuable in later years when the direction of a large union of sufferers was committed to his care. 

Having obtained his M.A. degree in economics at the 

University of Wisconsin in 1919, he was sent abroad for a post-graduate course in the social sciences at the 

Catholic University of Fribourg, Switzerland. There he received the doctorate in the department of laws in 1921. 

An additional year was spent abroad in attending lectures at the Sorbonne, Louvain, London, and Oxford. He assisted in the post-war reorganization of the International Association for Labor Legislation at Basle, and attended international conferences on social and economic questions in Switzerland, Germany, and France. 2 

Professor and Rector

Upon returning to Milwaukee in August, 1922, Dr. 

Muench was appointed professor of dogmatic theology and social sciences at the Seminary of St. Francis de 

Sales. Later on he became dean of the theological department. To the talented student, Dr. Muench was a source of inspiration, and to those less gifted his understanding heart was a fount of encouragement. How well we know from personal experience the inexhaustible patience of his understanding heart toward a student's inability to grasp the first explanation of a difficult subject.

As a teacher he possessed the gift of expounding the most profound truths in a simple, clear manner. Himself a tireless worker, he aroused in his students the spirit of work, earnest application, and industry. He maintained perfect discipline at all times. A combination of such qualities in one person was unusual and merited recognition by higher authorities. After seven years of teaching new honors but also added responsibilities were conferred upon him.

The Seminary is that institution wherein the seed of priestly sanctity is sown. Its spiritual father plays an important and at the same time delicate part in nurturing the priestly spirit. The responsibility of a rector is a weighty one, not only before God and the Church, but before the very candidates themselves. The future life of the priest depends in a great measure upon the training he has received in the seminary. The word seminary is taken from the Latin "seminare," meaning "to sow." It is the work of the rector not only to sow the seed and to see that it is sown by the professors, but to nurture that seed by his labors, prayers, and sacrifices. 

The resignation of Monsignor A. G. Breig in August, 1929, from rectorship of the Seminary, a position he had held for over nine years, left a vacancy difficult to be filled. On Friday, August 16, 1929, the seminary board of trustees at a special meeting, chose Dr. Muench as the next rector of the most renowned of seminaries in the Northwest. He was at that time one of the youngest priests in the United States to occupy so exalted a position. 

He proved to be an exellent executive, a qualified leader, and a wise administrator. Every phase of seminary activity received his attention; he was in every way a master of detail.

To the professors who at one time worked with him and now were under his leadership he was kind, grateful, and appreciative. He took a constant and personal concern in their interests and difficulties. Truly, his heart was in his work; he loved the seminary, the cradle of priests. 

Dr. Muench did not restrict his labors to the seminary, rather, his heart sought an outlet for the application of the principles he advocated. He participated actively in the work of the Catholic Central Verein of America by way of writing and lecturing; as a member of the advisory board of the St. Vincent de Paul Society he imparted counsel to its members; his position as spiritual director of the Missionary Association of Catholic Women for several years afforded an opportunity to display his zeal for the missions. He was also spiritual Director of the Holy 

Childhood Society, and treasurer of the St. Michael's

Priests Fund. In addition to this he contributed articles to America and other publications. Where he found the time to devote to these different activities has and will always be a mystery. Like St. Alphonsus de Liguori he never wasted a minute. Perhaps that accounts for the great amount of work he accomplished. 

In recounting all his duties and activities, we have not written one word about his unflinching devotion to the cause of the sick and the suffering. As I recall those first days, weeks, months, and years of the Apostolate's existence, and the innumerable hours he devoted to the establishment and development of this association, a feeling of admiration and awe steals over me. Many free hours spent by others in seeking recreation were given to this work. It was a long way from St. Francis to our home in the opposite part of the city of Milwaukee, requiring a

great deal of time just for traveling to and fro. Amid summer's heat and winter's blizzards he came, with always a cheery smile and encouraging word, giving me the impression that the Apostolate was the most important work in his priestly life. 

Dr. Muench was signally blessed with good health so necessary for the performance of all his priestly duties. 

Yet, he, too, entertained the angel of suffering on various occasions. In the early part of 1930 he was forced to spend several weeks away from his usual labors in order to regain the strength he had expended when Rev. 

Thomas Schmitz, one of his best friends and a splendid professor, was taken sick and after many weeks of suffering passed to his eternal reward. Anxiety, suspense, and grief take their toll, but after a period of rest Dr. Muench returned to the Seminary with new vigor and strength. 

In January of 1934 Dr. Muench was forced to undergo an emergency operation. This disabled him for a number of weeks, but due to his strong constitution and the prayers of his many friends he was able soon to resume his duties.

These personal experiences in the school of suffering served to increase his love for the sick and confirmed his conviction that those who suffer have need of the encouragement and the spiritual helps proferred by the Apostolate. 3 

Domestic Prelate to His Holiness 

Christmas eve in 1934! What memories they recall of the cherished gift the Christ-Child had in store for us on His birthday! Shortly before suppertime a friend called

me on the telephone to tell me the wondrous news that our spiritual director, Dr. Muench, had been elevated to the rank of a domestic prelate by our Holy Father, Pope Pius XI. Unbounded joy and deep gratitude to God filled the hearts of all connected with our work upon hearing that this signal honor and fitting recognition were conferred on him. At the midnight Mass offered in St. John's 

Cathedral and broadcast over the radio, these glad tidings were announced to the faithful. The papers carried the details on Christmas day, and very soon the news was spread throughout the country.

The solemn services of investiture took place on Tuesday the 22nd of January, 1935, in the historic Cathedral of St. John the Evangelist, just six days before it was destroyed by fire. Together with the other priests, among them Father Kroha, my very first spiritual Father who sowed the good seed within my soul, and Father Bernard, the beloved pastor of St. Michael's parish, he knelt before our kind Archbishop, the Most Rev. S. A. Stritch,

D.D. After the recitation of the Apostles' Creed, and the taking of the Oath of Loyalty to the Holy See, he was clothed with the pure white linen rochet and the royal purple mantelletta.

Due to the condition of my health and the season of the year I was not privileged to witness the beautiful ceremonies. As so often before the way of sacrifice was mine. Descriptions of the same, however, were given to me by my mother and other friends who attended the unusual, impressive, and century-old ceremonies. The 

Catholic and secular press carried the detailed accounts of the services.

Among the many felicitations received by Monsignor

Muench upon his elevation to the honors of a Domestic Prelate of the Holy Father those of his beloved friends in the Apostolate were treasured most highly. 

Knowing full well that the sick and afflicted desired to give their spiritual director some tangible remembrance on this auspicious occasion as a token of their appreciation, they were invited to join in gathering spiritual flowers for him in the garden of prayer and sacrifice. The response to this invitation was most gratifying, and our endeavors were blessed with success. Spiritual Flowers to the amount of 586,278 were received from 424 members residing in 22 States, 6 provinces of Canada, and in 

Panama. The Spiritual Bouquet consisted of the follow ing devotional flowers:
