This Spiritual Bouquet was put up in booklet form of nine pages. On the front cover was painted a scene from the investiture ceremonies. The first page contained the dedication followed by the good wishes and congratulations of the members. On the remaining pages there were designs appropriate to the various "Flowers." There was a chalice, missal, stole, ciborium, monstrance, grapes, and

wheat, all symbolizing the Eucharistic devotions. A rosary formed of white, yellow, and red roses, a cross entwined with a thorny crown, and passion flowers, spoke of devotion to the Mother of God, hours of suffering, and acts of self-denial.

This token of appreciation on the part of the members of the Apostolate brought untold joy, consolation, and happiness to our spiritual director. The many personal messages and good wishes gladdened his heart for it told him that his labors for the Apostolate and in behalf of the sick had fallen on rich soil and were bringing forth good fruit.

As an expression of his appreciation a souvenir card of his monsignorial investiture was sent to each member of the Apostolate with the June issue of "Our Good

Samaritan." This card had his photo and the date of investiture on it, and on the reverse a beautiful prayer composed especially to suit his position as spiritual director of this large family of sufferers. 

In the same issue there also appeared his open letter of appreciation to the members, with the promise of a Holy Mass for them on the Feast of Corpus Christi, June 20th. From this letter we reprint the following beautiful sentiments of good wishes: "May Our Eucharistic Lord keep you in His love, shower you with blessings, and give you strength on the way of suffering which you are privileged to walk with Him. In His company you will take with a smile whatever pain and suffering may come to you in life. United to him in suffering, you will be happy with Him in the triumph of the Resurrection. May 

His peace be with you now and forever. Pray for me as I do for you."

This letter and the souvenir card were received with profound thanks by the members, and even after his consecration as a bishop we still received requests for that card with the words "that prayer is OUR prayer for Bishop Muench." It was always a pleasure to send out copies of that particular card, for we knew that each card would bring numerous prayers to the throne of God for the one who guided the bark of the Apostolate safely and surely through troublesome waters to the port of its Tenth Anniversary.

In view of future events, a certain incident closely connected with the investiture is worthy of recording. Amid the glorious festivities, the angel of suffering hovered closely about Monsignor Muench. It seems strange that out of the twelve priests invested with the purple of the monsignorate, he alone, the spiritual father of the sick and the suffering, should be called upon to bear the cross of pain. Just six days before the solemn services Monsignor Muench had the misfortune to break his left arm at the elbow, On the day of the Investiture, the arm was still in splints, and caused extreme agony during the entire service. Again it was evident that the cross and the crown are always linked together, and that Calvary is never far removed from Tabor. The words he uttered to me many years ago echoed in my ears, "We must pay for everything we get." Somehow, this accident occurring at this particular time when all should have been of a joyful and festive nature seemed to have a special significance. I found myself wondering what further honors were being purchased by this price of pain. Seven months later the answer to my question and the verification of my premonition came in a mandate from Rome.

4 

A Successor to the Apostles

It was the feast of St. Clare of Assisi, August 12, 1935. 

A beautiful but extremely hot morning found me kneeling in dear old St. Joseph's Church, attending the funeral of an Apostolate member. There was nothing to indicate that this feast of my beloved patroness would be different from those of past years; no little bird whispered to me the news that was being flashed over the wires, announced over the radio, and prepared for the press. 

Upon returning home my first attention was given to the daily mail. Because of the feast there were more letters than on other days. Mother seemed a bit nervous, and finally said to me: "Monsignor Muench telephoned this morning. He will call you later." This seemed rather strange because a letter of felicitations from him had arrived in the mail. "Did something important happen, is anything wrong, is it good or evil news?" Mother tried to evade my questions thereby arousing my suspicion. 

Without waiting for an answer, and giving it no further thought there came from my heart the words: “Has he been made a bishop? How far is he going?" But mother would not commit herself. Expecting something unusual, I had no more patience to wait for his next call. Within a few seconds I called him and then heard the most wonderful message, "I have been appointed Bishop of 

Fargo," followed by the assuring words, “I shall continue as spiritual director of the Apostolate, and perhaps be able to do more for it now than ever before." The feelings of those moments defy description. Carried to the heights of joyful exultation at one moment, and then

sinking to the very depths of grief at the realization of what this promotion to the episcopacy and his eventual departure might mean to the Apostolate. Over it all, however, the spirit of profound gratitude prevailed. 

The afternoon found me before the tabernacle pouring forth my thanks to Jesus Eucharistic. A planned visit to the patients at Muirdale Sanatorium was carried out, but in a rather bewildered and absent-minded manner. 

There was just one thought uppermost in my mindonly one subject on which I could converse, Father 

Muench a Bishop. How unsearchable are the ways of God!

Consecration 

The feast of the great St. Theresa of Avila, October 

15, was the day selected by Bishop-elect Muench for his consecration. It was also the name day of his beloved mother and sister. Fair skies, bright dazzling sunshine enhanced the splendor of that day which the Lord indeed hath made. Long before the hour appointed for the sacred rites, the spacious Church of the Gesu, Milwaukee, was filled to overflowing with priests, religious, and lay persons who were privileged to witness one of the most solemn ceremonies of Holy Mother Church, surpassed in grandeur only by the coronation of a Pope or the canonization of a saint. Amid the solemn but joyful ringing of the bells and the swelling notes of the organ, the procession wended its way slowly down the center aisle to the sanctuary carpeted with green and before the gothic altar banked with ferns and chrysanthemums of russet and white.

His Excellency, the Most Reverend Amleto Cicognani, Apostolic Delegate to the United States, was the Con-

secrator. In the performance of these sacred rites of consecration he was assisted by two co-consecrators, the Most 

Reverend Christian H. Winkelmann, auxiliary bishop of St. Louis, Missouri, and the Most Rev. William R.

Griffin, auxiliary bishop of La Crosse, Wisconsin. Other priests taking part in the solemn services were professors from the Seminary, intimate friends among the clergy, and some of the junior clergy who had studied under his administration. 

Four Archbishops, eighteen bishops, four Abbots, many monsignori, and hundreds of priests from near and far attended the impressive ceremonies. It was but the second time that the consecration of a bishop took place in the history of the Archdiocese of Milwaukee, the first one having been consecrated some thirty years before. The multitude followed every ceremony of the ancient rites most attentively and with the aid of specially prepared books were able to understand them to a great extent. 

In that way the exalted dignity of a bishop, the greatness of the power conferred upon him, and the vast responsibilities placed upon him were brought home most forcibly to the people. 

Our own beloved Archbishop, the Most Reverend

Samuel A. Stritch, of Milwaukee, delivered the sermon. 

When the ceremonies were ended, the Mass offered, and the final blessing given, the procession filed from the church. The hearts of all were jubilant because another loyal son of Holy Mother Church had been invested with the power and dignity of the fullness of the Sacred Priesthood of Christ. I, too, by a special grace of God, was privileged to attend. 

The December issue of "Our Good Samaritan" was

dedicated to the newly consecrated bishop. It contained a description of the Consecration ceremonies, an explanation of his Coat of Arms, an account of the Holy Mass he offered in the home of the Apostolate before his departure, and his letter of appreciation to the members for the felicitations sent to him at the time of his consecration.

Due to the fact that only a few months before, on the occasion of his monsignorial investiture, the members had gathered a spiritual bouquet for him, we refrained from making a similar request at this time. Instead, we asked the members to offer their sufferings during the months of September, October, and November for the newly consecrated bishop and our own beloved Spiritual Director.

The First Pontifical Masses 

Sunday, October 20, was a day of triumph and jubilation for the parish of St. Boniface. On that day Bishop 

Muench returned to offer his First Solemn Pontifical Mass in the church of the same parish where twenty-two years ago he had ascended the altar to offer for the first time the Holy Sacrifice of the Mass. 

On the following Sunday, October 27, he honored St.

Michael's parish in the same manner, it being the only parish in which he had been assistant. 

On Thursday, October 24th, the feast of St. Raphael, Archangel, our beloved Spiritual Director deigned to honor the home of the Apostolate by coming and offering the Holy Sacrifice in our midst. A description of this event is given in another chapter of this history. 

There was great rejoicing at the Seminary of St. Francis de Sales on Monday, October 28, the feast of St. Jude

Thaddeus. The beloved rector was celebrant at a Pontifical Mass at the altar where he had knelt during the many years of preparation for the priesthood, where he had been ordained, and where he had so often offered the Spotless Victim during the years when he occupied the professors chair and filled the office of Rector. On that day he also exercised for the first time one of the powers bestowed upon him at his consecration, namely that of administering the Sacrament of Confirmation to eight young students of the classical course. 

The following days passed all too rapidly, and it certainly seemed that "time had wings." Then came the moments of bidding farewell to a beloved father and mother, sisters and brothers, relations, cherished friends, a revered Alma Mater, and a native city. 

On Thursday, October 31, Bishop Muench visited the home of the Apostolate once more before his departure. 

We reviewed the work of the past nine years, dwelled on that of the present, and anticipated plans for the future. 

The joy of having him with us for supper in our little family circle was somewhat shadowed by the sorrow which filled our hearts over his coming departure, but nevertheless the memory of that evening will always be treasured as a most happy and solemn event. 

On that evening he blessed our office, and then together we offered prayers for the sick, the dying, and the deceased members of the Apostolate before the relic of the True Cross of Christ. We concluded the little service with saying the Te Deum in thanksgiving for all the blessings God vouchsafed the Apostolate, its founders, and its members during the nine years of its existence. We knelt for the final blessing, kissed his ring, and then with a

smile we bade him farewell and wished him God-speed in his new field of labor. In the darkness, mother, dad, and I watched him as he drove away in his car. The emotions that swept over our hearts defies description. 

Silently we gazed at each other, each one wondering what the future would have in store for him- our best friend. 

On Monday, November 4th, the feast of St. Charles

Borromeo, the saint whose name he took in confirmation, Bishop Muench left for Fargo, North Dakota, to carry on

God's work and to guide the western flock entrusted to his care.

What tongue can adequately describe the ineffable happiness surging through the newly consecrated bishop's soul when in retrospection he traced the hand of God leading him through the various paths of life, and when his thoughts dwelt in anticipation on the vast amount of work there was to be done in the missionary diocese of Fargo. There was comfort in the thought that besides his parents, brothers and sisters, relations, and a host of friends, the prayers and sufferings of a veritable army of patient cross-bearers would follow and uphold him in the arduous labors and problems confronting him in the episcopacy.

By the "laying on of hands,"

In the fullness of the Priesthood Consecrated now he stands.

Installation as Bishop of Fargo 

During the time arrangements were made for the consecration of Bishop Muench in Milwaukee and plans for his departure were in formation, the faithful of the city and diocese of Fargo, North Dakota, cast aside their garb of mourning over the demise of their deceased bishop, the Most Rev. James O'Reilly, D.D., and prepared for the reception and the installation of their new bishop. 

Throngs crowded the railroad station and streets in the city when the train bearing the new bishop arrived on the afternoon of November 5, 1935, eager for a glimpse of their new shepherd and spiritual leader. Several drum and bugle corps, cars conveying the clergy and laymen, and numerous parishioners escorted the bishop to his residence. 

The ceremony of installation took place on Wednesday, November 6, in St. Mary's Cathedral, amid the impressive and colorful liturgy of the Church. The installation ceremony was conducted by the Most Reverend John Gregory 

Murray, D.D., Archbishop of St. Paul. The solemn Pontifical Mass was offered by the Most Reverend John L. 

Lawler, D.D., of Rapid City, South Dakota. The Rev.

John Garland of Hillsboro acted as presbyter assistant, and the Rev. Francis Slominski of Minot, and the Rev. 

William McNamee of Grand Forks were deacon and subdeacon at the Mass. The sermon was preached by Archbishop Murray in whose archdiocese the See of the new bishop is located.

Our own beloved Archbishop Stritch and about fifty priests together with a number of lay people had accompanied Bishop Muench from Milwaukee to Fargo.

There they witnessed his installation and were guests at the various receptions held for him. On their return to 

Milwaukee they gave glowing accounts of the royal and cordial welcome extended to the new Bishop. 

Civic receptions and banquets were tendered Bishop Muench for many weeks. Wherever he went both clergy and people strove to give him the best of welcomes. 

From Bishop Muench's sermon to his faithful flock gathered to witness his solemn installation, we cull the following brief paragraphs so expressive of his zeal for souls: "When a bishop comes to a diocese as its shepherd there is naturally great joy in Christian hearts. He comes to do the work of Him Who came upon earth nineteen hundred years ago to bring the means of salvation to a sin-stricken world. The bishop is a living witness to 

Christ's enduring work. By his very office he proclaims to men the fact that Christ's work is still a reality among men. Century has succeeded century, but the power of 

Christ continues to do its beneficent work. It is in the bishop that this power is embodied in all its fulness. He is among men the representative of the fulness of the priesthood of Christ.

"Our work in your midst is an important work because it is centered in interests than which there are none greater on this earth. A sacred challenge lies before us to serve the Church 'without spot or wrinkle', to support her in her great and holy task of bringing true culture to men, of encouraging them to sanctify their lives, and of promoting righteousness in the world that exalteth nations. "With the cross in hand we shall accomplish what the poor missionary once accomplished under circumstances more trying than those under which we now live. We shall succeed where all the powers of the world must fail. For Christ we live, for Christ we die-in all things, Christ."

The First Official Visit 

While the people of Fargo strove most zealously to give due honors to their new Bishop, the sick, less fortunate than their healthy fellowmen, had to content themselves with the descriptions given by those who attended the installation and what was printed in the papers. It was characteristic of Bishop Muench to be mindful of these shut-ins and invalids who were eager to have a glimpse of him. Amid all the festivities arranged in his honor he took time and made his very first official visits to the sick. 

How delighted, happy, and privileged they must have felt to be honored in such a special manner! Even though as bishop, he, in imitation of St. Paul, has all his flock "in his heart," there is a special corner reserved for those who have been called upon to follow the Crucified Master and carry their own cross to Calvary. Full well he knows that through their prayers and sufferings many graces

and strength will come to him who carries the burdens of a diocese. May they always be faithful and worthy of his confidence and the trust he places in them!

His Coat of Arms 

Every bishop has an escutcheon or coat of arms. This he selects upon his elevation to the episcopacy. He also adopts a motto that will serve as a watchword in guiding his flock. The motto appears either on, above, or below his coat of arms.

The coat of arms of Bishop Muench consists of a shield. divided into two parts. In the "dexter" or right half of the shield itself appear the arms of the diocese of Fargo, the 

See of his jurisdiction, namely, a golden cross, symbolizing the Catholic Faith, on a field of blue, the traditional color of the mantle of Our Lady, who is the patroness of the Cathedral Church of the diocese; a golden or yellow sheaf of wheat in the upper right hand denotes the prime industry of North Dakota; the horseshoe in the center alludes to the name of Fargo, a Welsh root meaning "smith." The horseshoe also refers to the fact that the "Pony Express" was founded by W. G. Fargo, after whom the city is named.

In the "sinister" or left half are seen the personal insignia of the Bishop, namely, the figure of an unhooded monk, on a background of silver, used because the family name of the Bishop is the German word for “monk”; the right hand is raised in blessing, the left hand holds a crozier. The four alternating bars of gold and black are an abbreviation of the family coat of arms of the Bishop's patron, St. Aloisius of Gonzaga.

Above the shield are seen the Bishop's cross, mitre, and crozier; above these is the Bishop's hat, and on both sides are three sets of tassels denoting the rank of the bishop. 

Below the shield is the motto selected by Bishop Muench, namely, "In Omnibus Christus." These words literally translated mean, "In all things, Christ," and are taken from St. Paul's letter to the Colossians wherein he writes "But Christ is all, and in all." (Col. 3. 11.) Looking at a treasured First Mass souvenir card, we find that the text on it chosen by Father Muench as a newly ordained priest twenty-two years ago, is the same that adorns his coat of arms as a Bishop. May these words of St. Paul, the great Apostle of the Gentiles, and so full of meaning to Bishop Muench, ever be to him an inspiration leading him on to accomplish great things, in, through, and for, Christ and His Church.

A souvenir card of the Consecration depicting the coat of arms, bearing the motto "In Omnibus Christus” and a prayer for the newly consecrated bishop was sent to each member with the December Consecration issue of "Our Good Samaritan" in 1935. In this way, our beloved invalids and shut-ins, as well as all other persons interested in our work, received not only a tangible remembrance of the greatest feast in the history of the Apostolate but also a reminder to pour forth their fervent prayers and supplications for this new shepherd and his flock.

CHAPTER XV WITHIN HALLOWED WALLS 

"Thou, O Lord, hast chosen this place, for Thy name to be called upon therein, that it might be a house of prayer and supplication for Thy people." 

Mach. 7.37.

ITHIN our hearts there is the wish that all the W members of our large family of sufferers could come and pay us a visit. We should like to show you the place where the Apostolate was founded and where the blessed work of comforting the sick is still being carried on. We know that for the greatest number of our members such a visitation is physically impossible. 

But what is to hinder you from an imaginary visit to our home and office? Come with us on such a trip, and see if the idea or picture you have formed in your mind resembles in the least the reality. 

Some of you have no doubt pictured us as being established on the eleventh or sixteenth floor of some large office building in the congested part of the city; others may have had visions of a luxurious home where space is plentiful, and where servants obey every beck and call. 

Or, mayhap you even surmised that we were comfortably settled within the walls of a convent, close to a chapel where we could “drop in for a chat" with the Friend of friends. Could it be that your imagination led you into the suburb of the city where fresh air revives a wearied 191

body and the beauties of nature calm the mind and soul? 

Or, did you consider us in the light of many other organizations who have a separate room given over to the various phases of a work as is that of the Apostolate, with a crew of workers at our disposal? Be prepared for a huge, perhaps disillusioning, but we hope pleasant surprise.

Our Home 

The home that shelters the Apostolate belongs to my parents with whom I reside. It is situated in the midwestern part of the city on the north side, within reach of three carlines. The parish to which we belong is that of 

St. Michaels, about twelve blocks away.

Next to the building on the southwestern corner of North 34th and West Galena Streets is a duplex flat. As we approach it there is nothing extraordinary about the home of the Apostolate of Suffering to distinguish it from the other similar buildings in the neighborhood. Yet, as one draws nearer, there can be seen the little red vigil light which is always kept burning before the little shrine of the Sacred Heart. As we enter the front door our eyes rest on a picture of Christ in Gethsemane. This is my favorite picture and has been the silent witness of many a bitter struggle in hours of intense suffering. The next things to attract our attention are the bookcases contain ing the volumes of the "Little Flower Traveling Library" 

established for the members of the Apostolate. Upon one stands a statue of the Blessed Mother, on the other that of St. Joseph; next to the statues are natural or artificial

flowers according to the season of the year, and before them is a vigil light.

Passing from the reception hall we enter the living room, the only room in the house which has not been invaded and turned into a working room. This we call "St. Clara's Oratory." In one corner is erected our shrine of the Sacred Heart; on it are flowers, blessed wax candles, the relics of the True Cross, St. Lidwina, and St. Camillus. 

On one side of the wall hangs the plaque of St. Lidwina, our patroness. On another wall are large photos of our prelates, Archbishop Stritch of Milwaukee, and our Spiritual Director, Bishop Muench of Fargo. There too is the piano, played only occasionally when our little group of helpers are gathered here for the purpose of sending out "Our Good Samaritan." On such occasions we take time out for prayers and hymns.

This room is very sacred to us because of the many memories connected with it. If the walls could speak, what scenes they could describe. Here the thoughts of the Apostolate were conceived, here it was founded, here too, were held the many sessions when planning the details of our work. Hours of suffering and sadness, but also of joy and gladness were spent in this room. But most sacred is it to us when we think of the many times it has served as our chapel when the Holy Sacrifice was offered upon an improvised altar with the piano serving as a background. Truly, when we step into the room, we feel that we are treading on holy ground where the angels of God at times bowed low in adoration. In all other aspects it resembles the living room of any other home occupied by people of moderate means. The furniture and furnishings are very simple. One large chair used to serve as my

desk when we first started the Apostolate. Because of my weakness it was necessary to have a support for my back. A board similar to those used by dressmakers, or by invalids, and termed a lap board, was placed over the arms of the chair. From this portable desk practically all my correspondence was sent out during the first eight years. As I grew stronger more of my work was performed at the desk in my room. Now the board has been stored away, but not discarded entirely. Who knows but what it might serve again in future years?

Passing into the next room, we find ourselves in what should be the dining room, but serves more as a workroom. Usually the table is stretched out to three-quarter length, and covered with a white oil-cloth. Here most of the addressing of the envelopes for sending out "Our Good Samaritan" is done. Up against the large bay window is a flat top desk, acquired in the autumn of 1936, on our tenth anniversary. Like every other busy person's desk it is at most times covered with papers and correspondence. In this room is my personal bookcase and writing desk, but the "personal" part has disappeared from it. Since the establishment of the Library my personal books are stored away in drawers in order to make room for the reference books of the Apostolate and also for some of the Library books. On this bookcase are the statues of St. Francis and St. Clare of Assisi with their respective relics. Here, too, rests the little silver case containing all our other relics. 

From this room we pass into my own room; though supposed to be a bed room it has the appearance of a real office. In it are a roll top desk, a sectional bookcase, our steel letter-file, the typewriter, and a large open face stor-

age case containing some of the materials required in our work. Yes, there is place for the bed and the dresser too! 

At first, as one piece of office furniture after another was added, I felt sort of stifled when in the room, but gradually this feeling wore off. 

On the bookcase in this room stand the statue and the relic of the Little Flower. This was the first bookcase of the Library, and somehow we could not bring ourselves to take down her statue and place it on one of the other sections.

Above the desk is the treasured large, autographed photo of our Holy Father Pope Pius XI commemorating the granting of his blessing and the plenary indulgence at the hour of death for all the members of the Apostolate. 

This was received on our first anniversary. As our eyes rest upon his kind countenance and his hand uplifted in blessing, we feel in truth that he is smiling down upon us and blessing the work being done for the afflicted lambs of his flock.

From the diningroom a small hall leads to another room. This, since the death of my father on Easter Sunday, April 12, 1936, has been in part turned into a library. 

In this room are a bookcase, supply cabinet, chairs, and a table. Herein all the actual work in connection with the library is accomplished. In winter this serves as mother's bedroom, thus saving the cost of heating another room, and also reducing the labor for mother. 

A large yard and plenty space between the adjoining houses provide plenty of fresh air, light, and sunshine. 

From early spring until late in autumn, the garden is filled with flowers of every description, beginning with tulips pushing their way through the last snow of winter,

and ending with the gladioli and dahlia being nipped by Jack Frost. From this garden thousands of flowers are cut for decorating our little shrines, and are sent to brighten the rooms of the sick whom we visit. 

This description gives evidence of the difficulties and the handicaps under which we have labored during these ten years. The need for more space and larger surroundings enabling us to carry on the work with greater ease is imperative. We place our trust in Divine Providence and foster the hope that through the kindness of future benefactors we shall one day be able to occupy the upper rooms of this dwelling for the purpose of carrying on this blessed work so dear to the heart of Christ. 2 

Bethlehem, Tabor, Calvary

St. John the Evangelist, in the first chapter of his gospel tells us that "Our Lord came unto His Own."

We know that this statement referred to the birth of Christ in Bethlehem but it can also be applied to the coming of Christ upon our altars whenever the Holy 

Sacrifice is offered.

At the time the Apostolate was founded, I had not been to church on Christmas or Easter for a period of eighteen years. This was due to my chronic infirmity which keeps me confined to the house during the winter months. From the first cool days when the sharp winds twirl the leaves in the air, until the balmy breezes of spring bring forth the new leaves, I, like so many of the members of the Apostolate, am a shut-in. Because of this condition I never again expected to have the joy of

attending Holy Mass on Christmas or Easter; yet, when these feasts arrived in each recurring year the same desire and longing filled my heart—ah, just to be able once again to attend Holy Mass on those days of days! 

One day, several months after the Apostolate was founded, I took courage and asked Fr. Muench if it would be possible to get permission to have a Holy Mass offered here at home on Christmas. Special circumstances, however, made the Christmas Mass impossible. He consoled me with the promise that if possible he would obtain that privilege for me for Easter Sunday. 

Living in hope was part of my life's work, but never before had hopes of this nature risen within my heart. In the month of February, the Most Reverend Archbishop S. G. Messmer graciously gave the requested permission to Fr. Muench because of my condition and of the charitable work in which we were engaged. Heartthrobs? Yes, countless ones were mine during the weeks that followed. What happiness there was in the very anticipation of that Easter so soon to come! What joy in the expectation of attending Holy Mass and receiving the glorified body of Our Lord on the anniversary of the day when He came forth from the tomb as Victor over sin and hell, over suffering and death! What pleasure too, in knowing that I could share this privilege and happiness with my parents, my brothers and their families, and the few loyal friends who assisted me in the work of the Apostolate. 

Easter morning, April 17, 1927, dawned bright and fair; all nature seemed to rejoice with us, even the mercury must have felt the excitement that pervaded the air for it soared to almost eighty degrees! Never since that year has the weather on Easter been so perfect!

Our home is small, space is limited, and therefore no elaborate preparations could be made for the coming of Christ. The piano served as a background for the altar, the living room table was used for the altar table. Statues of Our Lord, the Blessed Virgin, and St. Joseph were placed in the background, roses and candles adorned the altar. At that time I was not very well acquainted with the rubrics, and so I placed four candles upon the altar, which is the number required for a Bishop's Mass. Perhaps I had a premonition that the celebrant of that first Mass in the home of the Apostolate would in later years be elevated to the rank of a bishop and would need that many candles when he returned to offer the Holy Sacrifice.

To describe the feelings of my heart on that day is impossible. They can be compared to those of a first communicant, of a confirmant, of a bride on her wedding day, of a novice who takes her first vows, or of a newlyordained priest when he ascends the altar to offer his first Holy Mass.

The months from Easter to Christmas were filled with hopes of a similar privilege for the anniversary of the 

Christ-Child's birth. Again Archbishop Messmer was most gracious, and the home of the Apostolate became another Bethlehem on Christmas morn. Indeed, this was 

Christmas for me in the true sense of the word, for what is Christmas without Christ's Mass?

Easter and Christmas have come and passed many times, and each one brought with it the same privilege. 

The memory, however, of the first Masses on those days lingers on, over and above the others. 

When God called the venerable Archbishop to himself

on August 4, 1930, a little fear crept into our hearts lest perhaps his successor might not grant this inestimable and unusual privilege. God, however, granted the many fervent prayers offered, for, when on the 5th of December of that same year our newly installed Archbishop, the Most Rev. S. A. Stritch, sat at my bedside and I humbly and with a little trepidation made my request, he most graciously granted the privilege, solemnly telling me that I "must be up for Christmas for that is no day to be spent in bed.”

And so, with the Archbishop's permission, the Holy 

Sacrifice has been offered here each Christmas and Easter. 

At Christmas the spirit of innocent joy and simplicity pervaded the atmosphere around the little altar; at Easter the more solemn and profound joy of victory prevailed. 

There were times when I attended by being propped up with pillows or prostrate on the davenport, but God always gave me strength to supervise the preparations. 

There was but one exception, namely, on Christmas of 1935 when the critical illness of my father and my own confinement to bed due to a nervous breakdown forbade any thought of such a celebration. This Mass was postponed to St. Patrick's day, March 17, 1936, when both of us had practically recovered from illness and regained some strength.

Not all the Holy Masses offered in the home of the 

Apostolate were surrounded with the joy of Christmas and the splendor of the Resurrection. There were times when the spirit of Calvary hovered over us. One in particular stands out in my memory. In January, 1936, Bishop 

Muench spent a few days in Milwaukee with his aged parents. It was the time of my father's and my own

critical illness. He made arrangements to offer a Holy 

Mass here again on the First Friday of the month, the 

3rd of January. My father was propped in an armchair,

I was lying on the davenport, and mother and the nurses watched over us. I tried to picture Bethlehem, but Calvary was more vividly present in my mind. Truly, it was a "Mass for the Sick.' 

Several times during the course of the years, newly ordained priests were granted the privilege of offering a Holy Mass here when I was unable to attend their First Holy Mass in Church. These days were festal days, bright stars that illumined the darkness of days and weeks filled with much suffering. It was God's way of compensating for the many sacrifices He demands from those who bear the cross of illness. Indeed, He is never outdone in generosity.

The most solemn of any of these Holy Masses was that offered by our Spiritual Director, Bishop Muench, shortly after his consecration. God in His goodness had permitted me to attend that consecration, but I paid the price by developing one of my attacks of asthma. That was the end of my going out for that year, and so it was impossible for me to attend even one of his Pontifical 

Masses before he left for Fargo.

Knowing full well what a sacrifice this was for me, he promised to offer a Holy Mass here in the home of the Apostolate, yes, in the very room where the Apostolate was founded. The day selected for this great feast was that of October 24th, the feast of St. Raphael, the 

Archangel, whose name signifies "medicine of God."

How appropriate was the day for the offering of the Holy Sacrifice in that home where was founded that

association which has been a veritable spiritual medicine of God for the healing and comforting of the souls of the sick and the weary children of God!

Gradually my illness left me, and I was able to make the arrangements for this most solemn occasion. Again the altar was erected and this time adorned with golden yellow and white chrysanthemums, the colors of the Church. Kneeling in close proximity to the altar were a group of twenty-six persons, including mother, father, myself, six other members of the family, and a few of the ladies who labored so self-sacrificingly in behalf of the Apostolate and the Guild.

This Holy Mass was offered in thanksgiving to Almighty God for all the joys and the sorrows, the blessings and the graces that have come to His Excellency, to our family, to the Apostolate, to all its living and deceased members, and to its benefactors. 

What shall I say of the solemn moment of Consecration when our newly consecrated Bishop lifted on high the 

Sacred Host and the Chalice of the Precious Blood? Or of the sacred moment when from the consecrated hands of a successor to the Apostles we received Our Lord in Holy Communion? There are no words to describe the emotions and sentiments that permeated the hearts of all who were present, and who later gathered around our good Bishop like little children around a loving Father. Many blessings have come unto the Apostolate, but this we felt was the greatest of all. 

In all these Holy Masses the members of the Apostolate had a special share; they are included in the priest's "Memento" and his blessing goes forth to them. After 

Mass special prayers are recited for them. In this way

we share with them the graces derived from the privileges granted to us by our devoted Archbishop whose heart beats with love and sympathy for God's afflicted children. 3 

The Spiritual Powerhouse 

In our correspondence with the members of the Apostolate we find that the sick regret the fact that their infirmity hinders them in promoting our work or enlisting new members. This is a point which we can well understand. Confined to the house, having contact only with the people who come to visit them, their field of labor is very limited. Here, however, as in many other instances we must recall the words of St. Paul, “I planteth, Apollo watereth, but God giveth the increase." We have always stressed the greatness of the part which the fervent prayers and silent sufferings of the members play in this great work of comforting the sick. They can indeed be "silent partners" of us who labor actively in behalf of 

God's afflicted children.

There were times when our hearts were filled with joy and gratitude as we read of the prayers and sufferings which individual members promised to offer for us at certain hours of the day, or certain days in the week. 

We felt the need of these efficacious offerings, not only for our selves but in behalf of the many intentions commended to our prayers and to the prayers of the members of the Apostolate. Many who are in good health seek the intercession of the sick, whom they regard as chosen souls of God, and upon whose prayers they place great

value. At times the requests which come to us are very urgent, and intercessory prayer must be at once forthcoming. Yet, there was no way of reaching the members except through the columns of our little quarterly publication.

In June of 1934 a plan was devised and outlined to the members. By means of this, the deficiency mentioned above could be supplied. The home of the Apostolate was to be considered the main spiritual powerhouse; the sickrooms the substations; the members themselves the generators. Their prayers and sufferings are of such great spiritual value that they cannot be measured in terms of a scientific nature. Needs everywhere are great. They may be right here at the office, in the vicinity, or in the distance, yes, even in foreign lands where some missionary be in dire distress of soul or body, and in the realms of purgatory when the notice of the death of a member is received by us. Certainly, that which is possible for man to accomplish by means of electricity and the radio, Christ the Divine Transformer can do with prayer and suffering. When there is no special need for the application of these spiritual offerings contributed by the members we confidently place them with the mercy and goodness of God to be used as He may deem best. may

This plan met with a generous response from the members. They voiced their approval by promising to set aside the prayers and sufferings of certain hours of the day or night, either weekly, daily, or monthly. Some pledged themselves to offer special prayers each day, week or month. Responses were received from 227 members, including 161 lay persons and 66 religious, residing in 28

States, and in Canada, Nova Scotia, Panama, and Japan. 

Who can measure the spiritiual power stored in this powerhouse, awaiting the moment when God will apply it where it will do most good? We are confident that by means of this enterprise many things shall be accomplished by the members for God and souls. 

When a certain bishop was appointed to Cochin, China, his first act was to found a community of Carmelite nuns in his diocese. Some person commenting on this said, "Necessaries ought to precede luxuries in the building up of a diocese." Can you guess the nature of the answer given by the good bishop? Listen, and ponder well his words. The answer was, "What you think is a luxury is to me the first necessity of the Christian ministry. Ten nuns who pray will help me as much as twenty missionaries who preach." That bishop recognized the value of sacrificial prayer. Though the sick are not confined within cloistered walls, their prayers are of great value, especially when offered amid pain and suffering. 

The hours spent in a sickroom ought never be long; time should never hang heavy on the hands of those who suffer. It will not, if once they have the firm conviction that, somewhere, someone is in need of their prayers. 

Was It You?

The day was long, the burden I had borne 

Seemed heavier than I could longer bear, And then it lifted- but I did not know

Someone had knelt in prayer, Had taken me to God that very hour, And asked the easing of the load, and He,

In infinite compassion, had stooped down 

And taken it from me.

The Birthplace and Home of the Apostolate of Suffering

We can not tell how often as we pray For some bewildered one, hurt and distressed, The answer comes - but many times those hearts 

Find sudden peace and rest. 

SOME ONE had prayed, and Faith, a reaching hand, Took hold of God, and brought Him down that day!

So many, many hearts have need of prayer, – 

Oh, let us pray. 

4 

Precious Treasures

Whenever the priest ascends the altar of God to offer the unbloody sacrifice of Calvary, he kisses the altar stone wherein some relics of martyred saints are enclosed. This act is a mark of reverence and love for the saints, and for God who is the delight and crown of all the saints. This act of homage is repeated eight times during Holy Mass. 

It is a beautiful appeal to the saints of the Church Triumphant by the faithful of the Church Militant. 

The Council of Trent decided that the bodies of the martyrs and other saints who were living members of Jesus Christ and temples of the Holy Ghost are worthy to be honored by the faithful. From the earliest times Holy 

Mother Church celebrated Holy Mass over the tombs of the martyrs in the catacombs. Later, in Rome, splendid churches were erected to preserve the tombs of celebrated martyrs. Their bodies were placed under the high altar of the Basilica dedicated to them, and even now the relics of martyrs are inserted into a small sepulcher hollowed into the altar stone on which the Holy Sacrifice is offered to God.

To increase our veneration for the relics of the saints, Holy Mother Church instituted the feast of the Holy Relics. This feast is observed in some places on the 5th of 

November and is directed to the relics of those saints preserved in the churches of the diocese. Just as a supernatural virtue issued from the human body of our Lord and healed the infirmities of the afflicted who approached Him, just as the hem of His garment effected a cure of bodily suffering in behalf of the one who touched it, so too, God at times permits His saints to work miracles in behalf of the faithful here on earth who devoutly venerate their relics. Even as the sufferings of Our Lord and the sorrows of Mary are a source of grace and blessing to us, so, too, are the trials and the penitential works of the saints of God. It is impossible to think of this without experiencing a feeling of joy and gratitude. 

Here in the home of the Apostolate fourteen first class relics of saints are preserved. We look upon them as most valued treasures. The most precious of all is the splinter of the True Cross of Christ. This was given to us by the late Bishop James Anthony Walsh, M.M., of Maryknoll,

New York. He obtained it for us at the time he was consecrated in Rome. By a strange coincidence it reached us on the seventh anniversary of the foundation of the Apostolate, August 24, 1933, during the year of the extraordinary jubilee of the nineteen hundreth anniversary of Our 

Lord's passion and death upon the cross.

The relic of our patroness, St. Lidwina, was procured for us through the assistance of Rev. Hubert Van Meer during the month of May 1928.

The relics of St. Francis of Assisi and St. Timothy were

given to us by Rev. Sebastian Schaaf, O.M.Cap. From the very beginning he had taken an active interest in the work of the Apostolate. It was this kind priest who induced a Capuchin lay brother to give us the relic of St. 

Anthony of Padua. 

Through the influence of several priests of the Carmelite Order relics of the three great Carmelite Saints, St. 

Theresa of Avila, St. John of the Cross, and St. Therese of Lisieux, came into our possesion. 

A relic of the beloved and powerful patron of the sick, St. Camillus de Lellis, was obtained through the kindness of Rev. Carl Mansfeld, O.S.C., formerly superior of St.

Camillus Hospital for men here in Milwaukee. 

In the year 1932 one of our priest members, Rev. 

Arthur F. Shank, attended the Eucharistic Congress in

Ireland and also spent some time in Rome. While in the eternal city he made provisions for some relics to be sent to us. In the month of December we received precious relics of the following saints: St. Paul of the Cross, the founder of the Passionist Order; St. Ignatius of Loyola, the founder of the Jesuit Order; St. John of God, who organized the Religious Hospitalers of St. John; St. Clare of Assisi, foundress of the Poor Clares; and St. Gertrude, of the Order of St. Benedict. 

Through the kindness of Rev. Leo Hargarten, S.V.D., the son of one of the invalid members of the Apostolate, and rector of St. Richard's Mission House in Hadzor,

Droitwich, England, a relic of the great patron of the secular clergy, St. John Vianney, better known as the 

Cure of Ars, came into our possession. 

Upon the recommendation of the Rev. P. J. Byrne, of

Maryknoll, New York, the Most Reverend Jean Baptiste de Goesbriant, Titular Archbishop of Marcianopolis, and

Superior General of the Paris Foreign Mission Seminary, Paris, France, sent us a relic of Blessed Theophane Venard, the modern martyr so admired and loved by the 

Little Flower of Jesus. This kind bishop died March 5, 1935.

Papers of authentication were received with all these relics, and these are preserved carefully here in our office. 

Among the lesser relics in our possession are a piece of the amice worn by Father Damien, the heroic apostle of the lepers; a particle of the habit of St. Madeleine Sophie 

Barat, of St. Bernadette, St. Margaret Mary, St. Francis de

Sales, and a piece of cloth which touched the miraculous body of St. Francis Xavier in Goa.

Thus while in the midst of our work in behalf of the sick, we are surrounded by all these relics. Each evening as we kneel in prayer before our little shrine, we commend our members, living and deceased, to the intercession of the saints whose relics we revere. We recite the prayer said by the priest at Holy Mass when he kisses the altar stone: “We beseech Thee, O Lord, by the merits of 

Thy saints whose relics are here and of all the saints that 

Thou wouldst vouchsafe to forgive us all our sins. Amen."

What was the secret that made these holy men and women saints of God? It was "doing ordinary things extraordinary well." Through the intercession of these saints may all the members of the Apostolate learn to practice the secret that makes saints.

To play through life a perfect part, Unnoticed and unknown, To seek no rest in any heart, Save that of God alone. 

To bear the pain that He doth will 

To love the cross so great, To suffer grief and sorrow still 

And for the crown to wait.

This is a pathway hard to choose, A bitter life to share,

For human heart would still refuse 

A crown of thorns to wear;

But since we know the road is steep 

That leads to heav'nly bliss

Where higher graces could we reap 

Than in a life like this?

CHAPTER XVI SAFELY HOME 

"We have loved them in life, let us not forget them in death until by our prayers we have conducted them into the house of the Lord." - St. Ambrose. 

HENEVER the sad news of the death of an

W Apostolate member or benefactor reaches us, sorrow fills our hearts. The bond of suffering unites us closely to our members and endears them to our souls, even though we may never have had the privilege of meeting them in person.

Truly, we have loved them in life, yet mindful of the words of St. Paul, “Be not sorrowful, even as those who have no hope" we raise our thoughts to the world beyond, knowing that those who have gone before us shall be waiting to welcome us. Until then we pray that a place of refreshment, light, and peace be granted to them. 

Our Departed Shepherd

The first great sorrow and loss came to the Apostolate with the death of our beloved Archbishop, the Most 

Reverend Sebastian G. Messmer, D.D. For some years had been in bad health; but, an annual visit to his home he 210

Safely Home 

211 in Goldach, Switzerland, usually renewed his strength to such a degree as to enable him to carry on his labors for his cherished flock. In the early part of summer 1930, he again sought restoration through the invigorating air of his native land, but alas, this time it was not to be found. 

On the feast of St. Dominic, August 4, 1930, the angel of death summoned his soul to receive the reward due to a good and faithful shepherd of souls.

Within a few hours the news reached Milwaukee, and for the first time in twenty-seven years, the faithful of the Archdiocese of Milwaukee were without a shepherd. 

In compliance with a wish often expressed by the deceased Archbishop, he was laid to rest on August 7, 1930, beside the little church in his native village. 

Here in Milwaukee, in the beloved Archbishop's own Cathedral Church, a Pontifical Mass of requiem was offered by the Most Rev. Francis J. Beckman, D.D., Archbishop of Dubuque, Iowa, on the morning of Wednesday, August 13, 1930. He was assisted by the Rt. Rev.

Msgr. B. G. Traudt, acting head of the Archdiocese, as presbyter assistant, and the Rev. M. Domachowski, Milwaukee, as deacon, and the Rev. George Eilers of the 

Seminary of St. Francis de Sales, as subdeacon of the Mass.

The sermon on this occasion was preached by the Most 

Rev. Joseph Chartrand, D.D., Bishop of Indianapolis,

Indiana, who but three years later followed him in death. 

The empty episcopal throne and the pulpit were draped in black, and before the altar where the Archbishop had so often offered the Holy Sacrifice of Mass, there was a catafalque. Upon it was a stole, symbolic of his priesthood, and a mitre, symbolic of his episcopacy.

Ten bishops, and a score of monsignori were in the sanctuary; the Cathedral was filled with priests and lay people who had come to pray for the soul of their departed shepherd.

The death of Archbishop Messmer left a vacancy not only in our membership ranks, but in the very life of the Apostolate itself. For, in him we lost the kind and loving shepherd who sanctioned the founding of this Pious Union of the Sick. He was at all times most keenly interested in the progress of the Apostolate, encouraging us by his words of cheer whenever an occasion presented itself.

To this day I regret never having had the privilege of meeting him personally. Due to my physical condition, appointments could not be made in advance. In winter I was unable to leave the house, and in summer the Archbishop spent the beautiful days recuperating from periods of illness. At times, too, when appointments were sought, the Archbishop had other engagements made which could not be cancelled. There will, however, be a meeting in heaven on some future day when all debts of gratitude can be paid in person. Until then our appreciation must be expressed in prayer. 

From the December issue of 1927 we quote the words of cheer addressed to the members of the Apostolate by 

His Excellency: 

"The words of St. Paul, the great sufferer among the 

Apostles, 'rejoicing in hope; patient in tribulation; instant in prayer,' might well serve as a slogan for the members of the Apostolate of Suffering. "Patience and submission to God's will in all their tribulations and sufferings will come to them through

Safely Home 

213 that cheerful hope that if patiently enduring in union with the suffering Savior, they will also partake in His glory. That holy patience in suffering will be strengthened and supported in them by their constant prayers. 

The beauty and glory of patient suffering in union with the sufferings of Christ is one of the most consoling messages of our holy Faith. Hence I wish the "Apostolate of 

Suffering" and "Our Good Samaritan” an ever increasing diffusion to bring this joyful message to all our suffering brethren." 

On the first Friday, September 5, 1930, our Spiritual

Director, at our request, offered the Holy Sacrifice for the repose of the soul of the Archbishop. The members were asked to offer the entire day for the same intention in return for all the favors that His Excellency bestowed upon the Apostolate and thus indirectly upon each of them. "A loving father to his flock, So kind in word and deed 

A heart that knew the wants of men, And answered every need.

A life well lived, a fight well fought, His name will never cease 

To live in sore afflicted hearts - 

God grant he rest in peace!" 2 

Distinguished Members

On December 8, 1933, the Apostolate sustained another loss in the death of the Most Rev. Joseph Chartrand,

Bishop of Indianapolis, Indiana. He had been very interested in the work of the Apostolate and was one of our benefactors.

Another deceased prelate who manifested great interest in our work was the Most Rev. Alphonse J. Smith, D.D.,

Bishop of Nashville, Tennessee. During a siege of illness when confined to the Sacred Heart Sanitarium here in Milwaukee, he was enrolled in the Apostolate on November 26, 1929. From one of his letters we quote these words, "My enrollment in the Apostolate was a source of great comfort to me. Now, that by God's grace I hope soon to return to my work, I shall not forget my membership, but shall continue to pray for those members of the Apostolate who are sick or in suffering. May God bless you and your work." 

Five years later, on December 16, 1935, Bishop Smith entered his eternal reward. We feel confident of his continued prayers for us. 

On the 14th of April, 1936, the Tuesday after Easter, another dear friend of the Apostolate was called home.

Sorrow filled our hearts when the news of the death of the Most Rev. James A. Walsh, of Maryknoll, New York, reached us. It seems a strange and significant coincidence that Bishop Walsh who at all times took an interest in the Apostolate, and to whom we are indebted for the precious relic of the True Cross of Christ, should be called to his eternal reward on the feast of St. Lidwina, the patroness of the Apostolate.

Bishop Walsh had been a semi-invalid for over a year and a half before his death; it was then, as he expressed it, that he learned to appreciate the Apostolate of Suffering. On several occasions he had sent us leaflets and booklets for distribution among the sick whom we visit here

Safely Home 

215 in Milwaukee. From his place in heaven may he obtain further blessings for us.

The first priest and Domestic Prelate to precede our other members on their journey to a better land was the illustrious Right Rev. Msgr. Joseph Rainer, the rector emeritus of the Seminary of St. Francis de Sales, St. Francis, Wisconsin. The benign Monsignor had manifested a special interest in the Apostolate from the time of its inception. We felt honored to have his name on our register of members. He was called home on the 12th of January, 1927, at the age of 81 years. In union with the faculty and students of the Seminary we mourned the loss of him who was their father, friend, and counselor for over thirty-three years.

Thirty years of invalidism, in place of active work in the classroom, did not smother the cheerfulness of the 

Rev. Thomas F. Brown, S.J., of Marquette University, Milwaukee. Like the great St. Ignatius of Loyola, the founder of the Jesuit Order, he learned that out of suffering is born the grace that makes us saints. 

Father Brown joined the Apostolate on August 6, 1927. He observed his golden jubilee as a Jesuit with a 

Solemn High Mass and a reception at Marquette University on July 20, 1930. No greater tribute could be paid to this heroic priest than that uttered by Father Lord, "The University would rather lose its most brilliant professor than part with this helpless paralytic who is one of the happiest, most cheerful, and calmest souls in the world." After another year of suffering he answered the final call of his Master on July 27, 1931. Heaven welcomed another saint.

Another invalid priest and eminent scholar belonging to the Apostolate was the Rev. John Zybura, of the Archdiocese of Cincinnati, who because of ill health resided in 

Colorado Springs, Colorado. His illness extended over a period of seventeen years, many of which were spent in a wheelchair. He was enrolled in the Apostolate on 

May 19, 1927. From his letter of December 12, 1928, we take the following excerpt: "You will be glad to hear that I am able to celebrate Holy

Mass again. Imagine, if you can, what this means to me, after having been deprived of this ineffable glorious privilege for fully eight years. Please help me to thank Our Lord for this great grace. I am making and will continue to make a daily memento for your intentions and those of the Apostolate members." 

Later on, in February, 1929, he wrote: "Today I am bringing from Our Blessed Lord a gift for you and all the members of the Apostolate. I shall try to offer the 

Holy Sacrifice of Mass once a month for your and their intentions. I can not specify the day, however, for that will be conditioned by my other obligations."

Father Zybura was the author of several books. He donated two copies, Contemporary Godlessness and The 

Problem of Evil and Human Destiny to the Little Flower 

Library. After a few more years of suffering patiently borne he fell asleep in the Lord in October, 1934. How beautiful must have been the welcome he received from his loving Master!

Another renowned member of the Apostolate was 

Brother Joseph Dutton, a native of Wisconsin and a

Safely Home 

217 veteran of the Civil War. He was the faithful assistant and collaborer of the saintly Father Damien, and after the death of this holy priest he continued to serve the lepers of Molokai.

Brother Dutton was enrolled in the Apostolate on July 3, 1928. He was a real ministering angel to the afflicted. His was a kindly heart, a noble, brave, undaunted spirit; truly, he was a martyr to the cause of 

Christ and suffering humanity. He died on March 23, 1931. Here in the office we treasure several letters received from him. In silent tribute we utter a prayer, beseeching him to obtain for us and for our members the grace and strength to be in all the vicissitudes of life, a hero, a soldier of Christ, a saint. 3 

Other Heroic Sufferers 

Saintly souls among the sick are also found in the ranks of the laity. Among the many who have gone before us, the memory of one stands out particularly. It is that of Theresa Wiebelhaus, of Detroit, Michigan, who joined the Apostolate on October 6, 1927.

Her invalidism was caused by a fall as she alighted from a street car on a rainy day and slipped on the wet pavement. For thirty years she suffered continual pain, yet she accepted her suffering with complete resignation and fortitude.

Theresa was actively interested in the Apostolate, the 

Library, and the Burse Fund. It is truly astonishing what can be accomplished by invalids on their bed of pain. 

On May 25, 1931, she celebrated the silver jubilee of her

confinement in bed, by having a High Mass of Thanksgiving offered in the chapel of the Hospital where she resided.

After the silver jubilee, five more years of intense suffering were Theresa's portion, and then on October 20, 1936, just a few months after the celebration of our Tenth 

Anniversary, the angel replaced the silver crown of suffering with the golden crown of life everlasting. 

Examples of suffering heroically borne come to us also from the members in other countries and of other races. 

We cite the one of our first Japanese member, Miss Mary 

Josephine Nakamura of Yokohama, Japan. 

Through the kindness of Father Harold Felsecker she was introduced to the Apostolate. She was baptized at home on April 21, 1926, about a year after her illness commenced. She became an Apostolate member on 

December 7, 1931. In her first letter she stated that she had not been to church for about five years, but that she received Holy Communion at home twice a month, and that she would be confirmed at home by the Bishop. 

The sentiments she expressed in regard to her membership in the Apostolate, and her beautiful thoughts on the nobility of suffering, were truly an inspiration. After four more years of patient suffering caused by the lingering disease of tuberculosis so prevalent in the Orient, she succumbed to the fatal disease on September 23, 1935. May this first member from the Land of the Rising Sun obtain for her countrymen the grace of conversion, and, for the sake of her companions in suffering, also the establishment of an Apostolate for their comfort and consolation.

Safely Home 

219 Among Our Own The first member to leave the ranks of the Apostolate and enter that of heaven's citizens was Mr. Arthur Beyer of Milwaukee. He died on November 3, 1926, just a few weeks after his enrollment in the Apostolate. Shortly after his death, his bereaved wife, Mrs. Gertrude Beyer, called at the home of the Apostolate in order to become better acquainted with the consoling work being done in behalf of the sick. Her interest was aroused to the degree that she volunteered her service whenever it would be needed. To this very day she assists with the addressing of the envelopes when the little paper is prepared for the mails.

The first religious to join her Divine Spouse after enrollment in the Apostolate was Sr. M. Euphrasia, S.S.N.D. 

She slept away peacefully in the convent at Elm Grove, Wisconsin, on the 19th of November 1926. Sister Euphrasia had been the teacher of Bishop Muench when he attended St. Boniface parochial School in Milwaukee. Of her it can be said: "They that teach others shall shine as stars in all eternity." May our beloved Spiritual Director be the brightest jewel in her heavenly crown.

Within the first year of the Apostolate's existence the list of charter members showed a vacancy. It was caused by the death of Mrs. Barbara Janzer, who died on the 

29th of June, 1927. One of her daughters, Mrs. Isabel

Gengler, a former schoolmate of mine, has been a faithful helper in the work of the Apostolate throughout this decade of years, and an active member of the Good 

Samaritan Guild since its foundation in August, 1930.

The death of our first printer, Mr. Francis Schaafs, occurred in the month of December, 1933. His generosity to us at the time the Apostolate was founded enabled us to surmount many difficulties of a financial nature. 

In July, 1935, we lost another benefactor in the person of George Cannon, a member of the firm that has been doing our printing for the past six years. We pray that the benefits these men conferred upon us pleaded for them in their hour of need. 

The Fathers of the Founders 

It seems a strange coincidence that Our Blessed Lord called to Himself the fathers of the founders of the Apostolate within a few months of each other, and just shortly before the 10th Anniversary of the Apostolate.

In the wee small hours of Easter morn, on April 12, 1936, the Angel of Death, who had hovered over the home of the Apostolate for four months, entered silently through its portals and took with him the soul of my father, Mr. Matthias Tiry. 

To be privileged to bid farewell to earth after three years of suffering, well prepared for the final journey, and to enter into eternal life, our true heritage, in company with the Risen Savior, was indeed a beautiful thing, and a source of consolation to the bereaved family. Perhaps it was a special grace reserved for him who so generously had opened his home to accomodate the Apostolate and its various activities from the very day of its foundation.

Safely Home 

221 Just four months later, amid the joyful preparations for the celebration of our tenth anniversary, our hearts were saddened when again the angel of death entered the ranks of the Apostolate and summoned the father of our beloved Spiritual Director, Mr. Joseph Muench, to the embrace of His Master. Mr. Muench had been ill for a whole year. Already when the appointment of his son to the episcopacy arrived he was failing in health. For a time he rallied in response to the best of medical care. 

Through a special grace of God he was able to attend the consecration services, the installation of Bishop Muench at Fargo, and the wedding of his youngest child,

Dorothy, in June, 1936. After that there was a gradual decline in health, and on Thursday, July 23, he fell asleep in the Lord.

We are confident that the father of Bishop Muench, who like another Joseph watched faithfully over the family entrusted to him, will intercede for us at the throne of God. 

Waiting for Us

Within these ten years many cherished members have been called from the ranks of the Apostolate, and very often their going left a void in our hearts. The total number of members who have preceded us to heaven's glorious home is 2,356, including 14 charter members. A large number indeed, yet not surprising when we consider the fact that all our members are eligible for death, sooner or later. A large mortality rate must be expected in an association composed of sick persons.

To them we had given a pledge of our sympathy and understanding in the burdens and crosses they carried,

and in return we had received the staunch support of their prayers. Though in the world, facing its battles, and striving to give our best efforts to those who remain with us, we find our thoughts straying across the boundaries of this earth, out beyond the stars where dwell the companions of our yesterdays. We dream of the hour which will find us reunited never to know the poignancy of separation; we conjure visions of a little corner in the heavens set apart for those who belonged to the large family of sufferers here on earth; we frame the words of greetings that will burst from our lips as we shall be welcomed by those whose sorrow has been turned into joy. 

For, as sure as peace follows suffering, and eternal joy is the reward of earthly pain borne for and in union with the Master, so, too, after earth comes heaven, and out of our loss the gain.

In union with Holy Mother Church and with the priest when he offers the Holy Sacrifice we say, "To these, O Lord, and to all who rest in Christ, grant, we beseech Thee, a place of refreshment, light, and peace.”

CHAPTER XVII 

OUR TENTH ANNIVERSARY 

"One thing have I asked of the Lord, this will I seek after . that I may see the delight of the Lord, and may visit His temple."

Ps. 26.4. 

NNIVERSARY days are days of graces and blesings! The Lord is very good to us, for, almost each year at the Anniversary of the founding of the Apostolate or around its Approbation Day, He sent us some outward manifestation or token of His pleasure. These we regarded as signs that He approved of the work being done for the afflicted lambs of His flock.

The winter of 1935 found us making plans for the celebration of the Tenth Anniversary to be observed between August and October of 1936. This anniversary was to be observed in a special way, one that would give external and public manifestation of the gratitude that filled our hearts for the blessings that came to us and our members during ten years spent in this most consoling and beautiful work. Yet, we also wanted to celebrate in such a way that our members, at least those in the city of Milwaukee, the birthplace of the Apostolate, could participate in some measure, and join us in giving thanks to 

Almighty God.

After imploring the light of the Holy Spirit in fervent prayer it was decided to carry out the suggestion of the 223

Divine Good Samaritan Who on one occasion said: "When thou makest a feast, call the poor, the maimed, the lame, and the blind, and thou shalt be blessed."

Moreover, recalling that "It is more blessed to give than to receive,” a very unique celebration was planned. For about eight years we had been thinking of having a "Eucharistic Day for the Sick," similar to the celebrations held in Holland, Germany, Switzerland, and other countries of Europe.

In looking over the old copies of "Our Good Samaritan" we found that this idea was mentioned in the first printed issue of March, 1927. Nine years had elapsed, and the hopes and dreams of those days had not yet been realized. The Tenth Anniversary afforded an opportune occasion for a celebration of this nature. Well do I recall when I mentioned this subject to our Spiritual Director for the first time. The month of December found me in bed seriously ill with a nervous breakdown and other afflictions. It was during the time that my father was also very sick. Days lengthened into weeks and very little progress was made on the road to recovery. When in the latter part of January, 1936, Bishop Muench spent a few hours with us, I proposed this to him. Such a celebration, however, would entail a great deal of preparation; months of work would be required until all would be in readiness. His Excellency listened in silent thought; then after some of the most important points were discussed and settled he approved the plans. 

Gratitude filled my heart and I felt that my recovery would be but a matter of time. Slowly but surely health was restored to me. Each week thoughts and ideas were put in writing, with the hope of carrying them out as

soon as the warm weather arrived. But before action could be taken, the angel of death visited our home and called my father to his eternal home on Easter, April 12, 1936. This interrupted our plans for the time being, until the shock of the bereavement had passed. Then preparations began in earnest. Preparations

The church selected for the celebration of the Eucharistic Day for the Sick was that of St. Sebastian. It was chosen from among all other churches of the city because it was the one most adapted in every way for so unique a service for the sick.

The pastor of St. Sebastian's Church, the Rev. Otto A. 

Haertle, felt privileged to act as host to the sick and afflicted. In imitation of Our Blessed Lord he volunteered to open wide the doors of his church, and welcomed most heartily the opportunity to gather within the sacred and beautiful edifice those "whom the Lord loveth,” and who follow the Divine Master on the royal road of the cross.

About six weeks previous to the date set for the observance of our Tenth Anniversary, Archbishop Stritch sent a letter to all the pastors in the diocese informing them of our impending plans for the "Eucharistic Day for the Sick," and asking them to co-operate with us in our efforts to make possible this service for the sick in his diocese.

Although we had a goodly number of infirm and invalids on the register of the Apostolate, we felt convinced that there were hundreds who as yet had not

heard of our work. How could we get in touch with them? There was but one thing to do, namely, to go after them! In this endeavor we enlisted the aid of the pastors. 

A letter was sent to each one here in the city asking for a list of their infirm and disabled parishioners. 

In the meantime a questionnaire was sent to those who were on our membership list; when the lists from the pastors were received, they were taken care of in the same way.

The superiors of hospitals and sanatoriums were approached in regard to the supplies to be furnished, such as cots, bedding, and other necessary equipment needed for a celebration where sick persons are the guests of honor.

Wheel-chairs were furnished by wholesale dealers, individual families, hospitals, and sanatoriums. Kindhearted persons who owned trucks donated their service for transporting the wheel-chairs to and from the church, taking them up the afternoon before, and returning them to their respective owners after the last services in the church.

The co-operation of a number of physicians was enlisted, and nurses were promised by the various hospitals, sanatoriums, and convents. A number of nurses on private duty also volunteered their services. They were in attendance throughout the entire day.

The Christian Mother's of St. Sebastian's Church looked after the luncheon and dinner. Some of the food for the meals was donated by wholesale dealers. The 

Sisters of St. Francis in charge of the school arranged for the training of the little children for the procession in the afternoon, while the altar boys were under the direc-

tion of the able assistant, Rev. Anthony J. Herrmann. 

The flowers for decorating the altars were donated by a wholesale florist, and those carried by the children in the afternoon procession with the Blessed Sacrament were taken care of by the kind Sisters. 

It was but natural that an affair of this nature would entail a great expense. To meet the emergency, the ladies of  held a raffle of some religious articles donated by various religious church goods houses and individuals, while a group of girls called "The Elizabethans" sponsored a card party. Both affairs met with a fine response and netted a neat sum of money. 

Individual donations were also received from bishops, priests, and lay people.

The problem of transporting the sick was the greatest of all, perhaps because it was the first time such an event took place in this city. The good Lord, however, took care of the matter in His own way, and when the day arrived automobiles and drivers were promised to all the 

Isick who had made reservations.

Through the kindness of the Milwaukee Journal the morning service consisting of the Pontifical Mass and the sermon was broadcast over their station WTMJ for the length of one hour. This enabled those patients in homes, hospitals, and sanatoriums who were too ill to be removed or not permitted to leave the institution, to participate to some extent in this beautiful service arranged for their benefit. The services of the Rev. 

Eustace F. Brennan were engaged as commentator for the broadcast.

Letters had been sent to the Superintendents of the various public Sanatoriums in the State asking them to

announce this special program and to permit their patients to listen in at that hour. 

Admission to the church was by ticket only. The color of the ticket corresponded with the bow of colored ribbon attached to the pews. This facilitated the work of the ushers of the parish who never before dealt with a crowd of infirm and disabled persons. Perfect order was preserved to the astonishment of all who attended the unique service.

Policemen on motorcycles were engaged to take care of the traffic, and an ambulance was in readiness all during the day in case of an emergency.

The arrangements for the solemn services in church were in the hands of the Rev. William S. Gensler, the 

Moderator of the Apostolate. He also acted as chairman of the entire celebration.

The weeks preceding the feast were filled to capacity with work of a varied kind; trials and difficulties were not wanting, but the good Lord Who always "fits the back to the burden" also gave strength for this task. 

Each day had its specific problems to solve, but finally the evening of the last day came and passed, and with thankful hearts everybody retired in joyful expectation of the morrow's feast. 2 

The Eucharistic Day for the Sick 

On the morning of August 26, 1936, the words of St.

Valerian, "The day so ardently awaited has come at last. 

Behold the day sweeter to me than all the feasts of the earth," found an echo not only in the hearts of the invalids and the afflicted, but in the hearts of all who

shared their joyful anticipation of the "Eucharistic Day" arranged for their special benefit. 

Morning dawned after a night filled with electrical storms that made the hearts of the sick beat with apprehension. The skies were overcast but even though it threatened to rain, invisible hands seemed to hold back the rain-drops lest they mar the beauty of the day. There was no sunshine from the skies, but the interior sunshine that brightened the hearts of the sick could not be described in words.

On that morning all roads led to St. Sebastian's Church on the northwest side of Milwaukee where the celebration was held. Was it not fitting that this feast for the sick should take place in a church dedicated to a martyr who shed his blood for Christ? The example of the martyrs has always been an inspiration to the sick, urging them on to be martyrs in desire, and though they are not priviliged to lay down their lives for Christ they can live and suffer for Him. Who of us can say that their part is the easiest?

Shortly after eight o'clock the sick began to arrive at the church. Tender hands assisted them from automobiles, taxicabs, street cars, and busses, and led them carefully into the church. The invalids were placed gently into the wheel-chairs awaiting them and then wheeled slowly, through a side entrance reserved for them, into the church.
