There they occupied the place of honor, in front of the first pews; from there they were able to witness undisturbed in close proximity to the altar some of the most glorious celebrations and ceremonies of Holy Mother Church. Many of the sick were weeping, not tears of sorrow, but of joy, because once more they could

in truth say "I will go in to the altar of God." Their companions shared their joy and happiness; they, too, were weeping because of the privilege granted to their loved ones. Truly, it seemed that the church on that morning presented a scene from the days of Christ - the temple or street in Jerusalem where the sick had been carried when word had reached the inhabitants that the great Healer had arrived. In Jerusalem He was seen by mortal eyes; here in the church He was beheld by the sick with the eyes of faith. After they had taken in the beauty of the magnificent altar in the spacious sanctuary which had been decorated with stately yellow gladioli and banked with palms, ferns, and standards of the same beautiful flowers, they turned toward the tabernacle with a tender love-light gleaming in their eyes. There as within a prison-cell dwelt the Divine Master Who still calls out to the sick, "Come to Me all you who labor and are burdened, and I will refresh you." For many years they had not been able to accept His loving invitation, but today, on this days of days, kind hearts afforded them the opportunity.

Somewhere in the distance a bell rang softly, and while the choir boys of St. Amelians Orphan Asylum sang the "Ecce Sacerdos Magnus" the procession filed into the church. The Rev. Robert M. Hoeller who carried the processional cross was flanked by two priest acolytes, and followed by altar boys. After this came the priests, about ninety-five in number, and then the five monsignori. The officers of the Mass, the Rev. William V.

Groessel as deacon and the Rev. Frank E. Weis as subdeacon, preceded our Spiritual Director, the Most Reverend Aloisius J. Muench, Bishop of Fargo, North Dakota.

Our own venerable and beloved shepherd, the Most Reverend S. A. Stritch, Archbishop of Milwaukee, was escorted by the Rev. Oscar Ziegler and the Rev. James 

W. Huepper.

After a few moments of silent prayer before the altar, the Archbishop ascended the episcopal throne while Bishop Muench was robed with the vestments required for the celebration of a Solemn Pontifical Mass. The 

Mass used on this day was the Votive Mass of the Blessed Sacrament, a Mass filled with the spirit of joy and thanksgiving. The singing of the Gloria brought a gleam of 

Christmas light and peace into the hearts of the sick while the Credo strengthened their belief in the Triune 

God and His Church.

The Most Reverend Archbishop spoke to the stricken lambs of his flock. The unique audience listened attentively to his words of comfort, cheer, and encouragement, and to his plea for a remembrance in their prayers and sufferings. 99 At the Offertory of the Mass the hearts of the sick were offered to God that He might accept them as a sacrifice even as He accepted the Sacrifice of His Divine Son on Calvary and now again on the holy altar. In the 

Preface of the Mass was heard again that note of deep peace and profound joy of Christmas, and with the ringing of the bell at the "Sanctus, Sanctus, Sanctus,' a feeling of awe and reverance swept into the hearts of all who worshipped. Softly came the words of Consecration, and once more after years of privation the eyes of the sick looked upon the White Host and the Sacred 

Chalice with the Precious Blood as they were lifted on high by a successor of the Apostles, and then were lowered in silent and profound adoration.

The Agnus Dei with the kiss of peace, and the chanting of the Confiteor by the Deacon of the Mass announced the arrival of the happy moment of Christ's visit to their hearts. Assisted by his deacon, Bishop 

Muench came down from the altar, passed through the opened sanctuary doors, and distributed the Bread of Life to the patients in wheel-chairs and those who could not go to the communion rail. After this, the patients afflicted with lesser infirmities received Holy Communion, and then their relations and friends. Verily, the Lord had come unto His own; and like Zachaeus of old, the sick "received Him with joy."

Immediately after the Mass the liturgical blessing for the sick was imparted by Bishop Muench. The responses were made by all the priests who held their hands extended over the heads of the sick. The Archbishop advanced to the pulpit and bestowed the blessing of the Holy Father on the participants of the celebration. Bishop 

Muench then spoke a few words to the faithful and to the sick. He brought to their attention the fact that by a strange coincidence this touching celebration was taking place on the sixth anniversary of Archbishop Stritch's appointment to the Metropolitan See of Milwaukee.

Therefore this day was a double feast to the shepherd of this wounded flock. Bishop Muench expressed his delight at being able to be in their midst today and encouraged the sick to patience and resignation. Thereupon the choir intoned the Te Deum which was taken up by the multitude and concluded while the procession filed out of the church.

Able-bodied men, boy scouts, and nurses transported the sick to the school hall where luncheon was served to

them. After the rest hour period, a visiting hour was held. The Archbishop, Bishop Muench, the priests, relations, and friends gathered around the patients whose faces radiated joy and happiness. The time passed all too rapidly, and very soon the nurses made preparations for taking their charges back to church for the afternoon services.

At two o'clock the procession entered the church again as in the morning. The Blessed Sacrament was exposed, and then Bishop Muench recited an act of Adoration. 

This was followed by the Consecration of the Sick to the Heart of Jesus. All the sick joined in silent prayer using the leaflet which had been given to them as they entered the church. After this came the sermon. Bishop Muench had selected for his subject, "The Eucharist and the Sick." 

The Litany of the Sacred Heart was recited and then the following invocations similar to those used at the shrine of Our Lady in Lourdes, France, when the Eucharistic Procession takes place. These invocations were led by the 

Rev. Otto Haertle, pastor of St. Sebastian's Church, and the responses were made by the entire congregation. 

Lord, we adore Thee. Invocations 

Lord, we hope in Thee. 

Lord, we love Thee. 

Hosanna, Hosanna to the Son of David. 

Blessed is He Who cometh in the Name of the Lord.

Thou art Christ, the Son of the Living God. 

My Lord and my God. 

Let us adore forever the Most Holy Sacrament. (three times) 

Lord, we believe, but do Thou increase our faith.

Thou art the Resurrection and the Life. 

Help us, Jesus, we perish. 

Lord, if Thou wilt, Thou canst cure us. 

Lord, speak but one word, and I shall be healed. 

Jesus, Son of Mary, have mercy on us. 

Jesus, Son of David, have mercy on us. 

Spare, O Lord, spare Thy people, and be not angry with us forever. (three times) 

O God, be merciful to me, a sinner. 

O God, come to our aid. Lord make haste to help us. 

Lord, Thou knowest that I love Thee. 

Lord, he whom Thou lovest is sick. 

Lord, that I may see. 

Lord, that I may hear. 

Lord, that I may walk. 

Lord, that Thy Will may be done.

In the meantime, fifty little boys and girls dressed in white and carrying a yellow gladiolus in their hand had filed into the aisles of the church and joined the crossbearer and the acolytes who had come forth from the sanctuary. Fifty altar boys carrying lighted candles in their hands were next in line; these were followed by the priests who also carried lighted candles. Amid the ringing of the bells the thurifer incensed the Blessed Sacrament which was carried by Bishop Muench under the beautifully ornamented canopy held aloft by four priests.

The procession wended its way down the center aisle, returning on the right hand aisle, and crossing over to the left. Before each of the wheel-chair patients, the 

Bishop paused to bless them with the Monstrance wherein the Divine Physician reposed, a special blessing from the Giver of all good gifts to these His beloved crossbearers. All hearts were touched, all eyes were dimmed with tears. Solemn Benediction was given, and with the

singing of the Divine Praises and the Te Deum the first Eucharistic Day for the Sick was concluded. 

Throughout the services, white-robed sister nurses and registered lay nurses watched over the sick, rearranged their pillows, and made them comfortable in every way. 

Kindhearted doctors were in attendance, and boy scouts went up and down the aisles offering water and strengthening cordials to the patients. 

Happiness reigned supreme in the hearts of the invalids and the infirm. Their spirits were revived and strengthened and their courage renewed by participating in the holy joys of the Sacrifice of the Mass and of the Solemn Benediction. Having shared in the glory of the 

Master here as on a second Mt. Tabor, they were ready, nay, eager to face again the dark hours of their Gethsemani and mayhap a future Calvary. Would not the memory of this day hover about them like an angel of mercy to strengthen them in future trials and sufferings? 

We are confident that God in His generosity bestowed

His graces most lavishly upon the bodies and souls of the sick on this the Tenth Anniversary Celebration of the Apostolate. To those who had a share in the preparations and who witnessed the beautiful, touching, and inspiring scenes enacted it was and always will be a day never to be forgotten. 3 

When Shepherds Spoke 

From the touching and inspiring sermon of Archbishop Stritch we bring to the reader the following excerpts:

"For the first time, as far as I know, we are offering this morning to the sick of our flock an opportunity to assist at Holy Mass. What would the day of an archbishop, a bishop, or a priest be without Holy Mass? 

Those who by the will of God are kept at home by sickness or infirmity, have come to appreciate as others do not, the joy and privilege of attending Holy Mass. 

The Mass, this morning, has been arranged under the patronage of the Apostolate of Suffering and principally through the efforts of Bishop Muench of Fargo who has come to us this morning to stand at the altar of God and offer Mass in our midst, and of Miss Clara Tiry, whose work in the cause of the Apostolate is untiring. 

The Apostolate of Suffering is an association to which only those who suffer from pain and sickness may belong. This association has made possible this scene for you today. It exists primarily for the purpose of helping you to use your sickness, your pains, and your inconveniences for the sanctification of your own souls and the souls of your fellowmen; by conforming yourselves to the will of

God, you fit yourself for a higher place in the kingdom of God.

Today's feast presents to me and to the rest of us a picture that we shall never forget. We shall take from it a lesson and translate that lesson into a newer love and to newer and more zealous efforts in the cause of the Gospel of God. Today, the sermon, no matter what my words may be, is preached not from the pulpit but from the pews, from the pews that are occupied by the sick. 

Secondly, sickness is the vestibule of heaven. Thirdly, sickness is usually a blessing. Look at St. Ignatius Loyola, see how the wounds he received during the war made

The Eucharistic Day for the Sick 

Mr. Charles Brixius 

Archbishop Stritch

him change his life. There never would have been a St. 

Ignatius Loyola if there had not been the battle at Pampelona. The life and work of the great St. Clare of Assisi takes on a different aspect when we realize that fourteen years were spent in suffering for God. 

Fourthly, sickness and pain are a blessing and an invitation from God to live a more spiritual life. 

It is not in this life given to us to understand fully the mystery of pain and suffering. Suffering is unintelligible without the cross. The Apostolate of Suffering is rightly so called, for you are not dead members, but working for the hereafter. You as members of the Mystic Christ have been called to imitate the Suffering Christ in a special manner. In the mystic body, Christ is the Head; you are the members. As it was fitting that He should suffer, so also is it fitting that the life of each Christian should be touched by suffering. By your generous acceptance of the sickness or infirmity that is sent to you, strive to make yourselves saintly members of the Mystic Christ. You who are physically afflicted often are the strongest spiritual workers in the Church. Remember that there could have been no glory on Easter Sunday if there had not been first the dark gloom of Good Friday.

You are inactive in the natural but not in a spiritual sense. You are the most active members of the Church if you offer your sufferings for her and for her children. 

Did not the Little Flower by her prayers and mortifications save more souls than a missionary? Offer your prayers, your inconveniences, and your mortifications to

Jesus for souls. The Apostolate of Suffering is inviting you to help the Church to save souls. You, who are members of the Apostolate have a great share in Catholic

Action. Your prayers reach out to the mission fields and doubtless in answer to those prayers the light of faith ascends into souls. We know that the gifts of God come in answer to prayer. Your prayers reach out to the bishops and priests who are laboring in the Church, and doubtless in the many things they see happening to souls about them they also see the answers to and the result of your prayers. Your prayers reach out for the conversion of sinners and peace comes to souls whose stories you will never know until you read them in the book of the recording angel. Also in your prayers be mindful of the people in Mexico, Spain, and Russia. Even the Roman 

Emperor could not invent such tortures as some of these people have to suffer in these countries. 

Yours is the active life in the Catholic Church. We come today with gratitude for what you are doing and for what you have done. We know that prayers are the means by which we can propogate the Gospel of Christ. 

We are glad that you have the opportunity of being with us in church this morning. To you particularly we confide our problem of saving souls in this archdiocese. We ask you to pray for us, we ask for just a portion of the merits of your prayers so that when life is over we may not have to appear empty handed before the Divine Savior. We plead for a little of your sufferings that we may be true to the commission given to us by the Savior when He said: 'Go forth and preach the gospel to all nations, and baptize them in the name of the Father, the 

Son, and the Holy Ghost. Amen.'"

Bishop Muench's Sermon "Blessed be the God of all comfort Who comforteth us in all our tribulations."- 2 Cor. 1:3,4. "When I think back over the decade of years which have passed since the Apostolate was founded, many incidents crowd upon my mind. One in particular left a strong impression, namely, the research for a suitable picture for the little paper "Our Good Samaritan.” 

We thought it would be an easy matter; however, we were soon disillusioned. We searched through book after book without success. At the suggestion of the late Archbishop Sebastian G. Messmer we wrote to a man who had made it his hobby to collect works of art and copies of famous masterpieces. A search through his collection of thousands of pictures was fruitless. It seemed strange that no artist had been inspired to paint one of the many scenes recorded in the gospels of Our Lord's compassion for the sick and the afflicted. Finally, after much searching we found one by a modern artist who portrayed most vividly the love and sympathy of the Divine Good Samaritan.

There is no lack of inspiration in the gospels with regard to Our Lord's love for the sick. The gospel of St. 

Mark the sixth chapter describes for us such a scene. That scene is repeated here today.

A few years ago a rich philanthropist, suffering from an incurable disease, put an end to his own life. He left a note on which was written: "My work is done; I have nothing to live for.".That man was wrong, his work was not finished. No man's work is completed until God calls him home.

When an active life is cut short by accident or infirmity you may be tempted to think that your work is done. It is not, for your work is just beginning. 

Many of you have come at the cost of much sacrifice and many inconveniences to spend this day with Jesus. 

From the tabernacle there come the words, "Come to Me, all you who labor and are burdened, and I will refresh you.' There is the Great Comforter of Whom St. Paul says, "The God of all Comfort Who comforteth us in all our tribulations." 

Take home with yourselves the memory of this beautiful day. Let it be your comfort in hours of trials and darkness. Let it be your strength when pain and suffering are difficult to bear. Turn your thoughts to the Eucharistic Lord. Visit your Savior in spirit when you can not do so in person. Receive Him into your heart by a spiritual communion. He is at all times the God of all comfort 

Who comforteth us in all our tribulations. May His blessing be yours always." 4 

Echoes from Many Hearts

Many and varied were the comments of the sick, their relations, friends, and the clergy in reference to this Eucharistic Day. As we passed through the crowds, when we assisted the invalids, and during the meals we made mental notes of the things that were said, and like Mother Mary we "kept all these words in our hearts.' 

The joy which reigned supreme in all hearts can be surmised when reading the comments that follow.

"To be able to attend Holy Mass once more after twenty-nine years absence from church is so great a privilege and a joy that it can not be described." "The inspiration which I received at this Eucharistic Service for the Sick can not be expressed in words. May God reward all those who were responsible for this movement." "It is nineteen years since I was to Mass!" whispered a man of about thirty-five years of age, as his widowed mother dressed in black knelt at his side, tears of joy dimming her eyes because once more her son was able to witness some of the glorious ceremonies with which the church on certain festive occasions surrounds the celebration of the Holy Sacrifice. "It was the most beautiful service I ever attended. A wonderful peace came over me; I feel that I will never complain again of the little annoyances of daily life.” "I felt that heaven smiled down upon us as the Archbishop assured us that we, too, can help to save souls by bearing our sufferings with patience and resignation." "A feeling of awe and reverence came upon me as I entered the church. The very atmosphere seemed filled with heavenly graces which Our Lord was pouring out upon all who were present." "It is thirty-two years since I have been to Mass. I never dreamed that a feast like this would be in store for me. How good God is!" "Oh, it is heavenly," whispered a young man of thirty-six years, as he fingered the beads on his rosary caressingly. "It is seven years since I have been to Mass. I used to be an altar boy the sight of them and the innocent little boys and girls brought

tears to my eyes and a lump into my throat. It was too touching for words. How Christlike to have done all this for us!" "The nurses and the doctors were wonderful, and so concerned about our comfort. Their very presence brought a security to me." sense of "Your Excellency, this is the first time I ever had a glimpse of you. I am happy beyond words." Smilingly and reverently a lady of some sixty years looked up at the Archbishop who most graciously returned the smile. "The fact that an Archbishop and a Bishop stood next to me, actually shook hands with me, and spoke to me, is a grace and blessing in itself, to say nothing of the many spiritual graces which I am convinced were given to me this morning." 

"It was indeed a 'Day of Days'- we must have a similar service more often than once in ten years. The sick and the infirm need such a spiritual feast." 

new "The moment I entered the church, I braced right upstrength and courage came into my soul. I learned to appreciate more truly all that the Apostolate means to us who are afflicted." "Today was the climax of the many blessings which have come to us through the Apostolate. What a privilege to be so close to 

Our Lord and to the actual successors of His Apostles!"

CHAPTER XVIII LIFT UP YOUR HEARTS 

"May the Lord multiply you, and make you abound in charity, one towards another, and towards all men as we do also towards you." 

-1 Thess. 3. 12. 

Our Apostolate has been most fortunate in having merited the approval of the hierarchy and the clergy from its very beginning. Throughout the years many fine letters were received that brought joy to our hearts. We have selected a paragraph or two from some of the many letters received from them, on various occasions and for our Tenth Anniversary, in praise of the service rendered to the sick and afflicted members of Christ's flock. 

The Hierarchy 

The first place in our hearts is reserved for the words of encouragement received from the representative of the Holy Father, The Most Reverend Amleto G. 

Cicognani, D.D., the Apostolic Delegate. 

243

Apostolic Delegation United States of America 1811 Biltmore Street 

Washington, D. C. 

Most Rev. A. J. Muench, Fargo, N. Dakota.

Your Excellency: 

June 14, 1938.

I rejoice that you are commemorating the first period of the activity of the "Apostolate of Suffering." Your pious union is indeed a true bond of prayer, of comfort, and of hope. Those who belong to it know that Our Divine Master in the sermon on the mount said most solemnly: "Blessed are the mourners, for they shall be comforted" (Math. V. 5).

How great is the difference between those who believe this truth and those who have no hope, that is to say, those who judge suffering only by the senses and by the brief span of mortal life. 

For these latter, suffering, like all evil, becomes an incomprehensible enigma, for truly the senses obscure and hinder our understanding, and the road of life is beset with its shadows, its nights, its terrors, just as every long journey has its perils. 

But we look beyond. For beyond these temporary vicissitudes, beyond these present sufferings there is a world of light and immortality. Jesus Christ, He Who was the "Man of Sorrows," 

Who died upon the cross for the love He bears us, invites us to fix our eyes upon that future goal which is eternity. Gazing upon that, the Apostle, St. Paul, beheld our true destiny — to be one day united with Christ in the kingdom of Joy without end- and he tells us: "We suffer with Him that we may be also glorified with Him. For I behold that the sufferings of the present time bear no comparison to the glory that is to be revealed in us" 

(Rom. VIII. 17, 1). 

To every one of us comes his hour of suffering, his turn at Calvary; incorporated into the mystical body of Christ, let us accept suffering as a sacrifice of adoration and expiation both for ourselves and for our brethren.

How precious is this time upon which depends our one immense happiness. God would never have permitted evil and

suffering were it not that in His wondrous providence, omnipotence, and goodness, He knows how to bring good out of evil. 

Mere human philosophy has never known how to give adequate comfort. Only Christian patience and fortitude illumine the mind and heart in the hour of suffering by instilling confidence in God and by strengthening virtue for a better life. 

May the Lord bless the "Apostolate of Suffering" and each and all who take part in it either as sufferers or as consolers. 

With sentiments of highest regard and in union of prayers, I remain 

Sincerely yours in Christ,

A. G. CICOGNANI, 

Archbishop of Lasdicea, Apostolic Delegate. 

The second place in our hearts is reserved for the encouraging words of our own beloved Archbishop, the 

Most Reverend S. A. Stritch, D.D., here in Milwaukee,

Wisconsin, the birthplace and home of the Apostolate. 

Dear Miss Tiry:

Archbishop's House 

Milwaukee, Wisconsin 

April 16, 1938. 

You tell me in your letter of the 31st of March that you are writing a History of the First Decade of the Apostolate of Suffer ing. 

May I add just a few words to your manuscript? Seven years ago I came to Milwaukee, and you will recall that I was impatient to learn more of the Apostolate, which I knew only in a vague way. For some years previous I had come in touch with it and realized the blessed work which it was doing. Still I had not studied its work and aims, and on coming to Milwaukee I wanted to know more about it. Bishop Muench, then the Rector of my Seminary, gave me a full account of it and with him, you will recall, I paid you a visit. From that moment I came to realize the great work which you are doing and never through these

years have I failed to give you encouragement and such poor recommendations that were in my power.

These days we are near to Calvary and somehow understand better the true value of suffering. Does not the seed corrupt and grow ugly before there is the sprout? Is not the condition of life travail? Is there any good thing which is not born of suffering?

These thoughts tell me how consonant Calvary is with nature. You know that I feel that the suffering are the most active people in Holy Church. Recently the Holy Father was stricken with a malady from which he has recovered happily to the amazement of all. During these days of intense pain his thoughts were constantly with the suffering. Never in his life had he been sick. It was a new experience for him. Yet he knew full well the Apostolate which only the suffering can exercise. Somehow Calvary is the real solution. It may be hard and it may be dismaying. Did you not hear Christ's Prayer in Gethsemani, “Father, if it be possible let this chalice be taken away." Still the wondrous thing is that He added: "Not Mine, but Thy will be done." In springtime the planter sows his seed, it corrupts, the winds and the sun come and there is life and verdure. Mothers loving and pure go down into the shadow of death and a child is born. Christ, Our Savior, died in shame and ignominy and there came an Easter. 

Your associates in the Apostolate of Suffering are really very active members of Holy Church. Theirs is a Catholic Action beyond any words of description. Ask them to give to the Church the full benefits of their sufferings? I know that there are those who suffer and complain even against God. They know not their opportunities. All of us must suffer and blessed are they who know how to coin from sufferings the gold of eternity. 

I approve your Apostolate and am anxious to do all in my power to extend it and increase its beneficient works. 

With a blessing,

SAMUEL A. STRITCH, Archbishop of Milwaukee.

I shall be grateful to you if somehow you will find a way of expressing to the members of the Apostolate my deep appreciation of the work they are doing, and of conveying to them my word of encouragement and my insistence that the work must go on and must grow in this vale of tears.

I wish that I could do more than I am doing to encourage your work among the sick in the Apostolate of Suffering. It is a corporal work of mercy that must be very dear to the Heart of Jesus That knew so much suffering and That can, therefore, beat in sympathy with the sick as no other heart can. They who suffer patiently and they who work to alleviate suffering and to spiritualize it will find the Sacred Heart of Jesus to be for them a treasury of richest graces and blessings. That is why I should like to share in your work if it were at all possible.

Bishop's House 

Savannah, Georgia

It is a pleasure to commend the pious work of the Apostolate of Suffering, and to express the hope that this very touching form of Catholic Charity may enlist the enthusiastic support of an ever-widening circle of friends. When one is in possession of health and strength, little does he heed, as a rule, the sad lot of those who are confined to their beds for a long period of time

because of sickness or infirmity. One has to go through the weariness, monotony, and lonesomeness, not to speak of the physical sufferings, of the sick and infirm, in order to appreciate what it means to have others thinking of them, working for them, and praying for them. Such is the mission of the Apostolate of Suffering. Your Pious Union brings to the sickroom much of the sweetness and gentleness of the Divine Friend of the sick, Our Blessed Lord.

Bishop of Seattle. 

St. Mary's Convent 

Milwaukee, Wisconsin

The Apostolate of Suffering has been rightly named. In the Old and in the New Testament suffering was one of the signs of the Elect. It always has distinguished the Church. Christ was essentially the Man of Suffering. The Church must hold this characteristic in herself and in her members, in a special manner in those members that are so privileged by God. St. Paul points to the wonderful signs wrought by the Holy Ghost in the Church, and the offices assigned to the various members, but he admonishes the Christians to "be zealous for the better gifts." 

Among these better gifts we may place without danger of going astray, the gift of suffering. Suffering was a mark of Christ, it is a mark of the true Church, and it is a mark of the true Christian.

In suffering, provided that we suffer with patience, we make manifest the works of God, we show forth the power of God over nature. God has at all times distinguished the Church through miracles; not an insignificant miracle, but one of the greatest miracles is Joy in Suffering. This is a true Apostolate. 

A. F. SCHINNER,

Titular Bishop of Sala. 

Bishop's Residence 

Wheeling, West Virginia

Works of benevolence and charity have ever grown from the very bosom of the Catholic Church as naturally and as necessarily as the leaves, branches, and fruit grow from the living tree. Anything, therefore, that gives aid and comfort to the sick and the afflicted is in harmony with the Christian spirit and tradition. It is well to impress upon the sick and afflicted the merit they may obtain in suffering in union with Christ, and to remind them that it is their privilege to be called upon to make up what is “wanting of the sufferings of Christ" (Col. LV, 24). May God then prosper your work and bless it most abundantly. 

JOHN J. SWINT, 

St. Francis Hospital 

Wichita, Kansas 

Bishop of Wheeling.

I have always felt that the organization so beautifully named "The Apostolate of Suffering" has such an immense field for propaganda, because of the very large number of people who suffer from one cause or another. It shall be a pleasure for me to plead for your Apostolate in sacrifice and in prayer. 

J. HENRY TIHEN, 

Titular Bishop of Bosana.

Bishop's House 

Bismarck, North Dakota

I am highly pleased that the Tenth Anniversary of the Apostolate of Suffering is solemnly celebrated by a Divine Service. 

This will help to bring before our Catholic people a real understanding of this Apostolate. We must all be followers of Christ by doing all our duties in union with Him. He saved the world mostly by His sufferings and death; therefore Christians who are led by Jesus Christ to live a life of suffering should be considered as highly honored and privileged to bring to their families many special graces and blessings, provided they bear their sufferings patiently. Every Catholic family that has one or more such sufferers, should see in them Christ's image and realize that whatever services are done to them are accepted by Christ as done to Himself. 

VINCENT Wehrle, O.S.B., Bishop of Bismarck.

Collegeville, Minnesota

I am very much interested in the Apostolate. To me it seems it was a most happy inspiration, coming no doubt from the Lord, to bring home to those who are sick and suffering the thought that they can, by patient conformity with God's will, not only render their own affliction more endurable, but can make it a source of blessing also for their fellowmen and for the welfare of the Church in general. I pray God to bless the Apostolate so that it may grow and ever be productive of more fruit of grace and sanctity.

ALCUIN DEUTSCH, O.S.B., Our Lady of Gethsemani Abbey 

Trappist P. O., Kentucky Abbot.

It seems to me that the Apostolate of Suffering is most appropriate for the needs of the present day. The spirit of the world has but one end in view in everything and that is an excessive desire to avoid suffering, whether it be the heat in summer, or the cold in winter, or anything else, going even so far at times, as to try to shun it by means of suicide. By trying to teach the sick, invalids, and all poor sufferers, the dignity, the excellence, and the merit of suffering, with Christ and for Christ, you are co-operating in a most efficacious way in the uplift of poor humanity. 

FREDERICK M. DUNNE, O.C.S.O., Abbot.

Richardton, North Dakota

The Apostolate of Suffering is a noble one, for the sick and the suffering, as St. Paul says, "fill up those things that are wanting of the sufferings of Christ, in their flesh, for His body, which is the Church." Sufferings patiently borne are a wonderful means "for the edifying (i.e. the building up) of the body of Christ unto a perfect man, unto the measure of the age of the fulness of Christ."

CUTHBERT GOEB, O.S.B., 

St. Norbert Abbey

West De Pere, Wisconsin 

Abbot.

Your Pious Union is one of the most beautiful works of charity I ever heard of. It reaches those who are most in need of sympathy, namely, the sick and the suffering, who too often are forgotten even by so-called friends, and are left alone in their misery. For them to know that some sympathetic souls are interested in them even at a great distance, is surely a great comfort. May the Apostolate of Suffering prosper and bring consolation where mostly needed. 

B. H. PENNINGS, Ord. Praem., United States Marine Hospital 

Carville, Louisiana Abbot.

As I am at close range of many who suffer in mind and in body with little hope of future relief I can appreciate your Apostolate. They need strength of faith and unlimited graces of courage and patience. If they correspond with these graces they make giant strides on the way to God. But if they are weak in faith or have lost faith altogether they are apt to defy Divine Providence and all the world, and become moral wrecks a thousand times worse than their bodily ills. Hence, let the members of the 

Apostolate offer special prayers and acts of mortification for the unfortunates who suffer without hope and without profit. 

PAUL SCHAEUBLE, O.S.B., Abbot.

2 

Encouragement from the Clergy 

The priests of God, in imitation of their Divine Master, have the spiritual welfare of the sick at heart. Very often they are not able to spend as much time in the sickroom of their patients as they desire, duc to other pressing work and duties. Therefore they have at all times recognized the value of the Apostolate and the influence it exerted over the sick. In the absence of the priest, and in the intervals between his visits, the Apostolate takes his place and through "Our Good Samaritan" consoles and encourages them, ever leading them on to "seek the things that are above." The interest that the priests have taken in the Apostolate, and the prayers they have offered for its success as well as for its members have been at all times an incentive spurring us on to greater things, and a staff of support to the sick who wearily climb the steep and stony road that leads to Calvary. 

The Cross of Suffering 

For the Tenth Anniversary of the Apostolate of Suffering, I extend to the originators and the very soul of the Apostolate, as also to my fellow-members, my heartiest congratulations. I beg to assure you and them of my continuous prayers on behalf of one of the noblest works that was ever undertaken in the name of Christian charity. It supports the souls who carry a heavy cross of suffering, and it comforts them while encouraging them to walk patiently and cheerfully in the footsteps of Jesus up the steep way of Calvary, even to the very summit where Christ made the supreme sacrifice for our salvation, becoming obedient to the death of the Cross.

You are co-operating with Christ for the spread of His Kingdom on earth, and in promoting the interests of His Sacred

Heart. One of the finest things that the Apostolate of Suffering is doing is the encouragement it is constantly giving to those who are suffering to practice conformity, or rather perfect abandonment to the will of God. I pray God to bless you and all the members of the Apostolate. May we all meet in heaven where in 

Our Father's House there shall be no more tears but peace and joy, joy for evermore. —Rev. F. X. Lasance, Ohio.

The Heights of Sanctity 

What Apostolate could be dearer to the Heart of Christ than the Apostolate of Suffering? God will bless this work for it is based on the interior life and built on a foundation of solid spirituality. It will lead victims of painful afflictions to heights of sanctity, and will help souls who are in despair to find peace in abandonment to God's holy will. "Our Good Samaritan" will bring souls closer to Christ because each article is an appeal for personal holiness. I will recite the Divine Office for the members every Friday and Saturday, and will also remember them daily at Holy Mass as well as in the weekly hour of adoration. I shall do all in my power to make known this noble work which is dear to my heart. 

Rev. J. H. R., Minn. 

Suffering for the Missions

The Apostolate of Suffering is doing a great work for God's 

Kingdom, and I assure you that I am greatly interested in your

Pious Union. I shall be glad to give a memento at daily Holy 

Mass for the living and deceased members, and I shall recite the Divine Office for them each Friday. In return I ask their prayers and sufferings for this mission which is more in need of prayer than anything else. God's grace alone can conquer the many obstacles that beset us. - Rev. W. R., New Guinea. 

A Ray of Sunshine "Our Good Samaritan" is indeed a ray of sunshine for the sick. The balm which is poured forth from its pages will bring peace and happiness to many afflicted souls who all alone are trying to serve God as well as they can. May this good work bring solace to the sick in all parts of our own United States as

well as of distant shores. Daily my prayers will be that this work which is so dear to the Heart of Jesus may expand and flourish. - Rev. T. L., Mich. 

God's Chosen Children

I have followed with interest the developments of the "Apostolate of Suffering" and have often made it part of my memento at Holy Mass. May God continue to shower His blessings upon 

His chosen children who are called to unite their sufferings with those of Jesus, in order to atone for the sins of the world and to merit grace in abundance for themselves and others. The true value of the "Apostolate of Suffering" we shall see and admire fully only in the light of eternity. -Rev. B., O.M.Cap., Ind.

The Importance of Suffering 

The "Apostolate of Suffering" appeals to me very strongly. The tremendous importance and necessity of suffering in the development of Christ's Kingdom needs to be stressed. The older I get, the less reliance I place upon learning, eloquence, and natural gifts. I am convinced that the cause of souls is promoted through suffering. It is the lesson that our Lord preached so frequently and which he tried especially to impress upon the disciples on the way to Emmaus. May God bless the Apostolate most abundantly! - Rev. J. R., S.J., III.

The Power of Example 

In my mind the Apostolate is something providential and I see for it a great future. It is going to bring to the front a chapter of Christianity that until now was indeed acted, but always within four walls. No doubt all priests can cite from experience cases of souls that went through a veritable martyrdom of slight but slow and lasting pains suffered with the noblest of intentions. But these souls were always alone. The Apostolate brings them together and the example of one will encourage the other. I myself am among those whom I choose to call fortunate souls. I have not been sick enough to die and not well enough to do my work with energy and pleasure. So I am glad to ally myself with those who are thus favored by our good God. - Rev. A. B., Pa.

A Great Need 

When I read of the Apostolate I said: "Thank God for that; it is greatly needed!" I wish to be an apostle also. Let us be cheerful in the Lord! In my daily Mass I shall remember all the members of the Apostolate. - Rev. E., O.M.Cap., Kansas. 

A Message of Cheer

Every word, every line of “Our Good Samaritan" is a ray of hope, a message of cheer to some sad and suffering soul. If we priests could only bring the thousands of helpless invalids to realize the vast spiritual power which lies dormant within them, what an enthusiastic army of workers we would have to back our missionary endeavors! - Rev. L. C., O.F.M., Ill. 

True Christ Bearers

To be a member of the Apostolate is an incentive to be more than ever mindful of those chosen souls who in the School of Suffering are the true Christ bearers, instrumental in saving many souls redeemed by the Precious Blood of Christ. May this noble Apostolate ever lead those whom the hand of the Lord has touched to a high degree of virtue and add bright jewels of merit to the crown that awaits the followers of the Crucified Savior. -Rev. E. M. W., C.SS.R., N. Y. 3 

Voices from the Sickroom

Perhaps no better proof for the need of an Apostolate of Suffering can be found than the words coming directly from the members. Countless are the letters received at the office - each one expressing the joy and happiness, the peace and contentment, the consolation, and the resignation that have come to the writers through their affiliation with this Pious Union of the Sick. There is real inspiration in the excerpts we have selected from letters

received. May they help the readers to "Lift up their Hearts."

Her Heart's Desire 

It was in "The Savior's Call" of March, 1927, that I read about "The Apostolate of Suffering" for the first time. This was an answer to a long-felt desire and need to offer my sufferings in union with other afflicted persons. I enrolled at once, and have been faithful in reciting the daily prayer of the Apostolate every morning.

At that time I was a helpless invalid, unable to walk a step or to use my fingers with the exception of the thumb and index finger of my left hand. It affords me much happiness to say that now I am able to walk slowly, to help myself in many ways, to do a little housework and even to attend Holy Mass occasionally. 

I thank God and all, who have prayed for me. 

Every issue of "Our Good Samaritan" was read with pleasure and profit. I have striven to make the Apostolate known to every sufferer with whom I came in contact. May God bless the Apostolate! 

Miss M. S., Conn.

Joy and Consolation 

I wish to offer my heartiest congratulations to "The Apostolate of Suffering" on their Tenth Anniversary. I have been a member for six years, and I hope to remain one as long as I live. It has given me much comfort, and helped me to be more patient and resigned. It has offered me that sympathy needed by all who are shut-in and who have experienced how cruelly cold the world can be at times. I find true joy and abundant consolation in offering my little bit of suffering each day for the Holy Father's missionary intentions. The thought of the missions gives me strength to bear it, in order to help the brave priests and courageous Sisters and brothers who are leading such lives of selfsacrifice to help save souls for Christ. 

Miss J. L., Conn.

Beneficial Suffering 

My membership in the Apostolate has been a source of solace and help in my illness. Coupled with this is the comforting

thought that our sufferings are not wasted but put to work and made beneficial not only for ourselves but for all mankind. - Miss M. M., Minn.

Immeasurable Comfort 

The comfort which the Apostolate of Suffering and “Our Good Samaritan" bring to me is immeasurable. I look forward to each issue with eagerness, for each copy brings consolation and inspiration; it gives me new zest to continue my efforts and accept 

God's Holy Will. May God bless our beloved Spiritual Director and our devoted secretary. 

Sr. St. T., N. D.

Better Citizens 

Through the kindness of our parish priest I learned of the Apostolate. I know of no better way to start the New Year than by becoming a member in this Pious Union and joining other invalids in the spiritual happiness obtainable in this noble organization. God has a purpose in all He does and permits. I had planned on a journalistic career, however, it may be that I can serve God and others best in bed rather than in any way I had outlined for myself. This is only one of the many ways He has of making us finer people, better citizens, and more worthy heirs of the much desired eternal kingdom of heaven.

Not Forgotten 

- Miss E. W., Minn.

The Apostolate of Suffering and "Our Good Samaritan" are rightly named. Both have been a source of much consolation and comfort to me. Years ago I thought that shut-ins and sick people were forgotten by the world at large, but since this little "Visitor" came into life I realize that this is not the case. The many Holy 

Masses and prayers offered for us who are sick and shut-in tell us that we are well remembered. Our leaders, the Spiritual Director and the secretary, are doing a noble work for the sick and deserve God's richest blessings. I join my prayers with all the members in thanksgiving for all the graces and blessings which have come to the Apostolate and to us, the members. - Miss L. P., Wis. 

---

God's Providence

I am very grateful to God for having so providentially introduced me to the Apostolate of Suffering. I appreciate being a member and sharing my suffering with the other members. 

Although not an invalid, I suffer continually. "Our Good Samaritan" is worthy of its name, for it brings sunshine, comfort, and peace to afflicted hearts. I congratulate the Apostolate on the sublime work it has undertaken for the greater glory of God. 

May the dear Lord bless all who are connected with this noble work. 

Sr. M. B., Mo. 

A Blessing in Disguise 

It is beyond mere words to describe the consolation and joy which eight year's of membership in The Apostolate of Suffering have brought into my life. I welcome most cheerfully "Our Good

Samaritan," and take a keen interest in reading the instructive articles concerning our faith. I rejoice to see the successful progress made by the Apostolate in this space of time. I frankly admit that many disappointments and humiliations accompany my affliction, but since becoming a member, and reading "Our Good Samaritan" and "Comfort for the Sick" I have become reconciled to my suffering. I realize that my affliction is truly a blessing in disguise. - Miss F. K., N. Y. 

Peace to Troubled Souls 

I owe my membership in the Apostolate of Suffering to a saintly religious. Prayer purified by suffering must be very acceptable to God. I have to lie in one position all the time; I have not been able to move for two years, and suffer most acute pain nearly all the time. However, God has given me a priceless gift in my privations, for very rarely do I become blue or discouraged in my sufferings. May God grant continued success to your enterprise of bringing patience in suffering and peace to troubled souls. 

Miss E. A. H., Can. 

Splinters of the Cross 

I am really proud to be numbered among the blessed members, the "Passion Flowers" of Our Lord, and participating in so many

graces and spiritual blessings. We all know that sufferings are splinters of the Cross of Christ, and are given only to the best friends of God. May God bless all those who were instrumental in giving us this heavenly anchor. - Sr. M. C., Panama. 

Courage and Strength

It was through our devoted secretary's book "Comfort for the Sick" that I first became acquainted with the Apostolate of 

Suffering. The inspiring contents of "Our Good Samaritan" together with the many spiritual benefits derived from membership in this Pious Union have given me the courage and strength to persevere. I hope that every member has received as many graces and blessings through it as have come to me. Our "Spiritual 

Powerhouse" has also been an immense help to me. Very often do I knock at its little door for prayers and assistance in my manifold needs. May God bless our beloved Spiritual Director and our secretary with health and strength to continue their noble work for the afflicted. 

Miss M. B., Ill. 

Co-workers with Our Savior 

I consider it a great grace to belong to The Apostolate of Suffering. The little magazine "Our Good Samaritan" is as welcome to me as the early, fresh, scented flowers of Spring. Every item it contains is a ray of sunshine. How I wish that all who are suffering in this big world would belong to the Apostolate. 

How it would sweeten their hours of suffering, and sanctify their lives. To be a sufferer means to be a privileged chosen child of 

God. What a high ideal our association holds out to us - to become co-workers with our Savior in the great work of redemption by the offering of our daily sacrifices and sufferings. May we henceforth joyfully carry our little crosses, begging the Heart of Jesus to give us at least one soul each day—three hundred and sixty-five a year thus helping to fill the mansions of our 

Father's home in heaven. 

Sr. M. G., Wis. 

Flowers for the King 

I am very happy in belonging to so noble an association as the Apostolate. Therein, we, our Lord's chosen sufferers can in union

with each other return Our Lord's love by offering our afflictions to Him Who suffered for our salvation. My greatest trial in affliction was to see others go out and enjoy themselves while I had to remain at home. I have, however, overcome my impatience by meditating on the fact that others suffer the same trial. Each trial and pain I consider now as a flower in the gorgeous bouquet that we, God's chosen children, can offer to Our King. 

Life Seemed Useless - Miss H. D., Wis.

Up to the time that a religious, a very dear friend of mine, enrolled me in the Apostolate, I did not know that a society solely for the sick was in existence. During the past years I offered my suffering and inactivity to God, uniting them with those of His divine Son. My life seemed useless, however, whereas now, the very intentions for which my sufferings are offered make me feel that I am doing something; it seems to change the whole aspect of life. In the short time that I have been a member I derived much spiritual consolation from my enrollment. A few times when I was very tired, I lost heart for a few moments; then, the words of the morning offering "I accept from Thy loving hands, all the sufferings Thou dost send to me," came before me, and what a difference this made. Daily I ask God and

Our blessed Lady to bless materially and spiritually the Apostolate and everyone connected with it. Miss M. F., Nova Scotia. 

A Precious Cross 

My membership in the Apostolate renewed my courage and gave me strength to carry my cross. It enables me to look on the bright side of my illness, and even though I do become a bit impatient at times, I know that I need only take "Our Good Samaritan" and read it over in order to resign myself more completely to the Will of God. The greatest sacrifice God asked of me in my illness was the inability to enter the religious life. 

However, I hope and continue to pray that my dream of childhood days may yet come true. Even though this is a cross for me to bear, it is a very sweet and precious one, and if God wills that my hopes should never be realized, my cross shall grow even

more precious to me. I will be happy because I am privileged to assist our dear Savior to carry His burden. - Miss L. M. L., Wis. 

Happiness in Solitude 

The Apostolate of Suffering has helped me to find peace and joy, contentment and happiness in my suffering and solitude. In one of the issues of "Our Good Samaritan" a suggestion was made to say a prayer every time the clock strikes. This has been a valuable aid to me. Every time the hour and half hour is told by the clock I say my special prayers; I find that the time passes very rapidly. My hardest trial is that I am unable to attend Holy 

Mass, go to church, or to receive Holy Communion more frequently. God has given me the grace to say "Thy Will be done."

I have found that true happiness consists in complete abandonment to the holy will of God. 

Miss I. H., Iowa. 

Resignation Made Less Difficult

The hardest trial of my illness is trying to be resigned to the Holy Will of God, and to bear my sufferings patiently. I have not been able to overcome this fault entirely, but it has been made less difficult through my membership in the Apostolate of Suffering. It is a very great privilege to belong to this praiseworthy union, and it is a joy and a consolation to share in so many prayers and Holy Masses. When I read "Our Good Samaritan" 

I realize more fully how good the Lord is to me. May God bless the Apostolate! 

Miss E. K., Nebraska.

The Spirit of Gratitude 

My membership in the Apostolate has helped me to realize how much I have to be thankful for. I would not dare to complain when I see and hear how severe are the sufferings of other persons. 

It will be five years that I became a member. I try to be faithful in saying the morning offering. It is thirteen years since I had my breakdown in health; at first I was able to go to church, but now our dear Lord comes to me. I am thankful and contented that I am able to be up and around. I wait on myself and on another invalid, so I feel that I am of a little use in the world. -Miss L. N., New Hampshire.

Part of God's Plan 

I wish you could see the sick Sisters when “Our Good Samaritan" arrives. They read it many times with a relish that would make your heart happy. It becomes a part of them, it makes them forget themselves by living with beautiful thoughts. It has brought them the realization of their worth to the community and the world, of the privilege that the good God, their Spouse, has chosen them to love and suffer for His interests, and that the loneliness all sick must feel at one time or another is only a part of 

God's great plan to draw them closer to Himself. Resignation is what He desires, and a generous spirit in giving everything what He wants; refusing nothing will bring abundant blessings down upon the community and the world until there will be but one fold and one shepherd. May the God of our hearts bless your work by generously spreading it further and further throughout the world, thus consoling the sick and peopling heaven with souls. 

It is true our dear Lord has no need of our work, but He does need and want our love and in what more beneficial way could we prove it than by being apostles through love and suffering. 

Sr. M. A., Ky.

The Power of Prayer 

I was enrolled in the Apostolate of Suffering at a time when I was suffering from a serious physical breakdown and a severe spiritual trial. I passed through a trial that found me bordering on the verge of despair, and I was physically too weak to fight. 

Thanks to the many good works and powerful prayers of the

Apostolate, the members, and the priests, I found God again and have even learned to say "Thy Will be done." 

Living Crucifixes - Miss M. M. C., N. Y. 

My membership in the Apostolate of Suffering has helped me to perform all my duties with exquisite exactness in patience and cheerfulness. I am ever mindful of the beautiful title “Living

Crucifixes" bestowed upon us who are members of the Apostolate. 

The grace flowing from the crucifix carries with it the strength that sustains me every day of my life. - Mrs. H. R. G., Wis.

Wasted Suffering

The Apostolate of Suffering has rendered a message severely needed in this topsy-turvy world right at this present moment. 

It is really appalling to contemplate the immense volume of wasted suffering thrown to the four winds for want of a proper knowledge and instruction as to its minted worth. This wealth of suffering is in need of revaluation, so that it may become in the spiritual realm a standard of medium of exchange in spiritual values. Through the Apostolate of Suffering there is conferred on suffering humanity a distinct benefit. - Dr. F. S. P., Pa. 

A Mutual Bond 

I have been sick for seven years, flat on my back in bed without moving at all for five years. Even now I am only twenty-five years of age. It is a hard battle at times, but I am happy and thankful to say that my religious home and school training have fitted me in some measure to accept my cross and with God's help to carry it for love of Him. I realize that there is some purpose behind it all, and though I can not understand it, yet I am confident that it is all for the best.

Very deeply do I appreciate being enrolled in the Apostolate of Suffering. Membership therein will be a constant joy. Every word in the little paper is so helpful and encouraging. The feeling of a mutual bond between the other members and myself is an inspiration to play the role of a "good sport" for God, Who 

I feel must grow weary of our continual "Why and Wherefore," when we have to face life so differently than we had planned. 

There are ever so many other blessings for which I am grateful and in which I am made content. Meanwhile I face the present job in a manner pleasing to God, suffer cheerfully, and offer my suffering to Him for my own and other souls' salvation. The sooner we learn to do this the easier it becomes to suffer. I hope to follow faithfully the aim of the Apostolate. -Miss G. S., New Jersey.

Converted Through Suffering 

It was through being a shut-in for almost nineteen years that the Lord led me to the Catholic Church into which I will soon be

received. If it is God's will for me to suffer, I shall bear it patiently. I am happy to be a member of the Apostolate. 

Miss L. H., Texas. 

An Anchor of Hope "Our Good Samaritan" has been an anchor of hope to me.

Called away from the firing line to the relief corps and not even to be one of the corps but to be one of the poor ones they are to relieve, I now realize that the Good Master has given me another vocation the vocation of Suffering. How well the Apostolate teaches this to us! - Mr. C. A., Honolulu 

The Holy Father's Blessing

I was filled with joy to know that we poor sufferers have received the blessing of the Holy Father and the plenary indulgence at the hour of death. May “Our Good Samaritan" find its way into all the homes where there are invalids and shut-ins to console. – Mrs. C. H., S. Dakota.

The Best Way 

The first article that I read about the Apostolate of Suffering was so sweet, so full of comfort that it brought happy tears to my eyes. For two years I have been almost a helpless invalid. I realize now that my illness has been for my good, and the fact comes home to me that without being aware of it I have been serving 

God in the best possible way. – Mrs. R. A., W. Va.

Enjoying the Library 

I can not tell you what joy I am deriving from drawing books from the "Little Flower Library." Receiving packages of books by mail brings much happiness to the heart of a shut-in. It gives me added pleasure in knowing that many other sick members of our Apostolate are having this same joy come into their shut-in lives. 

Miss M. B., Pa. 

A Bishop's Kind Deed 

My affiliation with this Pious Union of the Sick has been such a comfort to me during the past years. I can not express my grati-

tude sufficiently to our good Bishop for having made it known to me. I pray for the members and their intentions every day. 

Sr. M. of J., La.

A Comforting Angel 

The Apostolate of Suffering sends its little paper “Our Good 

Samaritan" as a comforting angel on the pilgrimage of life! This work undertaken for the sick is great in the sight of God, and He will reward the deed a hundred-fold. May He bless your every effort and give you strength when the road is rough. 

Consecrated Hours of Pain 

Miss A. V., Cuba. 

The dear invalid Sisters in our infirmary appreciate the enrollment in the Apostolate of Suffering. They derive much consolation from the suggestions contained in “Our Good Samaritan.” 

Since suffering is so very precious to our Savior, and so efficacious in its silent influence, your efforts to gain many to consecrate their hours of pain and affliction will be most pleasing to the Heart of Christ. May God's blessing enable you to extend the Apostolate and thus make the sickroom become a place of grace and intercession. 

Mother M. I., Iowa. 

Proud of Patroness 

I am very proud of our patron saint, St. Lidwina. I also was born and raised in the city of Schiedam, Holland, the birthplace of St. Lidwina. I derive a wealth of consolation from "Our Good 

Samaritan." I am enclosing a little donation in honor of our patroness. — Mr. J. S., Ill. 

United Sufferings

How happy I am when I realize that I belong to this Pious 

Union of the Sick. I find "Our Good Samaritan" so comforting. 

You will never know what it means to me, for my mother died four months ago and I was unable to go to her because of my ill health. I am happy, however, to think that I can offer this sacrifice to God, along with my other trials. Sometimes life seems and

is so lonely, but to know that we can unite our prayers and sufferings with others for our spiritual good is very consoling. 

Miss L. B., New Mexico.

Light in Darkness 

The Pious Union of "The Apostolate of Suffering" and its little paper are wonderful! Each issue contains words of comfort which reach deep down into the corners of the heart, brightening the darkness and lightening the load. Mrs. L. J. L., Canal Zone. 

Sewing for the Altar

It has been my pleasure to make some altar linens for God's service. I am sending them to you. Although I am still a shut-in,

I am resigned and happy to be a member of so wonderful a Union of the Sick, which has so many wonderful spiritual benefits accruing to its members. I enjoy "Our Good Samaritan” and read it over many times. Faithfully do I recite the daily offering. May our dear Lord grant you and your assistants many years to carry on your wonderful work for His sick children.

Our Lord's Own Way 

Mrs. E. B. F., Mass. 

Truly, I did not fully appreciate my membership in the Apostolate of Suffering until I failed to receive "Our Good Samaritan.”

It must have been another of Our Lord's Own ways of showing me the advantages of such a wonderful Union of the Sick. I hope to be a member always, for though my health may return, my deformity will leave me eligible. - Miss M. E. H., Ind. 

Our Father's Business 

It is beyond my power to tell you what my membership in the

Apostolate has meant to me during the past few years. I always wanted to lead an active life, and since joining the Apostolate I can feel that I am active in the most important thing in life, namely, in our "Father's Business" of saving souls. That is a very great consolation. Then, too, I feel that though I am a shut-in, I am really a member of a very large family which consists of all who are united by the bond of suffering. “Our Good Samaritan"

is a great consolation, for, when I have grown impatient under the cross, it has come to chide me and to make me grateful to the 

Lord for having given it to me. 

Miss A. C. B., Md. 

A Golden Sacrifice 

Two weeks ago I received the report that I am destined to be a chronic invalid. At first it was hard to accept the news cheerfully. 

After the shock was over, I weighed the statement in the light of the Apostolate of Suffering and therein found the cross easier to bear. My desire to become a religious has been shattered; I have drawn the conclusion that to be able to realize one's desire must be silver, but to lay this desire on the altar of sacrifice must be gold in the eyes of God Who reads all hearts. Miss G. D., Vt. 

A Veritable God-send 

There are so many things I would like to say in favor of the Apostolate, but I find it difficult to write because of my affliction. 

I read every word of "Our Good Samaritan" two and even three times. It is a wonderful God-send to the sick and the afflicted. I love to read the letters written by the members, for then I compare my own little sufferings with those great ones of other members. It has helped me to acquire the virtue of patience. It gives me joy to know that the Holy Sacrifice is offered so frequently for us. What a consolation this is to the sick and the shut-ins! May 

God bless every person connected with this work! 

The Value of Pain 

Mrs. E. S., Ohio.

My appreciation of membership in the Apostolate is deep and sincere. What peace and joy we experience when we accept the crosses God is pleased to send us, in a loving and gentle spirit and bear them with patient fortitude. God only knows to how many ouls the meaning of suffering is brought home through their membership in the Apostolate. It teaches the heavily burdened the value of pain borne with faith and love in union with the sufferings of Christ in His great work of Redemption. For, since Christ

can no longer suffer, we, the cross bearers and members of His Mystical Body can and shall gladly do our part with the assistance of God's all powerful grace. 

Sr. M. C., Colo.

Spiritual Building Material 

The intimate relationship which membership in the Apostolate affords me with others like myself means the world to me. Our life's work is to build "the ladder by which we rise, from the lowly earth to the vaulted skies," with hours of suffering-suffering instead of the good works wherewith we would prefer to build. Although our material is not admired by the world, I feel it is pleasing to our heavenly Father Who wishes us to use it according to His designs, and thereby reach our eternal home. 

A Mine of Inspiration 

Mrs. M. J., Calif. 

Permit me to assure you that I consider it the greatest privilege to belong to the Apostolate of Suffering. Every issue of “Our

Good Samaritan" is a mine of inspiration, and provides wholesome food for serious thought. I am greatly consoled by the fact that I am sharing in so many Holy Masses.- Miss M. R., Oreg. 

Marking the Calendar

After reading and becoming thoroughly acquainted with the contents of "Our Good Samaritan" I record in my almanac all the days of the Holy Masses, the special feasts called to our attention, the anniversaries of which we are reminded, and other important items. Then I have some one place my copy in our bookrack in church with an appended note explaining the aims and purpose of the Apostolate. In this way I hope to do my little share in spreading the knowledge of our beautiful and consoling Apostolate. 

Miss G. I., Okla.

The Road to Heaven 

It consoles me to know that I am not alone in my suffering and that through my membership in the Apostolate I am united to others. Thus we are helping each other to bear our illness with patience and love. For me, suffering is the only road to heaven

and therefore I am content to travel it. Every morning I unite with all the members in prayer, knowing that I am remembered by them in return. - Mrs. G. H., Wash.

SUPPLEMENT A Tribute of Gratitude 

"Blessed be the God and Father of Our Lord Jesus 

Christ, Who hath blessed us with spiritual blessings." 

- Eph. 1:3 

Anniversaries are times of thanksgiving. Heeding the injunction of St. Paul, “Be ye thankful," our first expression of gratitude goes forth to the Heart of the Divine Good Samaritan for the manifold graces He deigned to shower upon the Apostolate, its founders, its members, its helpers, and its benefactors during this decade of years. 

From a small and humble beginning in 1926 it has spread over the entire country and even beyond its borders; from a charter membership of sixty it has grown steadily to an active membership which is now more than five thousand. The mustard seed planted ten years ago has by the grace of God grown into a mighty tree under whose branches the afflicted find refuge and shelter from the burdens of the day and the heat of the noon-day sun. 

Our heartfelt thanks go forth to our Holy Father, Pope

Pius XI, for his fatherly interest in our work, as well as for the many blessings and favors he bestowed upon us and our members.

We thank the hierarchy of our country and of other countries for the encouragement and commendation given us. 277

We thank the priests, the levites, the religious, and all who lavish the spiritual gifts of Holy Masses, the Divine Office, and other prayers and devotions upon our members.

We thank our kind benefactors, one and all, living and deceased, for their generosity which enabled us to carry on our work, and all who in some way, at some time, gave a helping hand to promote this noble cause. 

Finally, our thanks go forth to our beloved members, both living and deceased. Their expressions of gratitude and their assurance of having profited both physically and spiritually from their membership in the Apostolate was at all times a staff of support to us. In every work there are difficulties to be overcome, dark hours to be faced, and disappointments to be borne. Their letters of appreciation helped us to overcome the obstacles, to emerge from darkness to light, and to be fired with new zeal to labor more diligently for their spiritual welfare. 

To those who are still with us we say "God bless you" to those who have gone before us we whisper, "Rest in peace and pray for us."

Anniversaries are also times of hopeful expectations! 

God has been most generous to us in the past. May we not look forward with hope and confidence to receive even better and bigger things in the future? It is with these sentiments that we conclude the history of these ten years. We beg you to pray that we may find a way to bring the Apostolate with its uplifting message to many hearts who as yet are dwelling in the valley of desolation and despondency. Pray that we may be able to give solace to their saddened hearts, balm to their wounded spirits, and strength to their timid souls, so that they will grad-

Supplement 279 ually take their burden of pain and suffering to the foot of the Cross. Deposited there they will find refreshment in the Precious Blood of the Savior who died bleeding for us on Calvary. 

To Men, we offer heartfelt thanks, 

To God, — our adoration and praise forever.

Retrospection and Anticipation "This is the Lord's doing: and it is wonderful in our eyes." 

Ps. 117:23 

Ten years of service in behalf of the sick have been completed. A decade of years spent in scattering the seed of good words, suggestions, and comfort on the soil of suffering hearts. Ten years of sympathy with the sick and afflicted; ten years of trying to bring a little sunshine where there was spiritual darkness; of instilling resignation where there was a feeling of despondency; of bringing peace where all was turmoil; of planting hope where despair had taken root; of enthroning joy where bitterness had reigned; of offering understanding and friendship where the spirit of abandonment was uppermost in the hearts of those shut-in from the out-side world, cut off from intercourse with relations and friends. We hope that the efforts of those years have borne some fruit for time and above all for eternity. 

Another decade begins. May it bring us a greater measure of sympathy and understanding; a larger portion of strength, patience, and inspiration, that we may be "a lamp to the feet of the sick, and a light to their path"; a greater love for the afflicted to enable us to labor and not count the costs; increased strength to perform such deeds as will lighten the burden of the cross they carry. May the grace of God, manifested in so pronounced a way during the past decade, be with us in the future, 280

Retrospection and Anticipation 281 so that His strength may give power to each spoken and written word.

Another decade commences for our members. Within its span are enfolded the destinies of thousands of God's pets. Some of them, and some of us, if left alone might fall beneath the cross of pain and suffering, the instruments which God has chosen to carve our eternal destiny. 

But with the aid of sympathetic Simeons of Cyrene, of modern Veronicas, with the encouragement and above all the prayers of our companions, the new decade shall be for us who do the laboring and for the sick who do the praying, a decade blessed with God's choicest benedictions and filled with noble achievements that will bear fruit for all eternity.

Omnia Pro Te, Cor Jesu!

PRAYERS 

"My son, in thy sickness, neglect not thyself, but pray to the Lord, and He shall hear thee." -Ecclus. 38:9

Our Official Morning Offering 

O Almighty and merciful God, in a spirit of humble resignation, I accept from Thy loving hands, all the sufferings Thou dost send me to bear this day.

I offer them to Thee in union with the most bitter sufferings of Jesus during His sacred passion and on Calvary. 

In union with all the members, I offer all my sufferings to Thee for all the intentions of the Apostolate. 

Sacred Heart of Jesus, patient and rich in mercy, help us to be faithful unto death.

Our Lady, Health of the Sick, intercede for us. 

St. Lidwina, pray for us. 

Covenant with God

O My God, Lover of the sick and the afflicted, being cast down on my bed of pain, I can not pray as much as I desire. Accept each pain, each heartbeat, each tear, each sigh, as an act of love, of submission to Thy Holy Will, and of sorrow for my sins. My heart shall supply for what my lips can not do. Accept my good will for the deed and let my suffering be pleasing in Thy sight. Amen.

An Offering 

O God the Father, see, I stretch forth my hands to accept my sufferings as a gift from Thee, Thy Kingdom Come! 

O God the Son, see, I fold my hands to carry my sufferings as a treasure with Thee, Thy Kingdom Come! 282

Prayers 

283 O God the Holy Ghost, see, I raise my hands on high, to offer my sufferings as a holocaust through Thee, Thy Kingdom Come!

O most blessed Trinity, through Jesus, with Jesus, and in Jesus, may my sufferings render Thee all honor and glory. Amen. 

A Prayer for obtaining Indulgences 

To be said in the morning 

My Lord and my God, I humbly beseech Thee, grant unto me, in consideration of the merits of Our Lord Jesus Christ, all the indulgences attached to my prayers and works of this day. I desire to enter into the dispositions necessary to gain those indulgences, in order that I may satisfy Divine Justice and relieve the souls in 

Purgatory. Amen. -Taken from St. Andrew's Daily Missal.

An Hourly Prayer 

O Sweet Jesus, I commend to Thy Sacred Heart all that I have done and suffered during the past hours, that Thou mayest perfect it and offer it to the heavenly Father for His eternal praise and glory in union with all the Holy Masses of the past, present, and future. All that I shall think, say, do, or suffer shall be solely for love of Thee in union with Thy love and labor, with Thy passion and death, with the merits of Thy blessed Mother, St. 

Joseph and all the Saints.

O my Jesus, I believe in Thee, I hope in Thee, I love Thee with all my heart. For love of Thee, I repent of all the sins of my whole life. O Jesus, my heart longs for Thee, I wish to receive 

Thee. O sweet Jesus come to me; my body and soul I give to 

Thee. Amen. 

An Offering of Holy Mass 

O my God, as I can not this day enjoy the happiness of assisting at the Holy Sacrifice of the Mass, I transport myself in spirit to the foot of the altar; I unite with the Church, which by the hands of the priest offers Thee Thy adorable Son; I offer myself with 

Him, by Him, and in His name. I adore, I praise, and I thank

Thee, imploring Thy mercy, invoking Thy assistance and presenting Thee the homage I owe Thee as my Creator, the love due to 

Thee as my Savior. Apply to my soul, I beseech Thee, O merciful 

Jesus, Thy infinite merits; apply them also to those for whom I particularly wish to pray. Amen.

Another Offering 

Eternal Father, I unite myself to the intentions and love which the most sorrowful Mary had on Calvary, and I offer Thee the Sacrifice which Thy beloved Son Jesus made of Himself on the Cross, and which He now renews upon this altar: 

First, to adore Thee, and to give Thee the honor which is due to Thee; acknowledging in Thee the supreme dominion in all things, their absolute dependence on Thee, and acknowledging Thee to be our sole Ultimate End. 

Secondly, to thank Thee for the numberless benefits received. 

Thirdly, to placate Thy justice, offended by so many sins; and to render therefore worthy satisfaction. 

Fourthly, to implore grace and mercy for me; for N.N., for the afflicted and sorrow-laden; for the poor sinners; for the whole world; and for the blessed souls in purgatory. 

- An Indulgence of three hundred days, for those attending on the sacrifice of Mass; provided that at the beginning of Mass they shall have made the aforesaid offering. - Pius X, July 8, 1904. 

A Spiritual Communion 

My Jesus, I believe that Thou art present in the Blessed Sacrament. I love Thee above everything, and I long for Thee in my soul. Since I can not now receive Thee sacramentally, come at least spiritually into my heart. I embrace Thee and unite myself entirely to Thee; never permit me to be separated from Thee. Amen. 

Jesus, my good, my sweet love, Wound, inflame this heart of mine

So that it may be always and all on fire for Thee.
